are indicated in fat coloured paths (red and blue, as well as purple for their common part). Since
v
and
w
are visited before
u^{-}_{\varepsilon}
then one must necessarily go through either
u^{-}_{\varepsilon}
or
u^{+}_{\varepsilon}
in order to reach
u
, so they lie outside the ball centred at
u
and with radius
\varepsilon
.

The following geometric lemma should be clear on a picture, see e.g. Figure
7
right.

Lemma 3.4
.

Almost surely, the ball of radius
\varepsilon
centred at
u
in the looptree is contained in the interval
[u^{-}_{\varepsilon},u^{+}_{\varepsilon}]
.

It should also be clear from Figure
7
that the inclusion is strict in general; but it turns out that this rough inclusion will give us the correct upper bound for computing the dimensions.

Proof.

In the first case above, when
(u^{-}_{\varepsilon},u^{+}_{\varepsilon})\in A_{u}
, the path
X^{\mathrm{ex}}
achieves values smaller than
X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{u^{-}_{\varepsilon}-}=X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{u^{+}_{\varepsilon}}
immediately before
u^{-}_{\varepsilon}
and immediately after
u^{+}_{\varepsilon}
. We infer from the definition of
d_{{L}(X)}
that any time
t<u^{-}_{\varepsilon}
or
t>u^{+}_{\varepsilon}
has
d_{{L}(X)}(t,u)>d_{{L}(X)}(u^{-}_{\varepsilon},u)=d_{{L}(X)}(u^{+}_{\varepsilon},u)=\varepsilon
.
For the same reason, in the second case, any time
t>u^{+}_{\varepsilon}
has
d_{{L}(X)}(t,u)>d_{{L}(X)}(u^{+}_{\varepsilon},u)=\varepsilon
and any time
t<s_{\varepsilon}
has
d_{{L}(X)}(t,u)>d_{{L}(X)}(s_{\varepsilon},u)>\varepsilon
. Finally, times
t\in(s_{\varepsilon},u^{-}_{\varepsilon})
have
d_{{L}(X)}(t,u)>\min\{d_{{L}(X)}(s_{\varepsilon},u),d_{{L}(X)}(u^{-}_{\varepsilon},u)\}=d_{{L}(X)}(u^{-}_{\varepsilon},u)=\varepsilon
.
This shows that the complement of the interval
[u^{-}_{\varepsilon},u^{+}_{\varepsilon}]
is contained in the complement of the ball of radius
\varepsilon
centred at
u
in the looptree.
∎

3.2.
A volume estimate in the looptree

Recall the claim of Proposition
3.1
on the volume of balls in the looptree. Given Lemma
3.4
, it suffices to upper bound the length of the interval
[U^{-}_{\varepsilon},U^{+}_{\varepsilon}]
where
U
is a uniform random time independent of
X^{\mathrm{ex}}
.
In order to make calculations, we shall rely on the local absolutely continuity between the excursion and the unconditioned process, which simply comes from the construction of the excursion.
Precisely, let
(\overleftarrow{X}_{s})_{s\geq 0}
and
(\overrightarrow{X}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be two independent copies of the Lévy process
X
, then by (
2.1
) and (
2.2
), the pair

(X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{U}-X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{(U-s)-},X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{t+U})_{s\in[0,U],t\in[0,1-U]}

is locally absolutely continuous with respect to
(\overleftarrow{X}_{s},\overrightarrow{X}_{t})_{s,t\geq 0}
with bounded density.

We shall keep the same notation as in the preceding subsection and consider the sets
A_{0}
,
B_{0}
, and
B^{\varepsilon}_{0}
defined as above for
(-\overleftarrow{X}_{s-},\overrightarrow{X}_{t})_{s,t\geq 0}
.
In particular, the first elements of pairs in
A_{0}
are given by the negative of the set of times at which
(\overleftarrow{X}_{s})_{s\geq 0}
realises a weak record. Let us thus consider the running supremum process
(S_{t})_{t\geq 0}
and the local time at the supremum
(L_{t})_{t\geq 0}
of the process
(\overleftarrow{X}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
. We henceforth drop the arrow for better readability.
According to
[
DLG02
, Equation 96]
the random point measure
{N}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u)=\sum_{i\in I}\delta_{(t_{i},x_{i},u_{i})}
defined by:

{N}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u)=\sum_{s:S_{s}>S_{s-}}\delta_{(L_{s},S_{s}-X_{s-},(S_{s-}-X_{s-})/(S_{s}-X_{s-}))}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u)

(3.5)

is a Poisson random measure on
[0,\infty)\times[0,\infty)\times[0,1]
with intensity
\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\otimes x\pi(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x)\otimes\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u
.

Let us next define four subordinators by
X^{\prime}
,
X^{R}
,
\widetilde{X}
, and
\sigma
by setting for every
t>0
:

X^{\prime}_{t}=\beta t+\sum_{t_{i}\leq t}x_{i}\qquad\text{and}\qquad X^{R}_{t}=\beta t+\sum_{t_{i}\leq t}u_{i}x_{i},

as well as

\widetilde{X}_{t}=\beta t+\sum_{t_{i}\leq t}\min\{u_{i},1-u_{i}\}x_{i}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sigma_{t}=\beta t+\sum_{t_{i}\leq t}u_{i}(1-u_{i})x_{i}.

Notice that we give them all the same drift
\beta
for the continuous part.
Informally, in the infinite looptree coded by
(\overleftarrow{X},\overrightarrow{X})
with a distinguished path of loops by coded
A_{0}\cup B_{0}
, when reading this path starting from
0
, the jumps of process
X^{\prime}
code the total length of the loops on this path, those of
X^{R}
code their right length, those of
\widetilde{X}
the shortest length among left and right, so related to the looptree distance, and finally those of
\sigma
the product of the left and right lengths, which is related to a Brownian bridge on the loop.

Lemma 3.5
.

The Laplace exponents of these processes are given by:

\psi^{\prime}(\lambda)-\beta\lambda\quad\text{for }X^{\prime},\qquad\widetilde{\psi}(\lambda)\quad\text{for }X^{R},\qquad\widetilde{\psi}(\lambda/2)+\beta\lambda/2\quad\text{for }\widetilde{X},\quad\text{and}\quad\phi(\lambda)+\beta\lambda\quad\text{for }\sigma,

where
\psi^{\prime}
simply denotes the derivative of
\psi
and
\widetilde{\psi}(\lambda)=\psi(\lambda)/\lambda
and finally
\phi(\lambda)=\int_{0}^{\infty}x\pi(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x)\int_{0}^{1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u(1-\exp(-\lambda u(1-u)x))
.

Proof.

Recall the Poisson random measure
{N}
from (
3.5
), then for every
t,\lambda>0
, by exchanging the differentiation and integral, we get:

-\frac{1}{t}\log\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\exp\Bigl(-\sum_{t_{i}\leq t}x_{i}\Bigr)\Bigr]=\int_{0}^{\infty}(1-\operatorname{e}^{-\lambda x})x\pi(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x)=\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda}\int_{0}^{\infty}(\operatorname{e}^{-\lambda x}-1+\lambda x)\pi(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x)=\psi^{\prime}(\lambda)-2\beta\lambda.

For
X^{R}
, we have:

-\frac{1}{t}\log\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\exp\Bigl(-\sum_{t_{i}\leq t}u_{i}x_{i}\Bigr)\Bigr]=\int_{0}^{\infty}x\pi(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x)\int_{0}^{1}(1-\operatorname{e}^{-\lambda ux})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u=\frac{1}{\lambda}\int_{0}^{\infty}\pi(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x)(\lambda x-1+\operatorname{e}^{-\lambda x})=\widetilde{\psi}(\lambda)-\beta\lambda.

Noticing that the random variables
\min\{u_{i},1-u_{i}\}
have density
2\,\mathbf{1}_{[0,1/2]}
, and that

\int_{0}^{1/2}2\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v(1-\operatorname{e}^{-\lambda vx})=1+\frac{2}{\lambda x}(\operatorname{e}^{-\lambda x/2}-1)=\frac{2}{\lambda}(\operatorname{e}^{-\lambda x/2}-1+\frac{\lambda x}{2})
,
we have similarly:

-\frac{1}{t}\log\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\exp\Bigl(-\sum_{t_{i}\leq t}x_{i}\min\{u_{i},1-u_{i}\}\Bigr)\Bigr]=\int_{0}^{\infty}x\pi(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x)\int_{0}^{1/2}2\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v(1-\operatorname{e}^{-\lambda vx})=\widetilde{\psi}\Bigl(\frac{\lambda}{2}\Bigr)-\frac{\beta\lambda}{2}.

Finally

-\frac{1}{t}\log\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\exp\Bigl(-\sum_{t_{i}\leq t}x_{i}u_{i}(1-u_{i})\Bigr)\Bigr]=\int_{0}^{\infty}x\pi(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x)\int_{0}^{1}(1-\operatorname{e}^{-\lambda u(1-u)x})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u=\phi(\lambda).

This characterises the law of these subordinators.
∎

The following result claims that in the infinite model, on the distinguished chain of loops, the sum of the lengths of the loops we encounter is close to the sum of the shortest between their left and right length.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
\tau_{\varepsilon}=\inf\{t\geq 0\colon\widetilde{X}_{t}>\varepsilon\}
for every
\varepsilon>0
.
Fix
\delta\in(0,1)
, then almost surely, for every
\varepsilon>0
small enough, it holds
X^{R}_{\tau_{\varepsilon}-}\leq X^{\prime}_{\tau_{\varepsilon}-}\leq\varepsilon^{1-\delta}
.

Proof.

Clearly
X^{R}\leq X^{\prime}
so we focus on the second bound. Also, it is enough to prove the claim assuming that
\beta=0
. Indeed, when
\beta>0
, since
\beta
is the common drift coefficient to both
X^{\prime}
and
\widetilde{X}
, then by adding this contribution plus that of the jumps of
\widetilde{X}
, we infer from the
\beta=0
case that
X^{\prime}_{\tau_{\varepsilon}-}\leq\varepsilon^{1-\delta}+\widetilde{X}_{\tau_{\varepsilon}-}\leq\varepsilon^{1-\delta}+\varepsilon
.
We now assume that
\beta=0
. To simplify notation, we set
v_{i}=\min(u_{i},1-u_{i})
and
y_{i}=v_{i}x_{i}
.
For
K>1
, let us decompose the process
X^{\prime}
by introducing a cutoff for small values of the
v_{i}
’s as follows: for every
t>0
, write

X^{\prime}_{t}=\sum_{t_{i}\leq t}\frac{y_{i}}{v_{i}}\mathbf{1}_{\{v_{i}\geq 1/(2K)\}}+\sum_{t_{i}\leq t}\frac{y_{i}}{v_{i}}\mathbf{1}_{\{v_{i}<1/(2K)\}}.

Also, since the
v_{i}
’s have density
2\,\mathbf{1}_{[0,1/2]}
, we have

\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}\Bigl(v_{i}\leq\frac{1}{2K}\Bigr)=\frac{1}{K}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\frac{1}{v_{i}}\mathbf{1}_{\{v_{i}\geq 1/(2K)\}}\Bigr]=\int_{1/(2K)}^{1/2}\frac{2}{x}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=2\log K.

We claim that for
K
well-chosen, the first sum in the first display will be close to the sum of the
y_{i}
’s, whereas in the second sum, there are too few terms and this sum will be small.

Fix
\delta>\delta^{\prime}>0
and let
K_{\varepsilon}=\exp(\varepsilon^{-\delta^{\prime}})
. Then the Markov inequality implies:

\displaystyle\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\sum_{t_{i}\leq\tau_{\varepsilon}-}x_{i}\mathbf{1}_{\{v_{i}\geq\frac{1}{2K_{\varepsilon}}\}}\geq\varepsilon^{1-\delta}\bigm|(t_{i},y_{i})_{i\in I}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\sum_{t_{i}\leq\tau_{\varepsilon}-}\frac{1}{v_{i}}y_{i}\mathbf{1}_{\{v_{i}\geq\frac{1}{2K_{\varepsilon}}\}}\geq\varepsilon^{-\delta}\sum_{t_{i}\leq\tau_{\varepsilon}-}y_{i}\bigm|(t_{i},y_{i})_{i\in I}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq 2\varepsilon^{\delta}\log K_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\leq 2\varepsilon^{\delta-\delta^{\prime}}.

Note that the sum of the last quantity over all values of
\varepsilon
of the form
2^{-n}
with
n\in\mathbb{N}
is finite.

We next consider the other sum in the decomposition above, when the
v_{i}
’s are smaller than
1/(2K_{\varepsilon})
.
Recall that we assume that
\beta=0
and note in this case the identity in law (as processes):

\sum_{t_{i}\leq t}x_{i}\mathbf{1}_{\{v_{i}<\frac{1}{2K_{\varepsilon}}\}}\enskip\mathop{=}^{(d)}\enskip X^{\prime}_{t\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(v_{i}<\frac{1}{2K_{\varepsilon}})}=X^{\prime}_{t/K_{\varepsilon}}.

Recall from Lemma
3.5
that
X^{\prime}
has Laplace exponent
\psi^{\prime}
, then for every
t,x>0
,
we have by the Markov property and the upper bound
1-\operatorname{e}^{-y}\leq y
:

\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\sum_{t_{i}\leq t}x_{i}\mathbf{1}_{\{v_{i}<\frac{1}{2K_{\varepsilon}}\}}>x\Bigr)=\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(1-\operatorname{e}^{X^{\prime}_{t/K_{\varepsilon}}/x}>1-\operatorname{e}^{-1})\leq\frac{\operatorname{e}}{\operatorname{e}-1}\bigl(1-\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}\bigl[\operatorname{e}^{-X^{\prime}_{t/K_{\varepsilon}}/x}\bigr]\bigr)\leq\frac{\operatorname{e}}{\operatorname{e}-1}\frac{t}{K_{\varepsilon}}\psi^{\prime}(1/x).

On the other hand, since
\widetilde{X}
has Laplace exponent
\widetilde{\psi}(\cdot/2)
,

\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(\tau_{\varepsilon}\geq t)=\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(\widetilde{X}_{t}\leq\varepsilon)=\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}\bigl(\operatorname{e}^{-\widetilde{X}_{t}/\varepsilon}\geq\operatorname{e}^{-1}\bigr)\leq\operatorname{e}\cdot\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}\bigl[\operatorname{e}^{-\widetilde{X}_{t}/\varepsilon}\bigr]=\operatorname{e}\cdot\operatorname{e}^{-t\widetilde{\psi}(1/(2\varepsilon))}.

We infer that for any
t>0
, we have:

\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\sum_{t_{i}\leq\tau_{\varepsilon}-}x_{i}\mathbf{1}_{\{v_{i}<\frac{1}{2K_{\varepsilon}}\}}>\varepsilon^{1-\delta}\Bigr)\leq\frac{\operatorname{e}}{\operatorname{e}-1}tK_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\psi^{\prime}(1/\varepsilon^{1-\delta})+\operatorname{e}\cdot\operatorname{e}^{-t\widetilde{\psi}(1/(2\varepsilon))}.

For
t=\varepsilon^{-\delta}\widetilde{\psi}(1/(2\varepsilon))^{-1}
with
\delta>0
, we read:

\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\sum_{t_{i}\leq\tau_{\varepsilon}-}x_{i}\mathbf{1}_{\{v_{i}<\frac{1}{2K_{\varepsilon}}\}}>\varepsilon^{1-\delta}\Bigr)\leq\frac{\operatorname{e}}{\operatorname{e}-1}\frac{\psi^{\prime}(1/\varepsilon^{1-\delta})}{\varepsilon^{\delta}K_{\varepsilon}\widetilde{\psi}(1/(2\varepsilon))}+\operatorname{e}\cdot\operatorname{e}^{-\varepsilon^{-\delta}}.

Since
\widetilde{\psi}
is increasing and
\psi^{\prime}
grows at most polynomially (in fact, its upper exponent at infinity is
\boldsymbol{\eta}-1
), whereas
K_{\varepsilon}
grows exponentially fast as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
, then the sum of the upper bound when
\varepsilon
ranges over the set
\{2^{-n},n\in\mathbb{N}\}
is finite.
We conclude from our two bounds and the Borel–Cantelli lemma that almost surely for every
n
large enough, we have
X^{\prime}_{\tau_{2^{-n}}-}\leq 2^{-n(1-\delta)}
.
We extend this to all values of
\varepsilon>0
small enough by monotonicity.
∎

Recall that Proposition
3.1
claims that almost surely, the volume of the ball in the looptree centred at an independent uniform random point
U
and with radius
2^{-n}
is less than
\psi(2^{n(1-\delta)})^{-1}
for
n
large enough.

Proof of Proposition
3.1
.

As we have already observed, thanks to Lemma
3.4
, it suffices to prove that if
U
is an independent random time with the uniform distribution on
[0,1]
, then almost surely, for every
n
large enough, we have both

U-U^{-}_{2^{-n}}\leq\psi(2^{n(1-\delta)})^{-1}

and

U^{+}_{2^{-n}}-U\leq\psi(2^{n(1-\delta)})^{-1}
.
Let us first focus on the second bound, we shall then deduce the first one by a symmetry argument.
By the local absolute continuity relation which follows from (
2.1
) and (
2.2
), since the density is bounded and we aim for an almost sure result, it is sufficient to prove the claim after replacing
(X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{U}-X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{(U-s)-},X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{t+U})_{s\in[0,U],t\in[0,1-U]}
by
(\overleftarrow{X}_{s},\overrightarrow{X}_{t})_{s,t\geq 0}
.
In particular, we replace
U
by
0
and write
0^{+}_{\varepsilon}
for the time
u^{+}_{\varepsilon}
defined from
(\overleftarrow{X}_{s},\overrightarrow{X}_{t})_{s,t\geq 0}
for
u=0
.
Relying on the Borel–Cantelli lemma, it suffices to prove that
\sum_{n}\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(0^{+}_{2^{-n}}\geq\psi(2^{n(1-\delta)})^{-1})<\infty
for this infinite model.

Let us rewrite the definition of
0^{+}_{\varepsilon}
(recall (
3.3
) and (
3.4
)) in a more convenient way and let us refer to Figure
8
for a figurative representation.
Let
\widetilde{X}
and
X^{R}
be defined as previously from
\overleftarrow{X}
instead of
X
, let
\tau_{\varepsilon}=\inf\{s>0\colon\widetilde{X}_{s}>\varepsilon\}
, and
T(x)=\inf\{t>0\colon\overrightarrow{X}_{t}\leq-x\}
. Then we have
0^{+}_{\varepsilon}=T(X^{R}_{\tau_{\varepsilon}-}+(\varepsilon-\widetilde{X}_{\tau_{\varepsilon}-}))
.

Fix
\delta>0
, then according to Lemma
3.6
, almost surely for every
\varepsilon
small enough, it holds
X^{R}_{\tau_{\varepsilon}-}\leq\varepsilon^{1-\delta/4}
. We then implicitly work under the event that
X^{R}_{\tau_{\varepsilon}-}+(\varepsilon-\widetilde{X}_{\tau_{\varepsilon}-})\leq\varepsilon^{1-\delta/2}
, so it remains to upper bound
T(\varepsilon^{1-\delta/2})
. First, for every
x>0
, we have by the Markov inequality:

\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(T(\varepsilon^{1-\delta/2})\geq x)=\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}\bigl(1-\operatorname{e}^{-T(\varepsilon^{1-\delta/2})/x}\geq 1-\operatorname{e}^{-1}\bigr)\leq\frac{\operatorname{e}}{\operatorname{e}-1}\bigl(1-\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}\bigl[\operatorname{e}^{-x^{-1}T(\varepsilon^{1-\delta/2})}\bigr]\bigr).

It is well-known that
T
is a subordinator with Laplace exponent
\Phi=\psi^{-1}
the inverse of
\psi
, see e.g.
[
Ber96
, Theorem VII.1]
. Then, using also that
1-\operatorname{e}^{-y}\leq y
, we infer that

\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(T(\varepsilon^{1-\delta/2})\geq x)\leq\frac{\operatorname{e}}{\operatorname{e}-1}\bigl(1-\operatorname{e}^{-\varepsilon^{1-\delta/2}\Phi(x^{-1})}\bigr)\leq\frac{\operatorname{e}}{\operatorname{e}-1}\varepsilon^{1-\delta/2}\Phi(x^{-1}).

Consequently, for
\varepsilon=2^{-n}
and
x=\psi(\varepsilon^{-(1-\delta)})^{-1}=\psi(2^{n(1-\delta)})^{-1}
, we infer that

\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(T(2^{-n(1-\delta/2)})\geq\psi(2^{n(1-\delta)})^{-1})\leq\frac{\operatorname{e}}{\operatorname{e}-1}2^{-n(1-\delta/2)+n(1-\delta)}=\frac{\operatorname{e}}{\operatorname{e}-1}2^{-n\delta/2},

which is a convergent series as we wanted.
This implies that almost surely, for every
n
large enough, we have
0^{+}_{2^{-n}}\geq\psi(2^{n(1-\delta)})^{-1}
in the infinite volume model. By local absolute continuity, we deduce that almost surely, for every
n
large enough, we have
U^{+}_{2^{-n}}-U\leq\psi(2^{n(1-\delta)})^{-1}
in the excursion model.

Figure 8
:
Schematic representation of the times
u^{-}_{\varepsilon}
and
u^{+}_{\varepsilon}
in terms of the Lévy excursion. On the excursion, the blue and green vertical lines on the very left have the same length. The sum of this length and that of all the red vertical lines (which correspond to the geodesic from
u
to the root) equals precisely
\varepsilon
. This is less than
X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{u}-X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{u^{+}_{\varepsilon}}
, which codes the length of the path from
u
to
u^{+}_{\varepsilon}
in the looptree if one always follows the cycles on the spine on their right, and which is at most of order
\varepsilon^{1-\delta}
by Lemma
3.6
.

Let
B(U,r)
denote the closed ball centred at
U
and with radius
r
in
{L}(X^{\mathrm{ex}})
and let us denote by
|\cdot|
the volume measure. We just proved that almost surely, for every
n
large enough, we have
|B(U,2^{-n})\cap[U,1]|\leq\psi(2^{n(1-\delta)})^{-1}
.
Lemma
3.7
below, together with the symmetry of
U
, implies that the two sequences
(|B(U,2^{-n})\cap[0,U]|)_{n\geq 1}
and
(|B(U,2^{-n})\cap[U,1]|)_{n\geq 1}
have the same distribution, which completes the proof.
∎

We need the following lemma to conclude the proof of Proposition
3.1
. It roughly says that the looptree
{L}(X^{\mathrm{ex}})
is invariant under mirror symmetry.
This property is not immediate from the construction from the excursion path
X^{\mathrm{ex}}
, although we believe that a suitable path construction could map
X^{\mathrm{ex}}
to a process which has the same law as its time-reversal.
Also this mirror symmetry relates informally to that of the Lévy trees from
[
DLG02
, Corollary 3.1.6]
.
One could rely on this result (beware that it is written under the
\sigma
-finite Itō excursion measure), and indeed the exploration process
\rho
and its dual
\eta
, defined in the form of Equations 89 and 90 in
[
DLG02
]
, relate explicitly to the looptree distances.
However we prefer a self-contained soft argument using an approximation with discrete looptrees which are (almost) symmetric.

Recall from the previous proof of that
B(u,r)
denotes the closed ball centred at
U
and with radius
r
in
{L}(X^{\mathrm{ex}})
, seen as a subset of
[0,1]
and that
|\cdot|
denotes the Lebesgue measure.

Lemma 3.7
.

For every
u\in[0,1]
, the processes
(|B(u,r)\cap[0,u]|)_{r\geq 0}
and
(|B(1-u,r)\cap[1-u,1]|)_{r\geq 0}
have the same distribution.

Proof.

Fix
u\in[0,1]
. Combining Remark
2.2
and Remark
2.3
we infer the following: there exist discrete Łukasiewicz paths, obtained by cyclicly shifting a bridge with exchangeable increments, which converge in distribution to
X^{\mathrm{ex}}
once properly rescaled, such that if
d^{n}
denotes the corresponding looptree distance, normalised both in time and space, then
(d^{n}(u,t))_{t\in[0,1]}
converges to
(d_{{L}(X^{\mathrm{ex}})}(u,t))_{t\in[0,1]}
for the uniform topology. In particular, the processes
(|B(u,r)\cap[0,u]|)_{r\geq 0}
and
(|B(1-u,r)\cap[1-u,1]|)_{r\geq 0}
are the limits in law of the analogous processes for these rescaled discrete looptrees. We claim that these discrete volume processes for any given
n
have the same distribution as in fact the discrete looptrees are almost invariant under mirror symmetry.

Figure 9
:
Middle: a looptree obtained from a dotted plane tree by replacing the edges from each vertex to its parent by cycles.
Left: a looptree as we consider in this work, obtained from that in the middle by merging each internal vertex of the tree with its right-most offspring.
Right: a looptree obtained by first taking the mirror image of that in the middle and then again merging each internal vertex of the tree with its right-most offspring.
The looptrees on the left and on the right are the mirror image of each other, except for the loop of length
1
.

Recall that the looptrees we consider here are those on the left of Figure
9
, where each internal node in the associated tree (in the middle of the figure) is merged with its right-most offspring. Let us call such a looptree
L_{n}
. By exchangeability of the coding bridge, the law of the looptree, say
\Lambda_{n}
, in the middle of Figure
9
, without this merging operation, is invariant under mirror symmetry. Hence, if one starts from this looptree
\Lambda_{n}
, takes its mirror image
\widehat{\Lambda}_{n}
, and constructs a further looptree
L_{n}^{\prime}
by merging each internal vertex with its right-most offspring, then
L_{n}^{\prime}
has the same law as
L_{n}
.
Beware that the looptree
L_{n}^{\prime}
is not exactly the mirror image
\widehat{L}_{n}
of
L_{n}
. The only difference however is caused by loops of length
1
, which always pend to the left in
L_{n}
and
L_{n}^{\prime}
, but get moved instead to the right in
\widehat{L}_{n}
.
Recall that the random looptrees we are considering are obtained from a Łukasiewicz path which is a discrete approximation of
X^{\mathrm{ex}}
; we can always assume that this path has no null increment, and thus the looptree
L_{n}
has no such loop of length
1
, in which case
\widehat{L}_{n}=L_{n}^{\prime}
has indeed the same law as
L_{n}
.
We conclude by letting
n\to\infty
as in the first part of the proof.
∎

3.3.
On distances in the map

Let us now turn to the proof of Proposition
3.2
on the volume of balls in the map
(M,d_{M},p_{M})
.
Let us start by noticing that, similarly to the discrete setting, the distance in the map can be seen as a pseudo-distance on the looptree. This will be useful later.
Recall from Theorem
2.1
and Theorem
2.4
that
M
is defined as a subsequential limit of rescaled discrete random maps
M^{n}
, where
M^{n}
is coded by a labelled looptree, itself coded by a pair of discrete paths
(W^{n},Z^{n})
. We shall denote by
X^{n}=r_{n}^{-1}W^{n}_{\lfloor n\rfloor\cdot}
the rescaled Łukasiewicz path which converges to
X^{\mathrm{ex}}
almost surely by combining Theorem
2.1
with Skorokhod’s representation theorem.

Lemma 3.8
.

Almost surely, for every
s,t\in[0,1]
, if
d_{{L}(X^{\mathrm{ex}})}(s,t)=0
, then
d_{M}(s,t)=0
.

Proof.

Fix
s\leq t
such that
d_{{L}(X^{\mathrm{ex}})}(s,t)=0
, which means by Lemma
3.3
that

\text{(i)}\enskip\text{either}\enskip X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{s}=X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{t}=\inf_{[s,t]}X^{\mathrm{ex}}\qquad\text{(ii)}\enskip\text{or}\enskip\Delta X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{s}>0\enskip\text{and}\enskip t=\inf\{r>s\colon X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{r}=X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{s-}\}.

(3.6)

Suppose first that we are in the first case and in addition that
X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{r}>X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{s}=X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{t}
for all
r\in(s,t)
.
Fix some small
\varepsilon>0
, then on the one hand we have
\inf_{[s+\varepsilon,t-\varepsilon]}X^{\mathrm{ex}}>X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{t}+2\delta
for some
\delta>0
, and on the other hand by right-continuity there exists
s^{\prime}\in(s,s+\varepsilon)
such that
X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{s}<X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{s^{\prime}}\leq X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{s}+\delta
; finally since
X^{\mathrm{ex}}
has no negative jumps, then all values in
(X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{t},X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{t}+2\delta)
are achieved by some times in
(t-\varepsilon,t)
.
Recall that
X^{\mathrm{ex}}
is the almost sure limit in the Skorokhod
J_{1}
topology of some rescaled discrete Łukasiewicz path
X^{n}
.
Then for every
n
large enough, there exist
s_{n}\in(s,s+\varepsilon)
and
t_{n}\in(t-\varepsilon,t)
with
X^{n}_{s_{n}}=X^{n}_{t_{n}}=\inf_{[s_{n},t_{n}]}X^{n}
, namely the two times correspond to the same vertex in the discrete looptree. This implies that they also correspond to the same vertex in the discrete map, i.e. that
d_{M^{n}}(s_{n},t_{n})=0
. When passing to the limit along the corresponding subsequence and letting then
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
, we infer that indeed
d_{M}(s,t)=0
.

Suppose next that we are in the first case in (
3.6
), but now that there exists
r\in(s,t)
with
X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{r}=X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{s}=X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{t}
. Then by uniqueness of the local minima, neither
s
nor
t
are local minima.
In addition, as recalled in the proof of Lemma
3.3
, we have
\Delta X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{s}=0
in this case since otherwise the reversed process at time
r
would may a weak record by a jump.
This implies that for every
\varepsilon>0
, there exist
s^{\prime}\in(s-\varepsilon,s)
and
t^{\prime}\in(t,t+\varepsilon)
which satisfy
X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{s^{\prime}}=X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{t^{\prime}}<X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{r}
for all
r\in(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})
and we conclude from the preceding case.

Similarly, if we fall in the second case in (
3.6
), then the previous argument shows that
t
is not a time of local minimum for otherwise the reversed process started just after would reach this record again by a jump. Using the left-limit at
s
, we infer that for every
\varepsilon>0
, there exist times
s^{\prime}\in(s-\varepsilon,s)
and
t^{\prime}\in(t,t+\varepsilon)
with
X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{s^{\prime}}=X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{t^{\prime}}<X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{r}
for every
r\in(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})
and we conclude again from the first case.
∎

Let us next continue with a geometric lemma inspired by
[
LGM11
, Lemma 14]
which will allow us to include balls in the map in some well-chosen intervals analogously to Lemma
3.4
for looptrees.
Recall the sets
A_{u}
and
B_{u}
defined in (
3.1
) and (
3.2
) respectively.

Lemma 3.9
.

Almost surely for every
u\in(0,1)
and for every pair
(a,b)\in A_{u}\cup B_{u}
, we have

d_{M}(u,t)\geq\min\{Z_{u}-Z_{a},Z_{u}-Z_{b}\},

for every
t\in[0,a)\cup(b,1]
.

We shall assume throughout the proof of that both
Z_{a}<Z_{u}
and
Z_{b}<Z_{u}
otherwise the claim is trivial.

Proof.

Let us first consider the analogue in the discrete setting of a pointed map
(M^{n},v^{n}_{\star})
coded by a pair
(W^{n},Z^{n})
and the associate labelled looptree.
Here times are integers and we use the same notation for a vertex in the map different from the distinguished one, the corresponding vertex in the looptree, and a time of visit of one of its corners when following the contour of the looptree.
The assumption that
(a,b)\in A_{u}\cup B_{u}
means that either
a
and
b
are the same vertex, which is an ancestor of
u
, or they both belong to the same cycle between two consecutive ancestors of
u
, with
a
on the left and
b
on the right.

Then take a vertex
v
outside the interval
[a,b]
and consider a geodesic
in the map
from
v
to
u
. This geodesic necessarily contains an edge of the map with one extremity
x\in[a,b]
and the other one
y\notin[a,b]
.
From the construction of the bijection briefly recalled in Section
2.1
, this means that either
x
is the successor of
y
or vice versa.
Now if
x
is the successor of
y
, this means that
x
is the first vertex after
y
when following the contour of the looptree (extended by periodicity) which has a label smaller than that of
y
.
Since one necessarily goes through
a
when going from
y
to
x
by following the contour of the looptree, then

Z^{n}_{a}\geq Z^{n}_{y}\geq Z^{n}_{x}+1
,
see e.g. Figure
10
.
Symmetrically, if
y
is the successor of
x
, then
Z^{n}_{x}\leq Z^{n}_{b}
.
Hence
Z^{n}_{x}\leq\max\{Z^{n}_{a},Z^{n}_{b}\}
in any case. On the one hand this vertex
x
lies on the geodesic in the map between
u
and
v
, so
d_{M^{n}}(u,x)<d_{M^{n}}(u,v)
. On the other hand the labels encode the graph distance to the reference point
v^{n}_{\star}
in the map, so by the triangle inequality, we have
d_{M^{n}}(u,x)\geq Z^{n}_{u}-Z^{n}_{x}
, which leads to our claim in the discrete setting.

Figure 10
:
A portion of a discrete labelled looptree in plain lines. In dashed lines is represented a geodesic path between
t
and
u
in the associated map. Each dashed edge thus links a time to its successor. The pictures display the two situations: when
x
is the successor of
y
on the left and vice versa on the right. Since the edge between
x
and
y
steps over
a
or
b
, then the label of
x
is necessarily smaller than or equal to that of
a
or
b
in each case.

In the continuum setting, we argue by approximation. Recall that we assume
(X^{\mathrm{ex}},Z^{\mathrm{ex}})
to arise as the almost sure limit of a pair
(X^{n},Z^{n})
, which is already rescaled in time and space to lighten the notation.
Let us first consider the case when
(a,b)\in B_{u}
, namely that there exists
s<a
such that

X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{s}>X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{a}=\min_{[s,a]}X^{\mathrm{ex}}>X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{b}=\min_{[s,b]}X^{\mathrm{ex}}>X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{s-}.

We infer from the convergence
X^{n}\to X^{\mathrm{ex}}
in the Skorokhod
J_{1}
topology that there exist times
s_{n}<a_{n}<u_{n}<b_{n}
in
\mathbb{N}/n
which converge to
s
,
a
,
u
,
b
respectively and such that

X^{n}_{a_{n}}=\min_{[s_{n},a_{n}]}X^{n}>X^{n}_{b_{n}}=\min_{[s_{n},b_{n}]}X^{n}>X^{n}_{s_{n}-1/n}
.
This implies that
a_{n}
and
b_{n}
both belong to the same cycle between two consecutive ancestors of
u_{n}
, with
a_{n}
on the left and
b_{n}
on the right and we infer from the first part of the proof of that the graph distance in the rescaled map between
u_{n}
and any vertex
t_{n}
which lies outside
[a_{n},b_{n}]
is larger than or equal to
\min\{Z^{n}_{u_{n}}-Z^{n}_{a_{n}},Z^{n}_{u_{n}}-Z^{n}_{b_{n}}\}
. We finally can pass to the limit along the corresponding subsequence, using that
Z^{n}\to Z
for the uniform topology.

Suppose now that
(a,b)\in A_{u}
, which means that either

X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{a}=X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{b}=\min_{[a,b]}X^{\mathrm{ex}}

or

b=\inf\{t>a\colon X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{t}=X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{a-}\}
.
As shown in the proof of Lemma
3.8
, in each case we can approximate this pair by pairs that satisfy

X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{t}>X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{b}=X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{a}\qquad\text{for every }t\in(a,b).

We thus henceforth assume that the latter holds.
In particular, for any
\varepsilon>0
small, the values of
X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{t}
for
t\in[a+\varepsilon,b-\varepsilon]
stay bounded away from
X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{b}=X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{a}
.
From the fact that
X^{\mathrm{ex}}
is right-continuous at
s
and makes no negative jump, we infer that every value larger than but close enough to
X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{b}=X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{a}
is achieved both by times arbitrarily close to
a
and by times arbitrarily close to
b
.
Then the convergence
X^{n}\to X^{\mathrm{ex}}
in the Skorokhod
J_{1}
topology yields the existence of times
a_{n}<u_{n}<b_{n}
which converge to
a
,
u
,
b
respectively and such that for
n
large enough, we have
X^{n}_{t}\geq X^{n}_{a_{n}}=X^{n}_{b_{n}}
for every
t\in(a_{n},b_{n})\cap\mathbb{N}/n
.
It follows that
a_{n}
and
b_{n}
correspond to the same vertex in the looptree, and it is an ancestor of
u_{n}
.
We conclude from the discrete case again.
∎

Recall that Proposition
3.2
claims that almost surely, the volume of the ball in the map centred at an independent uniform random point
U
and with radius
2^{-n}
is less than
\psi(2^{2n(1-\delta)})^{-1}
for
n
large enough.
This now easily follows from Lemma
3.9
and a technical result that we state and prove in the next subsection.

Proof of Proposition
3.2
.

According to Lemma
3.9
, almost surely the ball of radius
r
centred at the image
U
in the map is entirely contained in the image of any interval
[a_{r},b_{r}]
such that the pair
(a_{r},b_{r})\in A_{U}\cup B_{U}
satisfies
\min\{Z_{U}-Z_{a_{r}},Z_{U}-Z_{b_{r}}\}\geq r
.
Consider the infinite volume model, obtained by replacing the excursion
X^{\mathrm{ex}}
by the two-sided unconditioned Lévy process and the time
U
by
0
.
Proposition
3.10
stated below shows that almost surely, for every
r>0
small enough, there exists such a pair
(a_{r},b_{r})\in A_{0}\cup B_{0}
such that
\min\{Z_{0}-Z_{a_{r}},Z_{0}-Z_{b_{r}}\}\geq r
and both
a_{r}
and
b_{r}
lie at looptree distance at most
r^{2(1-\delta)}
from
0
.
Take
r=2^{-n}
. We infer from the proof of Proposition
3.1

that almost surely, for every
n
large enough we have both

a_{2^{-n}}\geq U^{-}_{2^{-2n(1-\delta)}}\geq U-\psi(2^{2n(1-\delta)^{2}})^{-1}

and

b_{2^{-n}}\leq U^{+}_{2^{-2n(1-\delta)}}\leq U+\psi(2^{2n(1-\delta)^{2}})^{-1}
.
The claim follows by local absolute continuity.
∎

3.4.
A technical lemma with Brownian motions

In order to complete the proof of Proposition
3.2
, it remains to establish the following.

Proposition 3.10
.

For every
\varepsilon>0
, almost surely, for every
r>0
small enough, in the infinite volume labelled looptree coded by the pair of paths
X=(\overleftarrow{X}_{s},\overrightarrow{X}_{t})_{s,t\geq 0}
, there exists a pair
(a_{r},b_{r})\in A_{0}\cup B_{0}
which satisfies both

-Z_{a_{r}}\geq r
and
-Z_{b_{r}}\geq r

as well as

d_{{L}(X)}(a_{r},0)\leq r^{2(1-\varepsilon)}
and
d_{{L}(X)}(b_{r},0)\leq r^{2(1-\varepsilon)}
.

In the case of stable Lévy processes, Le Gall & Miermont
[
LGM11
, Lemma 15]
were able to find such a pair
(a_{r},b_{r})\in A_{0}
, namely that codes an ancestor of the distinguished point
0
. Their argument can be extended when the upper and lower exponents
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
and
\boldsymbol{\eta}
coincide, or when the exponent
\boldsymbol{\delta}\in[1,\boldsymbol{\gamma}]
introduced Duquesne
[
Duq12
]
as
\boldsymbol{\delta}\coloneqq\sup\{c>0\colon\exists K\in(0,\infty)\text{ such that }K\,\mu^{-c}\psi(\mu)\leq\lambda^{-c}\psi(\lambda),\text{ for all }1\leq\mu\leq\lambda\}
is not equal to
1
.
However we were not able to extend it when
\boldsymbol{\delta}=1
.
We therefore argue in a general setting, conditionally on the underlying looptree, and relying only on the properties of the Brownian labels.

Let us first introduce the setting in terms of Brownian motion and Brownian bridges. Let
\mathcal{C}
be a random closed subset of
[0,\infty)
containing
0
, its complement is a countable union of disjoint open intervals, say,
\mathcal{C}^{c}=\bigcup_{i\geq 1}(g_{i},d_{i})
with
d_{i}>g_{i}
.
For every
i\geq 1
, let us denote by
\delta_{i}=d_{i}-g_{i}
the length of these intervals and suppose we are given random lengths
\delta^{L}_{i},\delta^{R}_{i}\geq\delta_{i}
.
Independently, let
W=(W_{t},t\geq 0)
be a standard Brownian motion started from
0
. Consider its trace on
\mathcal{C}
, then independently for every
i\geq 1
, conditionally on the values
W_{g_{i}}
and
W_{d_{i}}
and on the lengths
\delta^{L}_{i}
and
\delta^{R}_{i}
, let us sample two independent Brownian bridges
W^{L,i}=(W^{L,i}_{t};t\in[0,\delta^{L}_{i}])
and
W^{R,i}=(W^{R,i}_{t};t\in[0,\delta^{R}_{i}])
with duration
\delta^{L}_{i}
and
\delta^{R}_{i}
respectively, both starting from
W^{L,i}_{0}=W^{R,i}_{0}=W_{g_{i}}
and ending at
W^{L,i}_{\delta^{L}_{i}}=W^{R,i}_{\delta^{R}_{i}}=W_{d_{i}}
and independent of the rest.

The connection with our problem is the following: the successive ancestors of
0
in the infinite looptree, correspond to the times of weak records of
\overleftarrow{X}
, and their labels form a subordinate Brownian motion, precisely
W_{\sigma(\cdot)}
, where
W
is a standard Brownian motion and
\sigma
is the last subordinator in Lemma
3.5
. We shall thus take
\mathcal{C}
to be the range of
\sigma
, and the labels on each side of the successive cycles between these ancestors roughly correspond to the collection
\{W^{L,i},W^{R,i}\}
.

Lemma 3.11
.

For every
\varepsilon>0
, almost surely, for every
r>0
small enough, the following alternative holds:

•

either there exists
t\in\mathcal{C}\cap[0,r^{2(1-\varepsilon)}]
such that
W_{t}\leq-r
,

•

or there exist
i\geq 1
with
g_{i}<r^{2(1-\varepsilon)}
and
s\in[0,\min\{\delta^{L}_{i},r^{2(1-\varepsilon)}\}]
and
t\in[0,\min\{\delta^{R}_{i},r^{2(1-\varepsilon)}\}]
such that both
W^{L,i}_{s}\leq-r
and
W^{R,i}_{t}\leq-r
.

The key point in this statement is that in the second case, both bridges reach
-r
in the same interval of
\mathcal{C}^{c}\cap[0,r^{2(1-\varepsilon)}]
.
The idea of the proof of is quite intuitive: first we know by the law of iterated logarithm that
W
reaches values much less than
-r
on
[0,r^{2(1-\varepsilon)}]
so, if the first alternative fails, then there exists an interval
(g_{i},d_{i})
on which
W
goes much below
-r
, while starting and ending above this value. Then this interval cannot be too small for the Brownian motion to fluctuate enough. Therefore, if one resamples two Brownian bridges with the same duration
\delta_{i}
and the same endpoints
W_{g_{i}}
and
W_{d_{i}}
, then they have a reasonable chance to both go below
-r
in this interval. Actually the Brownian bridges we sample have a longer duration, so intuitively, they fluctuate even more and have an even greater chance to reach
-r
.
However turning this discussion into a rigorous proof of is quite challenging, and the proof of Lemma
3.11
is deferred to Section
3.5
.

Let us finish this section by relying on this result in order to establish Proposition
3.10
, thereby finishing the proof of Proposition
3.2
.

Proof of Proposition
3.10
.

Recall the Poisson random measure
{N}
in (
3.5
) and the four subordinators defined from it.
By construction of the looptree, the successive ancestors of
0
in the infinite looptree, started from
0
, are coded by the negative of the first element of pairs in
A_{0}
(defined in (
3.1
)) and correspond to the times of weak records of
\overleftarrow{X}
.
Further, by construction of the label process, the labels of these ancestors form a subordinate Brownian motion, precisely
W_{\sigma(\cdot)}
, where
W
is a standard Brownian motion and
\sigma
is the last subordinator in Lemma
3.5
.
We shall apply Lemma
3.11
to the closed set
\mathcal{C}
given by the range of
\sigma
.

Every jump of this subordinator, and more precisely every atom of the Poisson random measure
{N}
, codes two consecutive ancestors separated by a cycle in the looptree, whose left and right length is encoded in the atom of
{N}
. In Lemma
3.11
, these two lengths correspond to
\delta^{L}_{i}
and
\delta^{R}_{i}
, whereas the length
\delta_{i}=d_{i}-g_{i}
equals
\delta_{i}=\delta^{L}_{i}\delta^{R}_{i}/(\delta^{L}_{i}+\delta^{R}_{i})\leq\min(\delta^{L}_{i},\delta^{R}_{i})
and corresponds indeed to the variance of a Brownian bridge of duration
\delta^{L}_{i}+\delta^{R}_{i}
and evaluated at
\delta^{L}_{i}
or
\delta^{R}_{i}
.
By standard properties of the Brownian bridge, conditionally on these lengths and on the labels of the two ancestors
W_{g_{i}}
and
W_{d_{i}}
, the labels on each side of the cycle indeed evolve like two independent Brownian bridges, given precisely by
W^{L,i}
and
W^{R,i}
.

Lemma
3.11
thus shows the following alternative: either there exists a value
t_{r}\leq r^{2(1-\varepsilon)}
in the range of
\sigma
, say
t_{r}=\sigma(s_{r})
, and such that
W_{\sigma(s_{r})}\leq-r
.
In this case, the time
s_{r}
, once time-changed by the local time at the supremum of
\overleftarrow{X}
, corresponds to the first element of a pair
(-a_{r},b_{r})\in A_{0}
.
Otherwise there exists a jump of
\sigma
, say
g_{r}=\sigma(s_{r}-)<\sigma(s_{r})=d_{r}
, with
g_{r}<r^{2(1-\varepsilon)}
, which corresponds to a cycle in the looptree, and then there exists one point on each side of this cycle, both at distance (on the cycle) at most
r^{2(1-\varepsilon)}
from
g_{r}
and with label less than
-r
.

Notice the easy bound
\min\{u,1-u\}\leq 2u(1-u)
for any
0\leq u\leq 1
which shows that the subordinators
\sigma
and
\widetilde{X}
from Lemma
3.5
are related by

\widetilde{X}\leq 2\sigma
.
Observe that
\widetilde{X}
encodes the looptree distance to
0
of the successive ancestors.
Therefore, in the first case of the alternative, the time
-a_{r}
lies at looptree distance at most
2r^{2(1-\varepsilon)}
from
0
. So does the time corresponding to
g_{r}
in the second case, and thus the two points on each side of the cycle lie at looptree distance at most
3r^{2(1-\varepsilon)}
from
0
. One can then get rid of this factor
3
by replacing
\varepsilon
by a smaller value.
∎

3.5.
Proof of the technical lemma

Let us end this section with the proof of Lemma
3.11
.
The statement can be rephrased as follows: if the Brownian motion
W
is such that
W_{t}>-r
for all
t\in\mathcal{C}\cap[0,r^{2(1-\varepsilon)}]
, then necessarily the two Brownian bridges we resample both reach
-r
in the same interval in
\mathcal{C}^{c}\cap[0,r^{2(1-\varepsilon)}]
.

We start with an easy intermediate result. We shall denote by
\tau_{z}=\inf\{t\geq 0\colon W_{t}=z\}
the first hitting time of
z\in\mathbb{R}
by the Brownian motion
W
.
For every
r\in(0,1)
small enough, we let

K_{r}
be the largest integer such that

K_{r}\leq\frac{\log(1/r)}{(\log\log(1/r))^{3}}.

(3.7)

Lemma 3.12
.

Almost surely, for every
r\in\{2^{-n},n\in\mathbb{N}\}
small enough, the following assertions hold:

(i)

The path
W
reaches
-r\log^{10K_{r}}(1/r)
before time
r^{2(1-1/\log\log(1/r))}
,

(ii)

For every
k\leq K_{r}
, the path
W
reaches
-r\log^{10k}(1/r)
before
r\log^{10k+3}(1/r)
,

(iii)

There exists a universal constant
c\in(0,1)
such that for at least
cK_{r}
different indices
k\leq K_{r}
the following holds:
at the first time the path
W
reaches
-r\log^{10k}(1/r)
, it was below
-r
for a duration at least
(r\log^{10k}(1/r))^{2}
and it previously never reached
r\log^{10k}(1/r)
.

Let us note that by comparing any
r\in(0,1)
to a negative power of
2
, the claims extend by monotonicity to any
r>0
small enough, provided a minor change in the exponents.

Proof.

Let us prove that for each event, the probability to fail is bounded above by a quantity which depends on
r
and such that the sum over all values of
r\in\{2^{-n},n\geq 1\}
is finite. The claims then follow from the Borel–Cantelli lemma.
For the first assertion, the reflection principle and the scaling property show that:

\displaystyle\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(\tau_{-r\log^{10K_{r}}(1/r)}>r^{2(1-1/\log\log(1/r))})

\displaystyle=\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(\inf\{W_{t},t\in[0,r^{2(1-1/\log\log(1/r))}]\}\geq-r\log^{10K_{r}}(1/r))

\displaystyle=\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(|W_{r^{2(1-1/\log\log(1/r))}}|\leq r\log^{10K_{r}}(1/r))

\displaystyle=\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(|W_{1}|\leq r^{1/\log\log(1/r)}\log^{10K_{r}}(1/r)).

Since
K_{r}=o(\log(1/r)/(\log\log(1/r))^{2})
, then for
r
small enough, we have

r^{1/\log\log(1/r)}\log^{10K_{r}}(1/r)\leq\exp\Bigl(-\frac{\log(1/r)}{2\log\log(1/r)}\Bigr),

whose sum of over all values of
r\in\{2^{-n},n\geq 1\}
is finite.
By a Taylor expansion of the last probability, written as the integral of the density, we obtain:

\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(\tau_{-r\log^{10K_{r}}(1/r)}>r^{2(1-1/\log\log(1/r))})=O\Bigl(\exp\Bigl(-\frac{\log(1/r)}{2\log\log(1/r)}\Bigr)\Bigr),

hence the sum of the left-hand side over all values of
r\in\{2^{-n},n\geq 1\}
is finite as we wanted.

Next, by the classical ruin problem, we have:

\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(\tau_{r\log^{10k+3}(1/r)}<\tau_{-r\log^{10k}(1/r)})=\frac{r\log^{10k}(1/r)}{r\log^{10k}(1/r)+r\log^{10k+3}(1/r)}=\frac{1}{1+\log^{3}(1/r)}.

Since
K_{r}=o(\log(1/r))
, then we infer by a union bound that

\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(\exists k\leq K_{r}\colon\tau_{r\log^{10k+3}(1/r)}<\tau_{-r\log^{10k}(1/r)})=o(\log^{-2}(1/r)),

and again the right-hand side forms a convergent series when summing over all values
r\in\{2^{-n},n\in\mathbb{N}\}
.

For the last claim, by symmetry, for any given
k
, the probability that
W
reaches
-r\log^{10k}(1/r)
before
r\log^{10k}(1/r)
equals
1/2
so if these events were independent, then this would follow e.g. from Hoeffding’s inequality. They are not independent, however by the previous bound, we may assume that at the first time
W
reaches
-r\log^{10k}(1/r)
, it has not reached
r\log^{10(k+1)}(1/r)
yet, and by the strong Markov property, the probability starting from this position to reach
-r\log^{10(k+1)}(1/r)
before
r\log^{10(k+1)}(1/r)
is (slightly) larger than
1/2
. Formally, one can e.g. apply the Azuma inequality to the martingale whose increments are

\Delta M_{k}=\mathbf{1}_{\{\tau_{-r\log^{10(k+1)}(1/r)}<\tau_{r\log^{10(k+1)}(1/r)}\}}-\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(\tau_{-r\log^{10(k+1)}(1/r)}<\tau_{r\log^{10(k+1)}(1/r)}\mid(W_{t},t\leq\tau_{-r\log^{10k}(1/r)})),

and since the conditional probability on the right is larger than
1/2
, then

\displaystyle\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\#\{k\leq K_{r}\colon\tau_{-r\log^{10(k+1)}(1/r)}<\tau_{r\log^{10(k+1)}(1/r)}\}<\frac{K_{r}}{4}\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\sum_{k=1}^{K_{r}}\Bigl(\mathbf{1}_{\{\tau_{-r\log^{10(k+1)}(1/r)}<\tau_{r\log^{10(k+1)}(1/r)}\}}-\frac{1}{2}\Bigr)<-\frac{K_{r}}{4}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\sum_{k=1}^{K_{r}}\Delta M_{k}<-\frac{K_{r}}{4}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\exp(-cK_{r}),

where
c>0
is some universal constant.
The sum of these exponentials over all values
r\in\{2^{-n},n\in\mathbb{N}\}
is finite again.

Similarly, for any
z>0
, the law of the time-reversed path
(z+W_{(\tau_{-z}-t)^{+}})_{t\geq 0}
is that of a three-dimensional Bessel process started from
0
, or equivalently the Euclidean norm of a three-dimensional Brownian motion, stopped at the last passage at
z
. The probability that a Bessel process does not reach
z/2
before time
z^{2}
is bounded below by some constant
c>0
independent of
z
.
We conclude as above, that the proportion of the number of indices
k\leq K_{r}
such that this occurs for
z=r\log^{10k}(1/r)
is bounded away from
0
.
∎

Let us finally prove Lemma
3.11
.

Proof of Lemma
3.11
.

Let us work on the event that
W_{t}>-r
for all
t\in\mathcal{C}\cap[0,r^{2(1-\varepsilon)}]
and let us prove that necessarily the second case of the alternative occurs.
By monotonicity, it is sufficient to prove the claim when
r
is restricted to the set
\{2^{-n},n\in\mathbb{N}\}
.
Let us implicitly assume also that the three assertions from Lemma
3.12
hold, and let us prove that there exists
k\leq K_{r}
, the integer from (
3.7
), such that on the interval of
\mathcal{C}^{c}
on which
W
reaches
-r\log^{10k}(1/r)
for the first time, the two bridges that we sample also reach
-r
.

Let us introduce some notation. For every
k\geq 1
, denote by
G_{k}
and
D_{k}
the boundaries
g_{i}
and
d_{i}
of the unique interval
(g_{i},d_{i})\subset\mathcal{C}^{c}
such that

W
reaches the level
-r\log^{10k}(1/r)
for the first time in the interval
(g_{i},d_{i})
.
Note that these intervals are not necessarily distinct from each others and that when
r
is small enough, by Lemma
3.12
, they all intersect
(0,r^{2(1-\varepsilon)})
for
k\leq K_{r}
, and only
D_{K_{r}}
may exceed
r^{2(1-\varepsilon)}
.
Let
\Delta_{k}=D_{k}-G_{k}
and let
\Delta^{L}_{k},\Delta^{R}_{k}\geq\Delta_{k}
denote the corresponding extensions; let us change the notation and write
W^{L,k}
and
W^{R,k}
for the Brownian bridges with duration
\Delta^{L}_{k}
and
\Delta^{R}_{k}
respectively, with initial and terminal value
W_{G_{k}}
and
W_{D_{k}}
respectively.
We shall upper bound the probability of the event

A_{r}=\bigcap_{k\leq K_{r}}\Bigl(\Bigl\{\inf_{s\in[0,\Delta^{L}_{k}]\cap(0,r^{2(1-\varepsilon)})}W^{L,k}_{s}>-r\Bigr\}\cup\Bigl\{\inf_{t\in[0,\Delta^{R}_{k}]\cap(0,r^{2(1-\varepsilon)})}W^{R,k}_{t}>-r\Bigr\}\Bigr)

by a quantity that only depends on
r
and whose sum over all values of
r\in\{2^{-n},n\geq 1\}
is finite, such as
\log^{-2}(1/r)
.
We then conclude as in the previous proof of from the Borel–Cantelli lemma.

Step 1: Proof of for Brownian motions.

Let us first prove the claim when the Brownian bridges
W^{L}
and
W^{R}
are replaced by Brownian motions. Precisely, for every
k\leq K_{r}
, conditionally on
\Delta_{k}
,
\Delta_{k}^{L}
,
\Delta_{k}^{R}
, and
W_{G_{k}}
, let
X^{L,k}=(X^{L,k}_{t};t\in[0,\Delta_{k}^{L}])
and
X^{R,k}=(X^{R,k}_{t};t\in[0,\Delta_{k}^{L}])
be two independent Brownian motions started from
X^{L,k}_{0}=X^{R,k}_{0}=W_{G_{k}}>-r
.

We first claim that if the interval
[G_{k},D_{k}]
is too long, then the Brownian motions
X^{L,k}
and
X^{R,k}
on it cannot reasonably stay above
-r
. Specifically, if we denote by
X
a generic Brownian motion independent of the rest, then by the same reasoning as in the previous proof, we have for every
k
:

\displaystyle\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\Delta_{k}>2(r+W_{G_{k}})^{2}\log^{6}(1/r)\enskip\text{and}\enskip\max\Bigl\{\inf_{t\in[0,\Delta_{k}/2]}X^{L,k}_{t},\inf_{t\in[0,\Delta_{k}/2]}X^{R,k}_{t}\Bigr\}>-r\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}\bigl(\inf\{X_{s},0\leq s\leq(r+W_{G_{k}})^{2}\log^{6}(1/r)\}>-(W_{G_{k}}+r)\bigr)

\displaystyle=2\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}\bigl(|X_{(r+W_{G_{k}})^{2}\log^{6}(1/r)}|<W_{G_{k}}+r\bigr)

\displaystyle=2\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(|X_{1}|<\log^{-3}(1/r))

\displaystyle=O(\log^{-3}(1/r)).

Since
K_{r}=o(\log(1/r))
, then a union bound combined with the Borel–Cantelli lemma imply that almost surely, all the events for
k\leq K_{r}
fail for
r
small enough (of the form
r=2^{-n}
).
Thus, in order to avoid that both
X^{L,k}
and
X^{R,k}
reach
-r
, we must have
\Delta_{k}\leq 2(r+W_{G_{k}})^{2}\log^{6}(1/r)
for every
k\leq K_{r}
.
Since
G_{k}
is smaller than the first hitting time of
-r\log^{10k}(1/r)
by definition, then combined with Lemma
3.12
, we have:

-r<W_{G_{k}}\leq r\log^{10k+3}(1/r)\qquad\text{and so}\qquad\Delta_{k}\leq 3(r\log^{10k+6}(1/r))^{2}\qquad\text{for each}\enskip k\leq K_{r}.

According to the strong Markov property applied at the first hitting time of
-r\log^{10k}(1/r)
, the path
W
behaves after this time as an unconditioned Brownian motion started from this value during the remaining time of the interval
[G_{k},D_{k}]
, which is at most
3(r\log^{10k+6}(1/r))^{2}
on the previous event.
This is not enough for
W
to further reach
-r\log^{10(k+1)}(1/r)
. Indeed, if
X
is a generic Brownian motion, then:

\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\inf_{[0,4(r\log^{10k+6}(1/r))^{2}]}X<-r\log^{10k+8}(1/r)\Bigr)=\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}\Bigl(|X_{1}|>\frac{1}{2}\log^{2}(1/r)\Bigr)\leq 2\exp\Bigl(-\frac{1}{4}\log^{2}(1/r)\Bigr),

and the right-hand side, multiplied by
K_{r}
, is again a convergent series when summing over all values
r\in\{2^{-n},n\in\mathbb{N}\}
.
Hence, we may also assume that the intervals for different values of
k\leq K_{r}
are disjoint, and so the Brownian motions
X^{L,k}
and
X^{R,k}
that we sample are independent.

Finally, by the last claim of Lemma
3.12
, for a positive proportion of the indices
k\leq K_{r}
we have both
W_{G_{k}}\leq r\log^{10k}(1/r)
and
\Delta_{k}>(r\log^{10k}(1/r))^{2}
, so there is a probability uniformly bounded away from
0
that both
X^{L,k}
and
X^{R,k}
reach
-r
, in the first half of the interval.
We conclude by independence that this occurs for a positive proportion of the indices
k\leq K_{r}
.

Step 2: Proof of for Brownian bridges.

Let us next prove the actual statement by comparing the Brownian motions
X^{L,k}
and
X^{R,k}
with Brownian bridges
W^{L,k}
and
W^{R,k}
. We aim at proving that the probability that one of these bridges stays above
-r
in the first half of the interval when
\Delta_{k}>(r+W_{G_{k}})^{2}\log^{6}(1/r)
is summable when
r
ranges over
\{2^{-n},n\in\mathbb{N}\}
, and then that if both
W_{G_{k}}\leq r\log^{10k}(1/r)
and
\Delta_{k}>(r\log^{10k}(1/r))^{2}
, then there is a probability bounded away from
0
uniformly in
r
and
k
that the two bridges both reach
-r
.
Let us only consider
X^{L,k}
and
W^{L,k}
since the same argument applies to
X^{R,k}
and
W^{R,k}
.
Since, conditionally on
W_{G_{k}}
,
W_{D_{k}}
,
\Delta_{k}
, and
\Delta^{L}_{k}
, the path
W^{L,k}
is a Brownian bridge from
W_{G_{k}}
to
W_{D_{k}}
with duration
\Delta^{L}_{k}\geq\Delta_{k}
, then the following absolute continuity relation holds: for any measurable and nonnegative function
F
,

\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}\bigl[F(W^{L,k}_{t},t\leq\Delta^{L}_{k}/2)\mid W_{G_{k}},W_{D_{k}},\Delta_{k},\Delta^{L}_{k}\bigr]=\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}\Bigl[F(X^{L,k}_{t},t\leq\Delta^{L}_{k}/2)\,\frac{p_{\Delta^{L}_{k}/2}(W_{D_{k}}-X^{L}_{\Delta^{L}_{k}/2})}{p_{\Delta^{L}_{k}}(W_{D_{k}}-W_{G_{k}})}\bigm|W_{G_{k}},W_{D_{k}},\Delta_{k},\Delta^{L}_{k}\Bigr],

where
p_{t}(x)=(2\pi t)^{-1/2}\exp(-x^{2}/(2t))
.
We then integrate with respect to
W_{D_{k}}
. Precisely, we claim that there exists a constant
C>0
such that
on the event
\{W_{G_{k}}>-r,W_{D_{k}}>-r,\Delta_{k}>2(r+W_{G_{k}})^{2}\log^{6}(1/r)\}
, we have

\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\frac{p_{\Delta^{L}_{k}/2}(W_{D_{k}}-X^{L}_{\Delta^{L}_{k}/2})}{p_{\Delta^{L}_{k}}(W_{D_{k}}-W_{G_{k}})}\bigm|X^{L}_{\Delta^{L}_{k}/2},W_{G_{k}},\Delta_{k},\Delta^{L}_{k}\Bigr]\leq C.

(3.8)

This allows to infer from the first part of the proof of that

\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\Delta_{k}>2(r+W_{G_{k}})^{2}\log^{6}(1/r)\enskip\text{and}\enskip\max\Bigl\{\inf_{t\in[0,\Delta_{k}/2]}W^{L,k}_{t},\inf_{t\in[0,\Delta_{k}/2]}W^{R,k}_{t}\Bigr\}>-r\Bigr)=O(\log^{-3}(1/r)),

and then that, as in the first part, we must impose
\Delta_{k}\leq 2(r+W_{G_{k}})^{2}\log^{6}(1/r)\leq 3(r\log^{10k+6}(1/r))^{2}
for every
k\leq K_{r}
in order to prevent the bridges
W^{L,k}
and
W^{R,k}
to reach
-r
.

Let us prove the upper bound (
3.8
).
Conditionally on
W_{G_{k}}
and
\Delta_{k}
, the path
(W_{t};t\in[G_{k},D_{k}])
has the law of a Brownian motion
Y
with duration
\Delta_{k}
, started from
W_{G_{k}}
, and conditioned on the event
E_{k}=\{\inf\{Y_{t};t\in[G_{k},D_{k}]\}\leq-r\log^{10k}(1/r)\}
. Then the law of
W_{D_{k}}
can be obtained by splitting at the first hitting time of this value; by the Markov, property, the remaining path after is an unconditioned Brownian motion. Recall also that we work implicitly on the event where
W_{G_{k}},W_{D_{k}}>-r
.
For
z>0
, let us write
q_{z}
for the density of the first hitting time of
-z
by a standard Brownian motion,
then:

\displaystyle\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\frac{p_{\Delta^{L}_{k}/2}(W_{D_{k}}-X^{L}_{\Delta^{L}_{k}/2})}{p_{\Delta^{L}_{k}}(W_{D_{k}}-W_{G_{k}})}\mathbf{1}_{\{W_{G_{k}},W_{D_{k}}>-r\}}\bigm|X^{L}_{\Delta^{L}_{k}/2},W_{G_{k}},\Delta_{k},\Delta^{L}_{k}\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(E_{k})}\int_{0}^{\Delta_{k}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\int_{-r}^{\infty}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\ q_{r\log^{10k}(1/r)+W_{G_{k}}}(t)p_{\Delta_{k}-t}(y+r\log^{10k}(1/r))\frac{p_{\Delta^{L}_{k}/2}(y-X^{L}_{\Delta^{L}_{k}/2})}{p_{\Delta^{L}_{k}}(y-W_{G_{k}})}\mathbf{1}_{\{W_{G_{k}}>-r\}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(E_{k})}\int_{0}^{\Delta_{k}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\frac{q_{r\log^{10k}(1/r)+W_{G_{k}}}(t)}{2\sqrt{\pi(\Delta_{k}-t)}}\int_{-r}^{\infty}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\exp\Bigl(\frac{(y-W_{G_{k}})^{2}}{2\Delta^{L}_{k}}-\frac{(y-X^{L}_{\Delta^{L}_{k}/2})^{2}}{\Delta^{L}_{k}}-\frac{(y+r\log^{10k}(1/r))^{2}}{2(\Delta_{k}-t)}\Bigr)\mathbf{1}_{\{W_{G_{k}}>-r\}}.

We have
\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(E_{k})=\int_{0}^{\Delta_{k}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}tq_{r\log^{10k}(1/r)+W_{G_{k}}}(t)
, it thus remains to prove that the integral in
y
of the rest of the expression is bounded, when in addition
\Delta_{k}>2(r+W_{G_{k}})^{2}\log^{6}(1/r)
.
Let us first consider the integral over
[W_{G_{k}},\infty)
. Indeed, if
y>W_{G_{k}}>-r
, then
(y-W_{G_{k}})^{2}\leq y^{2}\leq(y+r\log^{10k}(1/r))^{2}
and consequently

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi(\Delta_{k}-t)}}\exp\Bigl(\frac{(y-W_{G_{k}})^{2}}{2\Delta^{L}_{k}}-\frac{(y-X^{L}_{\Delta^{L}_{k}/2})^{2}}{\Delta^{L}_{k}}-\frac{(y+r\log^{10k}(1/r))^{2}}{2(\Delta_{k}-t)}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi(\Delta_{k}-t)}}\exp\Bigl(-\frac{(y+r\log^{10k}(1/r))^{2}}{2}\Bigl(\frac{1}{\Delta_{k}-t}-\frac{1}{\Delta^{L}_{k}}\Bigr)-\frac{(y-X^{L}_{\Delta^{L}_{k}/2})^{2}}{\Delta^{L}_{k}}\Bigr).

Suppose first that
\Delta^{L}_{k}\geq 2\Delta_{k}
so
\Delta^{L}_{k}\geq 2(\Delta_{k}-t)
for any
t\in(0,\Delta_{k})
and thus
\frac{1}{\Delta_{k}-t}-\frac{1}{\Delta^{L}_{k}}\geq\frac{1}{2(\Delta_{k}-t)}
. In this case, by simply removing the last term in the exponential, we have:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi(\Delta_{k}-t)}}\exp\Bigl(-\frac{(y+r\log^{10k}(1/r))^{2}}{2}\Bigl(\frac{1}{\Delta_{k}-t}-\frac{1}{\Delta^{L}_{k}}\Bigr)-\frac{(y-X^{L}_{\Delta^{L}_{k}/2})^{2}}{\Delta^{L}_{k}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi(\Delta_{k}-t)}}\exp\Bigl(-\frac{(y+r\log^{10k}(1/r))^{2}}{4(\Delta_{k}-t)}\Bigr),

which is the density of a Gaussian law, in particular its integral over the entire real line equals
1
. If
\Delta^{L}_{k}
is close to
\Delta_{k}
and
t
is small, one has to be more careful.
Suppose now that
\Delta_{k}\leq\Delta^{L}_{k}\leq 2\Delta_{k}
, then,

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi(\Delta_{k}-t)}}\exp\Bigl(-\frac{(y+r\log^{10k}(1/r))^{2}}{2}\Bigl(\frac{1}{\Delta_{k}-t}-\frac{1}{\Delta^{L}_{k}}\Bigr)-\frac{(y-X^{L}_{\Delta^{L}_{k}/2})^{2}}{\Delta^{L}_{k}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi(\Delta_{k}-t)}}\exp\Bigl(-\frac{(y+r\log^{10k}(1/r))^{2}}{2}\frac{t}{\Delta_{k}(\Delta_{k}-t)}-\frac{(y-X^{L}_{\Delta^{L}_{k}/2})^{2}}{2\Delta_{k}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi\Delta_{k}/2}}\exp\Bigl(-\frac{(y-X^{L}_{\Delta^{L}_{k}/2})^{2}}{2\Delta_{k}}\Bigr)\mathbf{1}_{\{0<t\leq\Delta_{k}/2\}}+\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi(\Delta_{k}-t)}}\exp\Bigl(-\frac{(y+r\log^{10k}(1/r))^{2}}{4(\Delta_{k}-t)}\Bigr)\mathbf{1}_{\{\Delta_{k}/2<t<\Delta_{k}\}}.

Again the integral over the entire real line is constant.
Finally, if
-r<y<W_{G_{k}}
then
(y-W_{G_{k}})^{2}\leq(r+W_{G_{k}})^{2}
. Here we use that we work on the event
\Delta_{k}>2(r+W_{G_{k}})^{2}\log^{6}(1/r)\geq 2(y-W_{G_{k}})^{2}\log^{6}(1/r)
. Indeed, this implies:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi(\Delta_{k}-t)}}\exp\Bigl(\frac{(y-W_{G_{k}})^{2}}{2\Delta^{L}_{k}}-\frac{(y-X^{L}_{\Delta^{L}_{k}/2})^{2}}{\Delta^{L}_{k}}-\frac{(y+r\log^{10k}(1/r))^{2}}{2(\Delta_{k}-t)}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi(\Delta_{k}-t)}}\exp\Bigl(\frac{1}{4\log^{6}(1/r)}-\frac{(y+r\log^{10k}(1/r))^{2}}{2(\Delta_{k}-t)}\Bigr),

and again the integral over
y
of the last line is uniformly bounded above.
This completes the proof of the bound (
3.8
).

Let us finally lower bound the probability that the Brownian bridge
W^{L,k}
reaches
-r
in the first half of the interval when both
-r<W_{G_{k}}\leq r\log^{10k}(1/r)
and
\Delta_{k}>(r\log^{10k}(1/r))^{2}
.
We argue similarly: conditionally on
\Delta^{L}_{k}
,
W_{G_{k}}
, and
W_{D_{k}}
, this probability equals

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\Delta^{L}_{k}/2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\ q_{-(r+W_{G_{k}})}(t)\frac{p_{\Delta^{L}_{k}-t}(W_{D_{k}}+r)}{p_{\Delta^{L}_{k}}(W_{D_{k}}-W_{G_{k}})}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\Delta^{L}_{k}/2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\ q_{-(r+W_{G_{k}})}(t)\sqrt{\frac{\Delta^{L}_{k}}{\Delta^{L}_{k}-t}}\exp\Bigl(\frac{(W_{D_{k}}-W_{G_{k}})^{2}}{2\Delta^{L}_{k}}-\frac{(W_{D_{k}}+r)^{2}}{2(\Delta^{L}_{k}-t)}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\geq\int_{0}^{\Delta_{k}/2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\ q_{-(r+W_{G_{k}})}(t)\sqrt{2}\exp\Bigl(-\frac{(W_{D_{k}}+r)^{2}}{2(\Delta_{k}-t)}\Bigr).

Without the exponential, the last integral is bounded away from
0
.
We then lower bound the expectation of the exponential with respect to
W_{D_{k}}
.
Recall that conditionally on
W_{G_{k}}
and
\Delta_{k}
, the path
(W_{t};t\in[G_{k},D_{k}])
has the law of a Brownian motion started from
W_{G_{k}}
and conditioned on the event
E_{k}
that it reaches
-r\log^{10k}(1/r)
at some point in the time interval, so by splitting at the first hitting time of this value, we have since we restrict our attention to
t\leq\Delta_{k}/2
,

\displaystyle\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\exp\Bigl(-\frac{(W_{D_{k}}+r)^{2}}{2(\Delta_{k}-t)}\Bigr)\mathbf{1}_{\{W_{G_{k}},W_{D_{k}}>-r\}}\bigm|W_{G_{k}},\Delta_{k}\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(E_{k})}\int_{0}^{\Delta_{k}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\int_{-r}^{\infty}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\ \frac{q_{-(r\log^{10k}(1/r)+W_{G_{k}})}(s)}{\sqrt{2\pi(\Delta_{k}-s)}}\exp\Bigl(-\frac{(y+r\log^{10k}(1/r))^{2}}{2(\Delta_{k}-s)}-\frac{(y+r)^{2}}{2(\Delta_{k}-t)}\Bigr)\mathbf{1}_{\{W_{G_{k}}>-r\}}

\displaystyle\geq\int_{0}^{\Delta_{k}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\int_{-r}^{\infty}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\ \frac{q_{-(r\log^{10k}(1/r)+W_{G_{k}})}(s)}{\sqrt{2\pi(\Delta_{k}-s)}}\exp\Bigl(-\frac{(y+r\log^{10k}(1/r))^{2}}{2}\Bigl(\frac{1}{\Delta_{k}-s}+\frac{1}{\Delta_{k}-t}\Bigr)\Bigr)\mathbf{1}_{\{W_{G_{k}}>-r\}}

\displaystyle\geq\int_{0}^{\Delta_{k}/2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\int_{-r}^{\infty}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\ \frac{q_{-(r\log^{10k}(1/r)+W_{G_{k}})}(s)}{\sqrt{2\pi\Delta_{k}}}\exp\Bigl(-2\frac{(y+r\log^{10k}(1/r))^{2}}{\Delta_{k}}\Bigr)\mathbf{1}_{\{W_{G_{k}}>-r\}}.

Consider the integral over
y
only in the last line, and without de density
q
. It equals half the probability that a Gaussian random variable with mean
-r\log^{10k}(1/r)
and variance
\Delta_{k}/4
is larger than
-r
, or equivalently that a Gaussian random variable with mean
0
and variance
1
is larger than
2(r\log^{10k}(1/r)-r)/\sqrt{\Delta_{k}}
. Since
\Delta_{k}>(r\log^{10k}(1/r))^{2}
, this probability is indeed bounded away from
0
uniformly in
k
and
r
.
Then the integral of
q
over
s
equals the probability that a standard Brownian motion reaches
-(W_{G_{k}}+r\log^{10k}(1/r))
in less than
\Delta_{k}/2
when
W_{G_{k}}\leq r\log^{10k}(1/r)\leq\sqrt{\Delta_{k}}
, which is again bounded away from
0
.

Combined with the results on
X^{L,k}
, we infer that there exists some constant
c>0
such that for all
r<1
, on each interval
[G_{k},D_{k}]
with
W_{G_{k}}\leq r\log^{10k}(1/r)
and
\Delta_{k}\geq(r\log^{10k}(1/r))^{2}
, there is a probability at least
c
that
W^{L,k}
reaches the value
-r
in the first half of the interval. The same holds for
X^{L,k}
and since they are independent, they both reach the value
-r
in the first half with probability at least
c^{2}
. We can then conclude from Lemma
3.12
that this occurs for a positive proportion of indices
k\leq K_{r}
.
∎

4.
Computation of the dimensions

Throughout this section, we let

{L}(X^{\mathrm{ex}})
denote the looptree constructed from the excursion
X^{\mathrm{ex}}
and as in the previous section, we let
(M,d_{M},p_{M})
denote a subsequential limit of discrete random maps, related to a pair
(X^{\mathrm{ex}},Z^{\mathrm{ex}})
, from Theorem
2.1
and Theorem
2.4
. In addition, thanks to Skorokhod’s representation theorem, we assume that all convergences in these theorems hold in the almost sure sense.
Our main goal is to compute the dimensions of these looptrees and maps stated in Theorem
1.1
and Theorem
1.2
respectively.
The upper bound on the dimensions will follow from Hölder continuity estimates established in
Section
4.1

while the lower bounds will follow in Section
4.2
from the estimates on the volume of balls with small radius established in the previous section.

4.1.
Hölder continuity of the distances

Recall from (
1.2
) the upper and lower exponents
\boldsymbol{\eta}
and
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
of
\psi
at infinity.
The main goal of this subsection is to prove that the canonical projections
[0,1]\to{L}(X^{\mathrm{ex}})
and
[0,1]\to M
are almost surely Hölder continuous with exponent
a
and
a/2
respectively for any
a\in(0,1/\boldsymbol{\eta})
.
We shall need the following tail bound.

Lemma 4.1
.

There exists a function
h\colon(0,\infty)\to(0,\infty)
and a constant
C>0
such that for every
t,x>0
,

\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\sup_{s\in[0,t]}|X_{s}|>x\Bigr)\leq C\cdot t\cdot h(x).

Furthermore, it has the following asymptotic behaviour near
0
:

\inf\Bigl\{c\geq 0\colon\limsup_{x\downarrow 0}x^{c}h(x)=0\Bigr\}=\boldsymbol{\eta}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\inf\Bigl\{c\geq 0\colon\liminf_{x\downarrow 0}x^{c}h(x)=0\Bigr\}=\boldsymbol{\gamma}.

(4.1)

Proof.

Let us first assume that
X
has no drift and no Gaussian component. We then deduce the general case in the end. The bound on the tail probability is given by Pruitt
[
Pru81
]
, even in the case where
X
may also have negative jumps, see Equation 3.2 there.
By combining
[
Pru81
, Equation 3.4]
with the work
[
BG61
]
, one can derive the upper exponent
\boldsymbol{\eta}
for
h
, but only lower bound the lower exponent of
h
by
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
.
Let us give an alternative proof of and prove that
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
is precisely the lower exponent of
h
.
The function
h
is given in
[
Pru81
]
(up to the choice of the convention for the Lévy–Khintchine formula) by:

h(x)=\overline{\pi}(x)+x^{-2}\int_{(0,x]}r^{2}\pi(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)+x^{-1}\int_{(x,\infty)}r\pi(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)

where we have set
\overline{\pi}(x)=\pi((x,\infty))
.
We claim that

h(x)\asymp\psi(1/x)\qquad\text{in }(0,1),

(4.2)

by which we mean that there exist two constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that
c_{1}\psi(1/x)\leq h(x)\leq c_{2}\psi(1/x)
for every
x\in(0,1)
.
The exponents of
h
will then follow from those of
\psi
in (
1.2
).

To establish (
4.2
) we rely on
[
Duq12
, Section 2.3]
.
Indeed, recall that
\phi=\psi^{\prime}
is the Laplace exponent of a subordinator which has Lévy measure
\nu(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)=r\pi(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
. Then
h(x)-\overline{\pi}(x)
equals precisely
x^{-2}J_{\psi^{\prime}}(x)
in the notation of
[
Duq12
, Equation 47]
. Recall the notation
\widetilde{\psi}(u)=\psi(u)/u
. By combining Equations 47 and 50 in
[
Duq12
]
, we infer that

h(x)-\overline{\pi}(x)=x^{-2}J_{\psi^{\prime}}(x)\asymp x^{-1}\psi^{\prime}(1/x)\asymp x^{-1}\widetilde{\psi}(1/x)=\psi(1/x),

in
(0,1)
.
This already gives the lower bound in (
4.2
) by simply lower bounding
\overline{\pi}
by
0
. As for the upper bound, simply observe that for every
x>0
, we have
\overline{\pi}(x)\leq x^{-1}\int_{(x,\infty)}r\pi(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r)
so
\overline{\pi}
is also upper bounded by some constant times
\psi(1/\cdot)
by the previous argument. This concludes the proof of of (
4.2
) when
\beta=0
.

In the case where
X
has a drift
d\neq 0
and a Gaussian parameter
\beta>0
, the Lévy–Itō decomposition reads
X=D+W+Y
in distribution, where
D_{t}=dt
, where
W
is
\sqrt{\beta}
times a standard Brownian motion, and where
Y
is independent of
W
and has no drift nor Gaussian component.
Recall that in this case, we have
\psi(\lambda)\sim\beta\lambda^{2}
as
\lambda\to\infty
.
By the first part of the proof,
Y
satisfies the claim with some
1\leq\boldsymbol{\gamma}\leq\boldsymbol{\eta}\leq 2
. So does
W
, with
h(x)=x^{-2}
, by e.g. an application of the reflection principle and a well-known tail bound for Gaussian random variables:

\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\sup_{s\in[0,t]}|W_{s}|>x\Bigr)\leq 4\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(W_{t}>x)\leq 4\exp\Bigl(-\frac{x^{2}}{2\beta t}\Bigr)\leq\frac{4}{\mathrm{e}}\frac{2\beta t}{x^{2}}.

The claim, with
h(x)=x^{-2}
, then easily follows from a union bound.
∎

Remark 4.2
.

Let us mention that the lower exponent of
\overline{\pi}
near
0
may be smaller than
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
, even in the case of a subordinator, see e.g. Theorems 6.1 and 7.1 in
[
BG61
]
for some more information.

Recall from
[
Mar22b
, Proposition 3.2]
that the distance function
d_{{L}(X^{\mathrm{ex}})}
from (
2.6
) is almost surely a continuous pseudo-distance, so the canonical projection

\mathbf{p}_{{L}(X^{\mathrm{ex}})}\colon[0,1]\to{L}(X^{\mathrm{ex}})

is almost surely continuous.
The next proposition shows that it is Hölder continuous; we will easily derive from this the upper bound for the Hausdorff and upper Minkowski dimensions of the looptree.

Proposition 4.3
.

For any
a\in(0,1/\boldsymbol{\eta})
, the projection
\mathbf{p}_{{L}(X^{\mathrm{ex}})}
is almost surely Hölder continuous with exponent
a
in the sense that almost surely, there exists
C_{a}\in(0,\infty)
such that:

d_{{L}(X^{\mathrm{ex}})}(s,t)\leq C_{a}\cdot|t-s|^{a}\qquad\text{for all }s,t\in[0,1].

(4.3)

We shall see that the exponent
1/\boldsymbol{\eta}
is optimal in the sense that
\mathbf{p}_{{L}(X^{\mathrm{ex}})}
is almost surely not Hölder continuous with exponent larger than
1/\boldsymbol{\eta}
, see Remark
4.7
.
As the proof of will show, the claim also holds for the unconditioned Lévy process
X
and its bridge version.

Proof.

We claim that given
a<1/\boldsymbol{\eta}
, there exists a deterministic constant
K_{a}\in(0,\infty)
which only depends on
a
, not even on the law of the Lévy process, such that almost surely, there exists
n_{0}\geq 1
(random, and which may depend on the law of the Lévy process) such that for every
n\geq n_{0}
, we have:

\sup_{0\leq i\leq 2^{n}-1}d_{{L}(X^{\mathrm{ex}})}(i2^{-n},(i+1)2^{-n})\leq K_{a}2^{-an}.

(4.4)

For
n\leq n_{0}
, there are only finitely many quantities involved, so we infer that almost surely, there exists a now random
C_{a}\in(0,\infty)
such that (
4.3
) holds for every pair
(s,t)
of the form
(j2^{-n},(j+1)2^{-n})
with
n\geq 1
and
j\in\{0,\dots,2^{n}-1\}
. It then extends to all dyadic numbers by a routing argument, after multiplying
C_{a}
by
2/(1-2^{-a})
, and then to the whole interval
[0,1]
by density, using that
d_{{L}(X^{\mathrm{ex}})}
is continuous almost surely. It remains to prove (
4.4
).
Let us first replace
X^{\mathrm{ex}}
by the unconditioned process
X
and then argue by absolute continuity.
According to (
2.7
), almost surely, for
s<t
, we have:

d_{{L}(X^{\mathrm{ex}})}(s,t)\leq X_{s}+X_{t-}-2\inf_{r\in[s,t]}X_{r}\leq 4\sup_{r\in[s,t]}|X_{r}-X_{s}|.

Indeed, informally the term in the middle is the length of the path if one goes from
s
to
t
in the looptree by always following a cycle on its right, instead of optimising between left and right.
Note that the Markov property implies that the right-hand side has the same law as
4\sup_{r\in[0,t-s]}|X_{r}|
.
This bound is too crude to be used directly because of the jumps of
X
, but we shall use it between two consecutive large jumps.

Precisely, fix
\delta>0
and let
\theta=(1+\delta)(\boldsymbol{\eta}+\delta)
; we shall take
a=1/\theta
.
Fix
n\geq 1
and
i\in\{0,\dots,2^{n}-1\}
. Let
(t_{n,i}(1),\dots,t_{n,i}(N_{n,i}))
denote the increasing enumeration of the times
\{t\in[i2^{-n},(i+1)2^{-n}]\colon\Delta X_{t}>2^{-n/\theta}\}
. Set also
t_{n,i}(0)=i2^{-n}
and
t_{n,i}(N_{n,i}+1)=(i+1)2^{-n}
.
Then combining the last display with the triangle inequality, we have:

d_{{L}(X^{\mathrm{ex}})}(i2^{-n},(i+1)2^{-n})\leq 4\sum_{j=1}^{N_{n,i}}\sup_{r\in[t_{n,i}(j),t_{n,i}(j+1)]}|X_{r}-X_{t_{n,i}(j)}|.

(4.5)

Let us first bound
N_{n,i}
. The latter has the Poisson distribution with rate
2^{-n}\overline{\pi}(2^{-n/\theta})
and the Chernoff bound shows that the tail probability of a Poisson random variable
Y
with rate
\lambda
satisfies
\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(Y\geq k)\leq\operatorname{e}^{-\lambda}(\operatorname{e}\lambda/k)^{k}
for every
k>\lambda
. Hence for any integer
N\geq 1
, there exists a constant
C_{N}
, which depends on
N
, such that:

\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(N_{n,i}\geq N)\leq C_{N}(2^{-n}\overline{\pi}(2^{-n/\theta}))^{N}.

Now recall from the proof of Lemma
4.1

that
\overline{\pi}
is upper bounded by some constant times
\psi(1/\cdot)
, so there exists a constant
C_{\delta}>0
such that

\overline{\pi}(x)\leq C_{\delta}x^{-(\boldsymbol{\eta}+\delta)}=C_{\delta}x^{-\theta/(1+\delta)}

for every
x\in(0,1)
.
We infer that

\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(N_{n,i}\geq N)\leq C_{N}C_{\delta}^{N}2^{-nN\delta/(1+\delta)}.

Let us next bound the variation of
X
between
t_{n,i}(j)
and
t_{n,i}(j+1)
.
By definition, between these times, the process
X
makes no jump larger than
2^{-n/\theta}
, therefore, at the first passage time above this level, the value is at most
2\times 2^{-n/\theta}
. Using the strong Markov property, one then easily gets by induction that for every integer
M\geq 1
, it holds:

\displaystyle\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\sup_{r\in[0,t_{n,i}(j+1)-t_{n,i}(j)]}|X_{r}|>2M2^{-n/\theta}\bigm|t_{n,i}(j),t_{n,i}(j+1)\Bigr)\leq\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\sup_{r\in[0,t_{n,i}(j+1)-t_{n,i}(j)]}|X_{r}|>2^{-n/\theta}\bigm|t_{n,i}(j),t_{n,i}(j+1)\Bigr)^{M}.

We next upper bound the probability on the right thanks to Lemma
4.1
. There exists a constant
C_{\delta}>0
such that, with the constant
C
and the function
h
from this lemma, we have
Ch(x)\leq C_{\delta}x^{-(\boldsymbol{\eta}+\delta)}=C_{\delta}x^{-\theta/(1+\delta)}
for every
x\in(0,1)
. Hence

\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\sup_{r\in[0,t_{n,i}(j+1)-t_{n,i}(j)]}|X_{r}|>2^{-n/\theta}\bigm|t_{n,i}(j),t_{n,i}(j+1)\Bigr)\leq C2^{-n}h(2^{-n/\theta})\leq C_{\delta}2^{-n\delta/(1+\delta)}.

Combined with union bounds, we obtain:

\displaystyle\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}\Bigl(N_{n,i}<N\quad\text{and}\quad 4\sum_{j=0}^{N_{n,i}}\sup_{r\in[t_{n,i}(j),t_{n,i}(j+1)]}|X_{r}-X_{t_{n,i}(j)}|>8N^{2}2^{-n/\theta}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}\Bigl(N_{n,i}<N\quad\text{and}\quad\exists j\leq N_{n,i}\colon\sup_{r\in[t_{n,i}(j),t_{n,i}(j+1)]}|X_{r}-X_{t_{n,i}(j)}|>2N2^{-n/\theta}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq NC_{\delta}^{M}2^{-nN\delta/(1+\delta)}.

We have thus shown that for every
N\geq 1
, there exists a constant
K
which depends on
N
and
\delta
, but neither on
i
nor
n
, such that

\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(d_{{L}(X^{\mathrm{ex}})}(i2^{-n},(i+1)2^{-n})>8N^{2}2^{-n/\theta})\leq K2^{-nN\delta/(1+\delta)}.

A further union bound implies then:

\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(\exists i\leq 2^{n}-1\colon d_{{L}(X^{\mathrm{ex}})}(i2^{-n},(i+1)2^{-n})>8N^{2}2^{-n/\theta})\leq K2^{n-nN\delta/(1+\delta)}.

If we take
N
such that
N\geq 2+\frac{1}{\delta}
, then the sum over all
n\geq 0
of the right-hand side is finite; the Borel–Cantelli lemma then yields (
4.4
) for the unconditioned Lévy process.

Actually, more than (
4.4
) we have shown that its upper bound (
4.5
), which is expressed in a simple way in terms of the Lévy process, is itself bounded as in (
4.4
).
This immediately extends to the first half the bridge
(X^{\mathrm{br}}_{t};0\leq t\leq 1/2)
, taking only the supremum over
i\in\{0,\dots,2^{n-1}\}
, by absolutely continuity with respect to the unconditioned process
(X_{t};0\leq t\leq 1/2)
.
In addition, the time- and space-reversal of the second half of the bridge has the same law as the first half, so the upper bound also holds for the second half and thus for the entire bridge.
Finally we transfer it to the excursion by applying the Vervaat transform. Notice that the latter changes the starting point and thus shifts the intervals, but this only mildly affects the constant
C_{a}
.
∎

Remark 4.4
.

Note that if one wanted to replace the exponent
1/\boldsymbol{\eta}
by the smaller one
1/\boldsymbol{\gamma}
, then instead of the uniform upper bound

h(x)\leq C_{\delta}x^{-(\boldsymbol{\eta}+\delta)}

for every
x\in(0,1)
, one would only have the existence of a sequence
x_{n}\downarrow 0
along which
\overline{\pi}(x_{n})\leq h(x_{n})\leq C_{\delta}x_{n}^{-(\boldsymbol{\gamma}+\delta)}
. Taking a subsequence if necessary, we may assume that
\sum_{n}x_{n}<\infty
and then the preceding argument shows that instead of (
4.4
) for any
a\in(0,1/\boldsymbol{\gamma})
, almost surely there exist
K_{a}>0
and a sequence
\varepsilon_{n}\downarrow 0
such that, for every
n
large enough,

d_{{L}(X^{\mathrm{ex}})}(i\varepsilon_{n},(i+1)\varepsilon_{n})\leq K_{a}\varepsilon_{n}^{a}\qquad\text{for all}\enskip i\in\{0,\dots,\lfloor\varepsilon_{n}^{-1}\rfloor-1\}.

This will be used to upper bound the packing and lower Minkowski dimensions.

We then turn to the map
M
and the label process
Z^{\mathrm{ex}}
.
Informally, we simply loose a factor
1/2
due to the regularity of the Brownian labels.

Corollary 4.5
.

For any
a\in(0,1/(2\boldsymbol{\eta}))
, the label process
(Z^{\mathrm{ex}}_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
has a modification that is Hölder continuous with exponent
a
.

As for looptree distances, the claim also holds for the label process associated with the unconditioned Lévy process or its bridge version.

Proof.

Recall the moment bound on the Brownian labels (
2.9
), then if the Hölder continuity (
4.3
) on the looptree distances held true with a deterministic constant
C_{a}
, then we could apply the classical Kolmogorov continuity criterion, showing that for any
a<1/\boldsymbol{\eta}
and any
q>0
, the process
(Z^{\mathrm{ex}}_{t})_{t}
has a modification that is Hölder continuous with any exponent
b<q^{-1}(aq/2-1)
, which is arbitrarily close to
1/(2\boldsymbol{\eta})
when
q
is large.
The idea then is to adapt the proof of this criterion, relying on (
4.4
) where the constant
K_{a}
is indeed deterministic.

As in the previous proof, it suffices to prove the claim in the infinite volume setting by replacing
X^{\mathrm{ex}}
by the unconditioned Lévy process
X
and constructing the associated looptree and label process
Z
.
By absolute continuity, it then extends to the bridge, and finally to the excursion by the Vervaat transform.
Fix
a<1/\boldsymbol{\eta}
and
q>0
and let
b<q^{-1}(aq/2-1)
and let
A_{n}
be the event

A_{n}=\Bigl\{\sup_{0\leq i\leq 2^{n}-1}d_{{L}(X)}(i2^{-n},(i+1)2^{-n})\leq K_{a}2^{-an}\Bigr\}

from (
4.4
), where
K_{a}
is a constant chosen such that
\sum_{n}\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(A_{n}^{c})<\infty
.
Then using a union bound, the Markov inequality for the conditional expectation given the process
X
, and the moment bound (
2.9
), we have for every
n\geq 1
:

\displaystyle\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}\Bigl(A_{n}\text{ and }\sup_{0\leq i\leq 2^{n}-1}|Z_{i2^{-n}}-Z_{(i+1)2^{-n}}|>2^{-nb}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{n}\sup_{0\leq i\leq 2^{n}-1}\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(A_{n}\text{ and }|Z_{i2^{-n}}-Z_{(i+1)2^{-n}}|^{q}>2^{-nbq})

\displaystyle\leq 2^{n}\sup_{0\leq i\leq 2^{n}-1}2^{nbq}\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{\{A_{n}\}}\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}[|Z_{i2^{-n}}-Z_{(i+1)2^{-n}}|^{q}\mid X]]

\displaystyle\leq 2^{n}\sup_{0\leq i\leq 2^{n}-1}2^{nbq}K_{q}\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{\{A_{n}\}}d_{{L}(X)}(i2^{-n},(i+1)2^{-n})^{q/2}]

\displaystyle\leq 2^{n}2^{nbq}K_{q}K_{a}^{q/2}2^{-anq/2}

\displaystyle\leq K_{q}K_{a}^{q/2}2^{n(1+bq-aq/2)},

which is a convergent series since
1+bq-aq/2<0
. Since
\sum_{n}\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(A_{n}^{c})<\infty
, then we infer that

\sum_{n\geq 1}\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\sup_{0\leq i\leq 2^{n}-1}|Z_{i2^{-n}}-Z_{(i+1)2^{-n}}|>2^{-nb}\Bigr)<\infty.

From here we can catch up with the proof of the classical Kolmogorov continuity criterion: the Borel–Cantelli lemma shows that almost surely, these events fail eventually; we then extend the bound to any pair of dyadic numbers and then to any times by density.
∎

From now on, we always deal with this Hölder continuous modification of
Z
and
Z^{\mathrm{ex}}
.
This further implies that the distances in the map are Hölder continuous.

Proposition 4.6
.

For any
a\in(0,1/(2\boldsymbol{\eta}))
, the projection
\mathbf{p}_{M}
is almost surely Hölder continuous with exponent
a
in the sense that almost surely, there exists
C_{a}\in(0,\infty)
such that:

d_{M}(s,t)\leq C_{a}\cdot|t-s|^{a}\qquad\text{for all }s,t\in[0,1].

(4.6)

As for Proposition
4.3
, we shall see that
\mathbf{p}_{M}
is almost surely not Hölder continuous with exponent larger than
1/(2\boldsymbol{\eta})
, see again Remark
4.7
.

Proof.

Define for every
0\leq s\leq t\leq 1
a pseudo-distance by:

D^{\circ}(s,t)=D^{\circ}(t,s)=Z^{\mathrm{ex}}_{s}+Z^{\mathrm{ex}}_{t}-2\inf_{[s,t]}Z^{\mathrm{ex}}.

(4.7)

Then the Hölder continuity of
Z^{\mathrm{ex}}
from Corollary
4.5
transfers to
D^{\circ}

which thus satisfies the upper bound (
4.6
).
Our claim follows then from the bound

d_{M}\leq D^{\circ}.

Indeed, a similar bound holds in the discrete setting, see e.g. Le Gall
[
LG07
, Lemma 3.1]
in a slightly different context, and it passes to the limit.
To explain it, in the construction of a discrete map from a labelled looptree, the labels on the looptree codes the distances in the map to a distinguished vertex. Precisely, the chain of successors define for every vertex a special geodesic path to the distinguished vertex.
Then if one takes two arbitrary vertices and follows these two special geodesic paths starting from each of them until they meet, then the sum of the two lengths is precisely the discrete analogue of
D^{\circ}
, and is clearly not smaller than the graph distance in the map between the two vertices.
∎

4.2.
Fractal dimensions

We are now ready to compute the dimensions of our spaces.
Recall that Theorem
1.1
about the looptrees precisely states that almost surely, we have:
