\dim_{H}{L}(X^{\mathrm{ex}})=\diminf{L}(X^{\mathrm{ex}})=\boldsymbol{\gamma}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\dim_{p}{L}(X^{\mathrm{ex}})=\dimsup{L}(X^{\mathrm{ex}})=\boldsymbol{\eta}.

For the maps, Theorem
1.2
states that the dimensions are twice these quantities.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
and Theorem
1.2
.

Let us refer to
[
Mat95
]
for the definition and basic properties of these dimensions. Let us only recall here
the following relations between these notions of dimension:

\dim_{H}\leq\diminf\qquad\text{and}\qquad\dim_{H}\leq\dim_{p}\leq\dimsup.

It thus suffices to upper bound
\diminf
and
\dimsup
and to lower bound
\dim_{H}
and
\dim_{p}
.

The upper bound for
\dimsup
is an immediate consequence of the Hölder continuity provided by Proposition
4.3
and Proposition
4.6
. Indeed, fix
\delta>0
and let
N_{n}=\lfloor 2^{n(\boldsymbol{\eta}+\delta)}\rfloor
; then by the former result, almost surely, for every
1\leq i\leq N_{n}
, the diameter of the image of
[iN_{n}^{-1},(i-1)N_{n}^{-1}]
in the looptree is small compared to
2^{-n}
. Hence, almost surely, for every
n
large enough, the space
{L}(X^{\mathrm{ex}})
can be covered by
N_{n}
balls with radius
2^{-n}
and this implies that
\dimsup{L}(X^{\mathrm{ex}})\leq\boldsymbol{\eta}+\delta
almost surely with
\delta
arbitrarily small.
The upper bound
\diminf{L}(X^{\mathrm{ex}})\leq\boldsymbol{\gamma}
is similar, using Remark
4.4
: with the sequence
(\varepsilon_{n})_{n\geq 1}
from this remark, we may apply the previous reasoning with
N_{n}=\lfloor\varepsilon_{n}^{-\boldsymbol{\gamma}-\delta}\rfloor
.
The upper bounds for the maps are proved similarly using Proposition
4.6
and the analogue of Remark
4.4
, which is derived from the latter.

The lower bounds on the Hausdorff and packing dimensions follow from our estimate on the volume of small balls.
Recall that we associate times on
[0,1]
by their projection in the looptree; let then
B(u,r)
denote the ball centred at
u\in[0,1]
and with radius
r\geq 0
for the looptree distance. Let also
|\cdot|
denote the Lebesgue measure on
[0,1]
.
Let
U
be an independent uniform random time on
[0,1]
and
\delta>0
.
We read from Proposition
3.1
that almost surely, for every
n
large enough, we have:

|B(U,2^{-n})|\leq\psi(2^{n(1-\delta)})^{-1}.

By definition of
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
, we know that
2^{-n(1-\delta)(\boldsymbol{\gamma}-\delta)}\psi(2^{n(1-\delta)})\to\infty
. Therefore, almost surely, for every
r>0
small enough, if
2^{-(n+1)}\leq r\leq 2^{-n}
, then

|B(U,r)|\leq|B(U,2^{-n})|=o(2^{-n(1-\delta)(\boldsymbol{\gamma}-\delta)})=o(r^{(1-\delta)(\boldsymbol{\gamma}-\delta)}).

On the other hand, by definition of
\boldsymbol{\eta}
, we know that
2^{-n(1-\delta)(\boldsymbol{\eta}-\delta/2)}\psi(2^{n(1-\delta)})
does not tend to
0
, so there is exists a subsequence which is bounded away from
0
and we infer similarly that for some
r_{n}\to 0
, it holds:

|B(U,\varepsilon_{n})|=o(r_{n}^{(1-\delta)(\boldsymbol{\eta}-\delta)}).

This shows that almost surely, for Lebesgue almost every
u\in[0,1]
, we have both:

\limsup_{r\downarrow 0}\frac{|B(u,r)|}{r^{(1-\delta)(\boldsymbol{\gamma}-\delta)}}=0\qquad\text{and}\qquad\liminf_{r\downarrow 0}\frac{|B(u,r)|}{r^{(1-\delta)(\boldsymbol{\eta}-\delta)}}=0.

We conclude from density theorems that
\dim_{H}{L}(X^{\mathrm{ex}})\geq\boldsymbol{\gamma}-\delta
and
\dim_{p}{L}(X^{\mathrm{ex}})\geq\boldsymbol{\eta}-\delta
, see e.g.
[
Mat95
, Theorem 6.9 and 6.11]
.
Again, this is adapted to the maps by simply replacing the estimate from Proposition
3.1
by that from Proposition
3.2
.
∎

Remark 4.7
.

Notice that the upper bounds on the Hausdorff dimensions only rely on the Hölder continuity results and extend as far as the latter do. Therefore if the canonical projection on the looptree (resp. on the map) was Hölder continuous with exponent
a>1/\boldsymbol{\eta}
(resp.
a>1/(2\boldsymbol{\eta}))
, then we would upper bound the dimension by
1/a<\boldsymbol{\eta}
(resp.
1/a<2\boldsymbol{\eta})
, and this would contradict the lower bounds.
In other words, if the bound (
4.3
) held with
a>1/\boldsymbol{\eta}
, then by taking
\delta>0
small enough, this would contradict the liminf in the last display of the previous proof.
Therefore the exponents in Proposition
4.3
and Proposition
4.6
are optimal.

5.
Approximations in the continuum world

In this section we ask ourselves the following question. Suppose that for all
\lambda>0
we are given a Lévy process
X^{(\lambda)}
with no negative jump and infinite variation paths, with exponent
\psi^{(\lambda)}
, and another one
X
with exponent
\psi
.
Suppose that
X^{(\lambda)}
and
X
satisfy the integrability condition (
1.3
), so they all have continuous transition densities
p^{(\lambda)}_{t}(x)
and
p_{t}(x)
respectively, and we can define bridges and excursions as before. If these transition densities converge, then it is well known that the Lévy processes converge in law; but do the corresponding looptrees, Gaussian labels, and continuum maps converge as well?
If the limit
X
has no Gaussian part, then it has the pure jump property from
[
Mar22b
]
and Section 4 there answers positively the question. Here we consider the general setting: we prove the convergence of labelled looptrees in Section
5.1
and that of maps (along a subsequence) in Section
5.2
.

A typical example of application we have in mind is the case where
X^{(\lambda)}
is an
\alpha
-stable spectrally positive Lévy process with drift
\lambda
, with varying
\alpha
and
\lambda
, studied in Section
6
.

Let us start as a warmup with an easy lemma.

Lemma 5.1
.

Suppose that for every
t\geq 0
, the transition density
p^{(\lambda)}_{t}
converges uniformly as
\lambda\to\infty
to
p_{t}
.
Then
X^{(\lambda)}\to X
in distribution for the Skorokhod
J_{1}
topology under the unconditional law as well as under
\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(\,\cdot\mid X^{(\lambda)}_{1}=\vartheta)
for any
\vartheta\in\mathbb{R}
, and finally under the excursion law.

Proof.

The convergence of the densities implies the convergence in distribution
X^{(\lambda)}_{t}\to X_{t}
for any
t\geq 0
fixed under the unconditional law. Since they are Lévy processes, this implies the convergence in distribution for the Skorokhod
J_{1}
topology, see e.g.
[
JS03
, Chapter VII]
for details. Next, fix
\vartheta\in\mathbb{R}
, then for every
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, recall that the bridges satisfy the absolute continuity relation:

\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}[F(X^{(\lambda)}_{t};t\in[0,1-\varepsilon])\mid X^{(\lambda)}_{1}=\vartheta]=\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}\Bigl[F(X^{(\lambda)}_{t};t\in[0,1-\varepsilon])\cdot\frac{p^{(\lambda)}_{\varepsilon}(\vartheta-X^{(\lambda)}_{1-\varepsilon})}{p^{(\lambda)}_{1}(\vartheta)}\Bigr],

for any continuous and bounded function
F
. We infer from the convergence in distribution of the unconditioned processes and the uniform convergence
p^{(\lambda)}_{\varepsilon}\to p_{\varepsilon}
that the right-hand side converges to the same quantity for
X
, hence the bridges converge on any interval
[0,1-\varepsilon]
. Notice then that the bridges are invariant under space- and time-reversal (since Lévy processes are), so the part on the interval
[1-\varepsilon,1]
converges as well, hence both are tight and the entire bridge on
[0,1]
is tight. The convergence on any interval
[0,1-\varepsilon]
then characterises uniquely the subsequential limits.
Finally, the convergence of bridges for
\vartheta=0
is transferred to the excursions by the Vervaat transform.
∎

In practice, the uniform convergence of transition densities can be often be checked by using the inverse Fourier transform. Precisely, we may write:

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}|p^{(\lambda)}_{t}(x)-p_{t}(x)|\leq\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{e}^{iuX^{(\lambda)}_{t}}]-\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{e}^{iuX_{t}}]|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u.

The right-hand side tends to
0
provided the integrand does and one can e.g. apply the dominated convergence theorem.
This will be the case in our application in Section
6
.

We assume for the rest of this section that the assumption of Lemma
5.1
is in force.

5.1.
Convergence of looptrees and labels

Let us first study the convergence of the looptrees and Gaussian labels associated with
X^{(\lambda)}
and
X
.
Recall from (
2.6
) and (
2.8
) respectively that they are defined using a parameter which acts as a multiplicative constant on the continuous part.
In the next theorem, we fix the value of this constant:
1/2
for looptrees and
1/3
for the labels.
We discuss this restriction after the statement.

Theorem 5.2
.

Suppose that for every
t\geq 0
, the transition density
p^{(\lambda)}_{t}
converges uniformly as
\lambda\to\infty
to
p_{t}
.
Under the unconditional law or the bridge or excursion law, we have jointly with the convergence in distribution
X^{(\lambda)}\to X
:

{L}^{1/2}(X^{(\lambda)})\enskip\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{(d)}_{\lambda\to\infty}\enskip{L}^{1/2}(X)\qquad\text{and}\qquad Z^{(\lambda,1/3)}\enskip\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{(d)}_{\lambda\to\infty}\enskip Z^{1/3}

for the Gromov–Hausdorff–Prokhorov topology and
for the uniform topology respectively.

Recall that the factor
1/2
in the looptree above refers to the multiplicative constant applied to the continuous part in (
2.6
) and similarly in (
2.8
) for
1/3
in the label process.
First notice that in absence of jumps in the limit, namely if
X
is a scaled Brownian excursion, then for any
c>0
, the looptree
{L}^{c}(X)=c\,{L}^{1}(X)
is a scaled Brownian tree and
Z^{c}=\sqrt{c}\,Z
is a scaled Brownian snake so the choice of
c
amounts to fix a normalisation.
In the opposite case, if this continuous part in the limit vanishes, then the choice of this constant is irrelevant. Similarly, and this will be the case in our application, if the continuous part in
X^{(\lambda)}
vanishes, then this constant is irrelevant in the sequence.
The reason for this restriction is the following. The continuous part for
X
appears as the limit of the continuous part of
X^{(\lambda)}
together with sum of quantities of the form
R^{t}_{s}
as defined in (
2.3
), corresponding to small jumps of
X^{(\lambda)}
. Now typically, the contribution to the looptree distances of these small jumps will be half of the sum of these
R^{t}_{s}
’s by symmetry, since the latter code the right-length of the cycles. Hence in order to control the looptrees with a different multiplicative constant than
1/2
, we must understand the precise contribution of the continuous part of
X^{(\lambda)}
and its small jumps to the continuous part of
X
, whereas with the constant
1/2
, in the looptree, they both contribute equally.

Let us first provide a tightness argument. We note that the latter does not require to restrict to the parameters
1/2
and
1/3
.

Proof of tightness.

Thanks to the upper bound (
2.7
) on looptree distances, the convergence of the excursions implies tightness of the associated looptrees.
Indeed, for any
s,s^{\prime},t,t^{\prime}\in[0,1]
we have by the triangle inequality:

\displaystyle|d^{1/2}_{{L}(X^{(\lambda)})}(s,t)-d^{1/2}_{{L}(X^{(\lambda)})}(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\leq d^{1/2}_{{L}(X^{(\lambda)})}(s,s^{\prime})+d^{1/2}_{{L}(X^{(\lambda)})}(t,t^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq d_{X^{(\lambda)}}(s,s^{\prime})+d_{X^{(\lambda)}}(t,t^{\prime}).

The last line converges in distribution to
d_{X}(s,s^{\prime})+d_{X}(t,t^{\prime})
under the unconditional law or the bridge or excursion law by Lemma
5.1
. This limit in turn is arbitrarily small when the pairs
(s,t)
and
(s^{\prime},t^{\prime})
are arbitrarily close to each other (for the Euclidean distance). Tightness of the looptree distances follows then easily from this control on the modulus of continuity.

Tightness of the label process is less immediate. Let us prove it providing that
d^{1/2}_{{L}(X^{(\lambda)})}\to d^{1/2}_{{L}(X)}
in distribution, which we shall prove below.
Our aim is again to control the modulus of continuity. Precisely, let us fix
\varepsilon,\theta>0
and let us prove that there exists
\delta>0
such that for every
\lambda
large enough, we have:

\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(\exists(s,t)\in[0,1]^{2}\colon|t-s|\leq\delta\quad\text{and}\quad|Z^{(\lambda,1/3)}_{s}-Z^{(\lambda,1/3)}_{t}|>\theta)\leq 10\varepsilon.

Let
C_{a}
be the random variable as in (
4.3
) for the limit process
X
, for some fixed admissible
a>0
.
Since
C_{a}<\infty
almost surely then there exists a deterministic constant
M\in(0,\infty)
such that
C_{a}\leq M
with probability at least
1-\varepsilon
.
Let
C>0
which will be chosen large enough, depending only on
\theta
and
M
(and thus
\varepsilon
), and let us set
\kappa=(\theta/C)^{2}
and then

N=\lceil(M/\kappa)^{1/a}\rceil=\lceil(MC^{2}/\theta^{2})^{1/a}\rceil
; we shall take
\delta=1/N
.
Consider the event:

G(N,\kappa,\lambda)=\{\forall(s,t)\in[0,1]^{2}\enskip\text{such that}\enskip|t-s|\leq 1/N\enskip\text{it holds}\enskip d^{1/2}_{{L}(X^{(\lambda)})}(s,t)\leq\kappa\},

and let us write
G(N,\kappa)
for the same event for the process
X
.
It follows from the convergence of looptree distances, the bound (
4.3
), and our choice of constants that

\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(G(N,\kappa,\lambda))\enskip\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{\lambda\to\infty}\enskip\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(G(N,\kappa))\geq 1-\varepsilon.

We shall work on the event
G(N,\kappa,\lambda)
and upper bound

\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(\{\exists(s,t)\in[0,1]^{2}\colon|t-s|\leq 1/N\quad\text{and}\quad|Z^{(\lambda,1/3)}_{s}-Z^{(\lambda,1/3)}_{t}|>C\sqrt{\kappa}\}\cap G(N,\kappa,\lambda)).

By the triangle inequality and a union bound, this probability is less than or equal to

N\max_{0\leq i<N}\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(\{\exists s\in[0,1/N]\colon|Z^{(\lambda,1/3)}_{s+i/N}-Z^{(\lambda,1/3)}_{i/N}|>C\sqrt{\kappa}/4\}\cap G(N,\kappa,\lambda)).

Recall that conditional on
X^{(\lambda)}
, the label process
Z^{(\lambda,1/3)}
is a centred Gaussian process with a so-called canonical or intrinsic pseudo-distance given by:

\rho^{(\lambda)}(s,t)=\sqrt{\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}[(Z^{(\lambda,1/3)}_{t}-Z^{(\lambda,1/3)}_{s})^{2}\mid X^{(\lambda)}]}\leq\sqrt{d^{1/2}_{{L}(X^{(\lambda)})}(s,t)},

where the upper bound follows by replacing the product of left and right length along cycles by the minimum.
Let then
N^{(\lambda)}(r,N,i)
denote the number of
\rho^{(\lambda)}
-balls of radius
r
needed to cover the interval
[i/N,(i+1)/N]
. Then on the event
G(N,\kappa,\lambda)
we have
N^{(\lambda)}(r,N,i)=1
for
r\geq\sqrt{\kappa}
and
N^{(\lambda)}(r,N,i)\leq\lceil\sqrt{\kappa}/r\rceil
for
r<\sqrt{\kappa}
.
Then Dudley’s entropy bound
[
BLM13
, Corollary 13.2]
shows that there exists a universal constant
K
such that

\displaystyle\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\sup_{s\in[0,1/N]}|Z^{(\lambda,1/3)}_{s+i/N}-Z^{(\lambda,1/3)}_{i/N}|\Bigm|X^{(\lambda)}\Bigr]\,\mathbf{1}_{\{G(N,\kappa,\lambda)\}}

\displaystyle\leq K\int_{0}^{\infty}\sqrt{\log N^{(\lambda)}(r,N,i)}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\,\mathbf{1}_{\{G(N,\kappa,\lambda)\}}

\displaystyle\leq K\int_{0}^{\sqrt{\kappa}}\sqrt{\log\lceil\sqrt{\kappa}/r\rceil}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\leq K\sqrt{\kappa}\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{\log\lceil 1/x\rceil}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,

and the integral is some universal constant as well. Let us write
L\sqrt{\kappa}
the last line. Recall the exponent
a<1/\boldsymbol{\eta}
which we have fixed. If we were able to take
a>1/2
then we could conclude by the Markov inequality. However in the case
\boldsymbol{\eta}=2
, in particular in the Brownian case, we need a slightly better bound. A very strong bound
[
BLM13
, Theorem 5.8]
which follows from standard Gaussian concentration results is the following: for every
u>0
, we have

\displaystyle\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}\Bigl(G(N,\kappa,\lambda)\cap\Bigl\{\sup_{s\in[0,1/N]}|Z^{(\lambda,1/3)}_{s+i/N}-Z^{(\lambda,1/3)}_{i/N}|>u+\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\sup_{s\in[0,1/N]}|Z^{(\lambda,1/3)}_{s+i/N}-Z^{(\lambda,1/3)}_{i/N}|\Bigm|X^{(\lambda)}\Bigr]\Bigr\}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\mathbf{1}_{\{G(N,\kappa,\lambda)\}}\exp\Bigl(-\frac{u^{2}}{2\sup_{s\in[0,1/N]}\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}[(Z^{(\lambda,1/3)}_{s+i/N}-Z^{(\lambda,1/3)}_{i/N})^{2}]}\Bigr)\Bigr]

\displaystyle\leq\exp\Bigl(-\frac{u^{2}}{2\kappa}\Bigr).

Now recall that the expectation in the first line is bounded above by
L\sqrt{\kappa}
where
L
is a universal constant, hence

\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}\Bigl(G(N,\kappa,\lambda)\cap\Bigl\{\sup_{s\in[0,1/N]}|Z^{(\lambda,1/3)}_{s+i/N}-Z^{(\lambda,1/3)}_{i/N}|>u+L\sqrt{\kappa}\Bigr\}\Bigr)\leq\exp\Bigl(-\frac{u^{2}}{2\kappa}\Bigr),

for every
u>0
. Combining all the previous displays, we infer with
u=(C/4-L)\sqrt{\kappa}
that for every
\lambda
large enough, it holds:

\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(\exists(s,t)\in[0,1]^{2}\colon|t-s|\leq 1/N\quad\text{and}\quad|Z^{(\lambda,1/3)}_{s}-Z^{(\lambda,1/3)}_{t}|>\theta)\leq 2\varepsilon+N\exp\Bigl(-\frac{(C/4-L)^{2}}{2}\Bigr).

Recall that
\theta=C\sqrt{\kappa}
and
N=\lceil(M/\kappa)^{1/a}\rceil=\lceil(MC^{2}/\theta^{2})^{1/a}\rceil
where
M
was chosen only in terms of
\varepsilon
in order to get the upper bound
2\varepsilon
. Since
L
is a universal constant, then we can choose
C
depending only on
\theta
and
M
(and thus
\varepsilon
) such that the right-hand side above is less than
3\varepsilon
. This fixes the value of
N
in terms of
\theta
and
\varepsilon
only and the proof of tightness is complete.
∎

To complete the proof of Theorem
5.2
, it remains to characterise the subsequential limits. We first consider the convergence under the unconditional law of Lévy processes.

Proof of uniqueness of the limits for the unconditioned processes.

Let us here consider the unconditioned Lévy processes and let us first prove that for any
t>0
fixed, the looptree distance from
0
to
t
for
X^{(\lambda)}
converges in distribution to that for
X
. Then we discuss the convergence of the distance between any
s
and
t
fixed, the argument for more general finite dimensional distributions is the same.
We then briefly mention the modifications to control the labels.

Step 1: distance from
0
to
t
.

Fix
t>0
. Let us denote by
C^{(\lambda)}_{t}
and
R^{(\lambda),t}_{s}
respectively the continuous part and jump part that appear in the looptree distance (
2.6
) associated with
X^{(\lambda)}
.
By standard properties of the Skorokhod
J_{1}
topology, the convergence
X^{(\lambda)}\to X
implies that for any
\delta>0
, the jumps larger than
\delta
of
X
are the limits of large jumps of
X^{(\lambda)}
, and quantities such that
R^{(\lambda),t}_{s}
for such jump times
s
converge as well to their analogue for
X
.
Hence, the contribution of the times of jumps larger than
\delta
in the looptree distance associated with
X^{(\lambda)}
converges to that for
X
. It remains to consider the joint contribution of the continuous part and the small jumps, namely to prove that:

\frac{1}{2}C^{(\lambda)}_{t}+\sum_{s\prec t}\min(R^{(\lambda),t}_{s},\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}-R^{(\lambda),t}_{s})\mathbf{1}_{\{\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}\leq\delta\}}\enskip\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{(d)}\enskip\frac{1}{2}C_{t}

(5.1)

as first
\lambda\to\infty
and then
\delta\to 0
.

First note that the convergence in the Skorokhod
J_{1}
topology also implies that
X^{(\lambda)}_{t-}
converges to
X_{t-}
, hence as a consequence of the previous lines, we have for any
\delta>0
fixed:

C^{(\lambda)}_{t}+\sum_{s\prec t}R^{(\lambda),t}_{s}\mathbf{1}_{\{\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}\leq\delta\}}=X^{(\lambda)}_{t-}-\sum_{s\prec t}R^{(\lambda),t}_{s}\mathbf{1}_{\{\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}>\delta\}}\enskip\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{(d)}_{\lambda\to\infty}\enskip X_{t-}-\sum_{s\prec t}R^{t}_{s}\mathbf{1}_{\{\Delta X_{s}>\delta\}}=C_{t}+\sum_{s\prec t}R^{t}_{s}\mathbf{1}_{\{\Delta X_{s}\leq\delta\}},

and the right-hand side further converges to
C_{t}
as
\delta\to 0
.
We shall compare the left-hand side of this display with (twice) that of (
5.1
).
First note that as a consequence of this last convergence, if for
\varepsilon>0
we let

A_{\lambda,\delta,\varepsilon}=\Bigl\{\Bigl|C^{(\lambda)}_{t}+\sum_{s\prec t}R^{(\lambda),t}_{s}\mathbf{1}_{\{\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}\leq\delta\}}\Bigr|<\varepsilon^{-1/2}\Bigr\},

then the probability of this event goes to
1
as first
\lambda\to\infty
, then
\delta\to 0
, and finally
\varepsilon\to 0
.
Unless specified, we shall below consider limits in this sense.

Let us introduce a second event:

B_{\lambda,\delta,\varepsilon}=\Bigl\{\sum_{s\prec t}\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}\mathbf{1}_{\{\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}\leq\delta\}}\leq\varepsilon\Bigr\}.

Recall that
\min(R^{(\lambda),t}_{s},\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}-R^{(\lambda),t}_{s})\leq R^{(\lambda),t}_{s}\leq\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}
.
Fix a bounded and
K
-Lipschitz function
F
for some
K>0
, then we have:

\displaystyle\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\Bigl|F\Bigl(\frac{1}{2}C^{(\lambda)}_{t}+\sum_{s\prec t}\min(R^{(\lambda),t}_{s},\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}-R^{(\lambda),t}_{s})\mathbf{1}_{\{\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}\leq\delta\}}\Bigr)-F\Bigl(\frac{1}{2}\Bigl(C^{(\lambda)}_{t}+\sum_{s\prec t}R^{(\lambda),t}_{s}\mathbf{1}_{\{\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}\leq\delta\}}\Bigr)\Bigr)\Bigr|\mathbf{1}_{\{B_{\lambda,\delta,\varepsilon}\}}\Bigr]

\displaystyle\leq\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}\Bigl[K\Bigl(\sum_{s\prec t}\min(R^{(\lambda),t}_{s},\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}-R^{(\lambda),t}_{s})\mathbf{1}_{\{\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}\leq\delta\}}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{s\prec t}R^{(\lambda),t}_{s}\mathbf{1}_{\{\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}\leq\delta\}}\Bigr)\mathbf{1}_{\{B_{\lambda,\delta,\varepsilon}\}}\Bigr]

\displaystyle\leq K\cdot\frac{3\varepsilon}{2}.

On the complementary event
B_{\lambda,\delta,\varepsilon}^{c}
, we may adapt the argument in the proof of Theorem 1.2 in
[
CK14
]
.
Precisely, we can write
R^{(\lambda),t}_{s}=\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}\times u^{t}_{s}
, where
\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}
is the size of the jump of
X^{(\lambda)}
at time
s
, and in the unconditioned process, the random variables
(u^{t}_{s};s\prec t)
are i.i.d. with the uniform law on
[0,1]
and are independent from
(\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s};s\prec t)
. Consequently,

\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}\biggl[\frac{\sum_{s\prec t}\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}\mathbf{1}_{\{\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}\leq\delta\}}u^{t}_{s}}{\sum_{s\prec t}\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}\mathbf{1}_{\{\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}\leq\delta\}}}\bigm|\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s};s\prec t\biggr]=\frac{1}{2}

and

\mathrm{Var}\biggl(\frac{\sum_{s\prec t}\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}\mathbf{1}_{\{\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}\leq\delta\}}u^{t}_{s}}{\sum_{s\prec t}\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}\mathbf{1}_{\{\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}\leq\delta\}}}\bigm|\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s};s\prec t\biggr)=\frac{\sum_{s\prec t}(\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s})^{2}\mathbf{1}_{\{\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}\leq\delta\}}}{12(\sum_{s\prec t}\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}\mathbf{1}_{\{\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}\leq\delta\}})^{2}}\leq\frac{\sup_{s\prec t}\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}\mathbf{1}_{\{\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}\leq\delta\}}}{12\sum_{s\prec t}\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}\mathbf{1}_{\{\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}\leq\delta\}}},

which
is bounded by
\delta/(12\varepsilon)

on the event
B_{\lambda,\delta,\varepsilon}^{c}
. The same holds if we replace
u^{t}_{s}
by
\min(u^{t}_{s},1-u^{t}_{s})
, in which case the numerator on left represents the contribution to the looptree distance of the small jumps, the only difference is that the expectation is now
1/4
.
We infer that if we let

C_{\lambda,\delta,\varepsilon}=\biggl\{\frac{\frac{1}{2}C^{(\lambda)}_{t}+\sum_{s\prec t}\min(R^{(\lambda),t}_{s},\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}-R^{(\lambda),t}_{s})\mathbf{1}_{\{\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}\leq\delta\}}}{C^{(\lambda)}_{t}+\sum_{s\prec t}R^{(\lambda),t}_{s}\mathbf{1}_{\{\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}\leq\delta\}}}\in\Bigl[\frac{1/4-\varepsilon}{1/2+\varepsilon},\frac{1/4+\varepsilon}{1/2-\varepsilon}\Bigr]\biggr\},

then

\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(B_{\lambda,\delta,\varepsilon}^{c}\cap C_{\lambda,\delta,\varepsilon}^{c})\leq\frac{\delta}{L\,\varepsilon^{3}},

for some universal constant
L
coming from the variances.
Let
g(\varepsilon)=\max(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1/4-\varepsilon}{1/2+\varepsilon},\frac{1/4+\varepsilon}{1/2-\varepsilon}-\frac{1}{2})
.
Recall the event
A_{\lambda,\delta,\varepsilon}=\{|C^{(\lambda)}_{t}+\sum_{s\prec t}R^{(\lambda),t}_{s}\mathbf{1}_{\{\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}\leq\delta\}}|<\varepsilon^{-1/2}\}
and recall that
F
is
K
-Lipschitz.
Then we have:

\displaystyle\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\Bigl|F\Bigl(\frac{1}{2}C^{(\lambda)}_{t}+\sum_{s\prec t}\min(R^{(\lambda),t}_{s},\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}-R^{(\lambda),t}_{s})\mathbf{1}_{\{\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}\leq\delta\}}\Bigr)-F\Bigl(\frac{1}{2}\Bigl(C^{(\lambda)}_{t}+\sum_{s\prec t}R^{(\lambda),t}_{s}\mathbf{1}_{\{\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}\leq\delta\}}\Bigr)\Bigr)\Bigr|\mathbf{1}_{\{A_{\lambda,\delta,\varepsilon}\cap C_{\lambda,\delta,\varepsilon}\}}\Bigr]

\displaystyle\leq\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}\Bigl[K\cdot\Bigl|\frac{1}{2}C^{(\lambda)}_{t}+\sum_{s\prec t}\min(R^{(\lambda),t}_{s},\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}-R^{(\lambda),t}_{s})\mathbf{1}_{\{\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}\leq\delta\}}-\frac{1}{2}\Bigl(C^{(\lambda)}_{t}+\sum_{s\prec t}R^{(\lambda),t}_{s}\mathbf{1}_{\{\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}\leq\delta\}}\Bigr)\Bigr|\mathbf{1}_{\{A_{\lambda,\delta,\varepsilon}\cap C_{\lambda,\delta,\varepsilon}\}}\Bigr]

\displaystyle\leq\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}\Bigl[K\cdot g(\varepsilon)\cdot\Bigl|C^{(\lambda)}_{t}+\sum_{s\prec t}R^{(\lambda),t}_{s}\mathbf{1}_{\{\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}\leq\delta\}}\Bigr|\mathbf{1}_{\{A_{\lambda,\delta,\varepsilon}\cap C_{\lambda,\delta,\varepsilon}\}}\Bigr]

\displaystyle\leq K\cdot g(\varepsilon)/\sqrt{\varepsilon},

which converges to
0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
since
g
is approximately linear near
0
.

Recall that
F
is
K
-Lipschitz and bounded, say
|F|\leq K^{\prime}
. Then by partitioning the space into the four events:

B_{\lambda,\delta,\varepsilon}\cup\{A_{\lambda,\delta,\varepsilon}^{c}\cap B_{\lambda,\delta,\varepsilon}^{c}\}\cup\{A_{\lambda,\delta,\varepsilon}\cap B_{\lambda,\delta,\varepsilon}^{c}\cap C_{\lambda,\delta,\varepsilon}\}\cup\{A_{\lambda,\delta,\varepsilon}\cap B_{\lambda,\delta,\varepsilon}^{c}\cap C_{\lambda,\delta,\varepsilon}^{c}\}
,
we conclude that

\Bigl|\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}\Bigl[F\Bigl(\frac{1}{2}C^{(\lambda)}_{t}+\sum_{s\prec t}\min(R^{(\lambda),t}_{s},\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}-R^{(\lambda),t}_{s})\mathbf{1}_{\{\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}\leq\delta\}}\Bigr)\Bigr]-\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}\Bigl[F\Bigl(\frac{1}{2}\Bigl(C^{(\lambda)}_{t}+\sum_{s\prec t}R^{(\lambda),t}_{s}\mathbf{1}_{\{\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}\leq\delta\}}\Bigr)\Bigr)\Bigr]\Bigr|

is upper bounded by:

K\frac{3\varepsilon}{2}+2K^{\prime}\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(A_{\lambda,\delta,\varepsilon}^{c})+K\frac{g(\varepsilon)}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}+2K^{\prime}\frac{\delta}{L\,\varepsilon^{3}},

which goes to
0
as first
\lambda\to\infty
, then
\delta\to 0
, and finally
\varepsilon\to 0
.
Recall from the display below (
5.1
) that the second expectation above tends to
\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}[F(C_{t})]
along these limits, hence so does the first one. This proves (
5.1
) by the Portmanteau theorem.

Step 2: distance from an ancestor to
t
.

So far, we have proved the convergence of the looptree distance from
0
to any given
t>0
. Let
s\in(0,t)
and suppose first that
s\prec t
in the looptree coded by
X
, namely that
X_{s-}\leq X_{r}
for all
r\in[s,t]
.
There are two cases according as wether
X
jumps at time
s
or not.

If
\Delta X_{s}>0
, then recall from the proof of Lemma
3.3
that
X
cannot make a local minimum at the height
X_{s-}
. In particular, either
X_{t}>X_{s-}
or
t=\inf\{r>s\colon X_{r}=X_{s-}\}
. In the second case, this means that we can find
t^{\prime}<t
arbitrarily close to
t
, with
X_{t^{\prime}}>X_{t}=X_{s-}
and
X_{t^{\prime}}
is arbitrarily close to
X_{t}
; this implies that the looptree distance between
t
and
t^{\prime}
is arbitrarily small and
t^{\prime}
falls into the first case. Therefore we may assume when
\Delta X_{s}>0
that
X_{t}>X_{s-}
and actually
X_{r}>X_{s-}
for all
r\in[s,t]
.
In this case, as before, there are times
s^{(\lambda)}\prec t^{(\lambda)}
for
X^{(\lambda)}
, such that
X^{(\lambda)}
jumps at time
s^{(\lambda)}
, and such that
s
,
t
,
\Delta X_{s}
and
R^{t}_{s}
are the limits of the analogous quantities in
X^{(\lambda)}
and the previous argument extends readily to prove the convergence of the looptree distance from
s^{(\lambda)}
to
t^{(\lambda)}
for
X^{(\lambda)}
to that from
s
to
t
for
X
.

Suppose next that
\Delta X_{s}=0
. We aim at finding similarly times
s^{(\lambda)}\prec t^{(\lambda)}
that converge to
s
and
t
to which we can apply the previous argument.
Again there are two cases.
In the case where
X_{r}>X_{s}
for all
r\in(s,t]
, there exists
\varepsilon>0
small such that
X_{r}>X_{s}+2\varepsilon
for
r\in(s+2\varepsilon,t]
. The Skorokhod convergence implies that for all
\lambda
sufficiently large, there exists
r^{(\lambda)}\in(s-\varepsilon,s+\varepsilon)
such that
X^{(\lambda)}_{r}>X^{(\lambda)}_{r^{(\lambda)}}+\varepsilon
for all
r\in(r^{(\lambda)}+4\varepsilon,t]
. This further implies this existence of some time
s^{(\lambda)}\in(r^{(\lambda)},r^{(\lambda)}+4\varepsilon)\subset(s-\varepsilon,s+5\varepsilon)
with
s^{(\lambda)}\prec t
and we conclude in this case.
A last case is when both
\Delta X_{s}=0
and
X_{r}=X_{s}
for some
r\in(s,t)
. Then necessarily this time
r
is a time of local minimum and since the latter are unique, then
s
is not a time of local minimum so we can find
s^{\prime}<s
arbitrarily close to
s
which falls into the previous case.

Step 3: pairwise distances.

Assume now that
s\not\prec t
in the looptree coded by
X
. Then we can cut at their last common ancestor
s\wedge t
and apply the same reasoning as above: if this last common ancestor is a jump time, then the quantities of interest associated with this macroscopic loop are limits of those for
X^{(\lambda)}
and the two branches from
s\wedge t
to
s
on the one hand and from
s\wedge t
to
t
on the other hand converge by the previous paragraph.
The case when
s\wedge t
is not a jump time is controlled similarly.
The same argument extends to control the pairwise distances between any finite collection of times, cutting at the corresponding branch-points.
Details are left to the reader.

Step 4: labels.

Let us end with a few words on the Gaussian labels on the looptree. Recall that the conditional covariance function given the Lévy process is very close to be the looptree distance: one just replaces the infimum between the left and right length of the loops by their product. Thus the only modification in Step 2 is that one should replace
\min(R^{(\lambda),t}_{s},\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}-R^{(\lambda),t}_{s})
by
R^{(\lambda),t}_{s}(\Delta X^{(\lambda)}_{s}-R^{(\lambda),t}_{s})
. If
U
has the uniform law on
[0,1]
, we used previously that
\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}[\min(U,1-U)]=1/4
, then this value is now replaced by
\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}[U(1-U)]=1/6
. Once divided by
\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}[U]=1/2
, we obtain the constant
1/3
, which explains why we consider
Z^{1/3}
where we considered
{L}^{1/2}(X)
.
Step 2 and 3 are easily adapted.
∎

We finally turn to conditioned processes.

Proof of uniqueness of the limits for bridges and excursions.

From the two previous proofs, we know that the looptree distances and labels associated with the bridges are tight and those associated with the unconditioned processes converge. Fix
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and notice that the looptree distance between all pairs
s,t\in[0,1-\varepsilon]
is a measurable function of the trajectory of the process restricted to the time interval
[0,1-\varepsilon]
. The same holds for the covariance function of the labels on the time interval
[0,1-\varepsilon]
.
Then the convergence of these looptree distances and labels for the bridge follows from the absolute continuity relation as in the proof of Lemma
5.1
. This characterises the subsequential limits and we conclude from tightness.

We next turn to the excursion versions. Recall that we recover the bridge
X^{\mathrm{br}}
by cyclicly shifting the excursion
X^{\mathrm{ex}}
at an independent uniform random time
U
.
Notice that the looptree distance in the excursion between any two times
s,t\in[0,U]
is the same as the looptree distance in the bridge between their images
1-U+s
and
1-U+t
after the cyclic shift. However they differ if one or both times lie after
U
.
Using the same time
U
to relate the excursion and bridge versions of each
X^{(\lambda)}
, we infer from the case of bridges that the looptree distances restricted to
[0,U]
in the excursions converge in distribution to those in
X^{\mathrm{ex}}
.

Assume next that
s<U<t
and consider
q=\inf\{r>U\colon X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{r-}\leq\inf_{[r,t]}X^{\mathrm{ex}}\}
the first ancestor of
t
that lies after
U
and
p=\sup\{r<U\colon X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{r-}\leq X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{q-}\}
the last ancestor of
t
before
U
. Then the looptree distance between
t
and
q
in the excursion is the same as that of their images
t-U
and
q-U
in the bridge, and all the other contributions to the distance between
s
and
t
depend only on the trajectory up to time
U
as well as the value
X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{U}-X^{\mathrm{ex}}_{q-}
.
Finally if both
s,t>U
, then it may be that their last common ancestor also satisfies
s\wedge t\geq U
, in which case the looptree distance in the excursion is that of their images in the bridge. If
s\wedge t\geq U
, then we easily adapt the previous reasoning.
In any case we infer the convergence in distribution of the looptree distances in the excursion from the joint convergence of the bridges and the associated looptree distances.

The same reasoning shows the convergence of the covariance function of the label processes, which combined with the tightness argument implies the convergence in distribution.
∎

5.2.
Convergence of maps

Let us suppose that for every
\lambda>0
, the pair
(X^{(\lambda)},Z^{(\lambda,1/3)})
arises as the scaling limit of discrete paths
(W^{(\lambda,n)},Z^{(\lambda,n)})
in the sense of Theorem
2.1
. Suppose that these paths encode a bipartite planar map
M^{(\lambda,n)}=(M^{(\lambda,n)},d^{(\lambda,n)},p^{(\lambda,n)})
. Then Theorem
2.4
implies the convergence of the rescaled map along a subsequence to a limit space
M^{(\lambda)}=(M^{(\lambda)},d^{(\lambda)},p^{(\lambda)})
.
Now assume that for every
t\geq 0
, the transition density
p^{(\lambda)}_{t}
converges uniformly as
\lambda\to\infty
to
p_{t}
, so both Lemma
5.1
and Theorem
5.2
are satisfied, and let us study the convergence of
M^{(\lambda)}
as
\lambda\to\infty
, again along a subsequence.

Theorem 5.3
.

Suppose that the previous assumptions are satisfied.
Then from every sequence tending to infinity, one can extract a subsequence along which the convergence in distribution

d^{(\lambda)}\enskip\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{(d)}_{\lambda\to\infty}\enskip D,

holds for the uniform topology on
[0,1]^{2}
, jointly with Theorem
5.2
.
The limit
D
is a random continuous pseudo-distance which satisfies:

D(U,\cdot)\enskip\mathop{=}^{(d)}\enskip Z^{1/3}-\min Z^{1/3},

where
U
is sampled uniformly at random on
[0,1]
and independently of the rest.
Furthermore, along this subsequence, we have

M^{(\lambda)}\enskip\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{(d)}_{n\to\infty}\enskip{}M=[0,1]/\{D=0\}

in the Gromov–Hausdorff–Prokhorov topology.
Finally, if the excursion of
X
reduces to a constant
\beta
times the Brownian excursion, then these subsequential limits agree with
\sqrt{\beta/3}
times the standard Brownian sphere.

Proof.

The argument is close to that of Theorem
2.4
.
Indeed, from this theorem we have the identity in law
d^{(\lambda)}(U,\cdot)=Z^{(\lambda,1/3)}-\min Z^{(\lambda,1/3)}
where
U
is sampled uniformly at random on
[0,1]
and independently of the rest. Since
Z^{(\lambda,1/3)}
converges in distribution to
Z^{1/3}
by Theorem
5.2
, then tightness of
d^{(\lambda)}
follows from the triangle inequality as in the discrete setting and so does the identity in law
D(U,\cdot)=Z^{1/3}-\min Z^{1/3}
for any subsequential limit
D
, and the convergence in distribution
d^{(\lambda)}\to D
holds jointly with that of
(X^{(\lambda)},Z^{(\lambda,1/3)})\to(X,Z^{1/3})
under the excursion law.

Suppose now that the limit excursion
X
equals
\beta
times the standard Brownian excursion, so
Z^{1/3}
has the law of
\sqrt{\beta/3}
times the standard Brownian snake driven by the standard Brownian excursion. We know from
[
LG13
,
Mie13
]
that the distance
D^{\ast}
in the Brownian sphere satisfies the identity in law:
Z^{1/3}-\inf Z^{1/3}=\sqrt{\beta/3}\cdot D^{\ast}(U,\cdot)
. We thus have the identity in law:

D(U,V)\enskip\mathop{=}^{(d)}\enskip\sqrt{\beta/3}\cdot D^{\ast}(U,V)

for every subsequential limit
D
where
U
and
V
are sampled uniformly at random on
[0,1]
and independently of the rest and of each other.
Following Le Gall’s trick from
[
LG13
]
, we claim that this identity holds almost surely. If this holds true, then we can sample countably many uniform random variables on
[0,1]
, say
(U_{i})_{i\geq 1}
and infer that almost surely
D(U_{i},U_{j})=\sqrt{\beta/3}\cdot D^{\ast}(U_{i},U_{j})
for every
i,j\geq 1
. By continuity, this implies that
D=D^{\ast}
almost surely.

Let us recall from
[
LG13
,
Mie13
]
that
D^{\ast}
is a pseudo-distance on
[0,1]
constructed from another one
D^{\circ}
defined in (
4.7
).
As opposed to
D^{\circ}
, the pseudo-distance
D^{\ast}
can be seen as a pseudo-distance on the Brownian tree, in the sense of Lemma
3.8
. In fact, it is the largest such pseudo-distance bounded above by
D^{\circ}
.
Now by this lemma, the pseudo-distance
d^{(\lambda)}
can also be seen as a pseudo-distance on the looptree
{L}(X^{(\lambda)})
. Then the same argument used to prove this lemma, using the approximation
X^{(\lambda)}\to X
instead of discrete paths, shows further that the subsequential limit
D
can be seen as a pseudo-distance on the looptree
{L}(X)
, which reduces to a scaled Brownian tree.
Moreover, using the discrete approximation, for every
\lambda>0
, the pseudo-distance
d^{(\lambda)}
is upper bounded by the same function as
D^{\circ}
but constructed from
Z^{(\lambda,1/3)}
. Letting
\lambda\to\infty
along our subsequence, we infer that
D
is upper bounded by the same function as
D^{\circ}
but constructed from
Z^{1/3}
, which we recall is
\sqrt{\beta/3}
times the standard Brownian snake.
We infer that
D\leq\sqrt{\beta/3}D^{\ast}
almost surely by the maximality property of the latter.
This allows to extend the equality in law
D(U,V)=\sqrt{\beta/3}\cdot D^{\ast}(U,V)
to an almost sure one.
∎

6.
Looptrees and maps driven by stable excursions with a drift

Let us present in this section applications of our general results in the case where the coding process is a spectrally positive stable Lévy process with a drift, defined just a few lines below.
In Section
6.1
we first motivate this model by recalling how such objects appear as limits of multiconditioned discrete models introduced in
[
KM23
]
.
We state our main result in Section
6.2
, which is a general version of Theorem
1.3
in the introduction where both the self-similarity index and the drift may vary.
The convergence in the Brownian regime is then proved in Section
6.4
, relying on an exponential change of law briefly discussed in Section
6.3
and on the results from Section
5
.
The condensation regime is treated in Section
6.6
, relying on some technical estimates on stable densities stated in Section
6.5
and proved in Section
6.8
.
In Section
6.7
we also briefly discuss a missing case in Theorem
6.2
.

For every
\alpha\in(1,2)
, let us denote by
X^{\alpha}
the stable Lévy process characterised by the Laplace exponent
\psi(\lambda)=\lambda^{\alpha}
, or equivalently by the characteristic function:

\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}[\exp(iuX^{\alpha}_{t})]=\exp\Bigl(t|u|^{\alpha}\Bigl(\cos\Bigl(\frac{\pi\alpha}{2}\Bigr)-i\sigma(u)\sin\Bigl(\frac{\pi\alpha}{2}\Bigr)\Bigr)\Bigr),

(6.1)

for every
u\in\mathbb{R}
and
t>0
, where we used the notation
\sigma(u)=\mathbf{1}_{\{u>0\}}-\mathbf{1}_{\{u<0\}}
for the sign function to distinguish it from the sine function.
The right-hand side satisfies the integrability condition (
1.3
), and thus it admits positive, bi-continuous, transition densities
p^{\alpha}_{t}
given by inverse Fourier transform, from which one can define the conditional law
\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(\,\cdot\mid X^{\alpha}_{1}=\vartheta)
for any
\vartheta\in\mathbb{R}
, characterised by (
2.1
). For any
\vartheta\in\mathbb{R}
, let us also denote by
X^{\alpha,\vartheta}_{t}=X^{\alpha}_{t}-\vartheta t
the stable process with drift
-\vartheta
, which has transition densities
p^{\alpha}_{t}(\cdot+\vartheta t)
.
One can also define bridges of this process and we let
X^{\alpha,\vartheta,\mathrm{br}}
denote the version of
X^{\alpha,\vartheta}
under the conditional law
\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(\,\cdot\mid X^{\alpha,\vartheta}_{1}=0)
.
A straightforward consequence of the absolute continuity relation (
2.1
) is that
X^{\alpha,\vartheta,\mathrm{br}}
has the same law as
(X^{\alpha}-\vartheta t)_{t\in[0,1]}
under
\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(\,\cdot\mid X^{\alpha}_{1}=\vartheta)
. We shall rather use the latter representation of
X^{\alpha,\vartheta,\mathrm{br}}
.
Finally let
X^{\alpha,\vartheta,\mathrm{ex}}
denote the excursion obtained by applying the Vervaat transform (
2.2
) to
X^{\alpha,\vartheta,\mathrm{br}}
.

6.1.
Limits of biconditioned planar maps

Recall from Section
2.1
the definition of Boltzmann random planar maps sampled proportionally to
\prod_{f\text{ face}}q_{\deg(f)/2}
for some weight sequence
(q_{k})_{k\geq 1}
.
From the bijections also recalled in this section, maps relate to Łukasiewicz paths, that is to discrete excursions, with increments in
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq-1}
, stopped upon reaching
-1
.
The Boltzmann law on maps induces a law on Łukasiewicz paths, in which a path is sampled using the weights
\widetilde{q}_{k}=\binom{2k+1}{k+1}q_{k+1}
for
k\geq 0
and
\widetilde{q}_{-1}=1
on the increment sizes.
Since by definition the sum of the increments of a Łukasiewicz path equals
-1
, then if one replaces the weight sequence by
q^{\prime}_{k}=a^{k}\widetilde{q}_{k}
for some given
a>0
, then the total weight of any path is simply divided by
a
. Thus, one can replace
\widetilde{q}
by
q^{\prime}
without affecting the law of the random Łukasiewicz path associated with a
q
-Boltzmann map.

In particular, Boltzmann maps with
n
edges relate to
excursions with duration
n+1
of random walks with step distribution
(\nu_{q}(k);k\geq-1)
given by:

\nu_{q}(-1)=\frac{1}{Z_{q}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\nu_{q}(k)=Z_{q}^{k}\binom{2k+1}{k+1}q_{k+1}\qquad\text{for}\enskip k\geq 0,

provided such a
Z_{q}>1
exists to ensure that
\nu_{q}
is indeed a probability measure.

When
\nu_{q}
is centred and has finite variance, the corresponding random walk excursion converges after scaling to the Brownian excursion, and accordingly, the Boltzmann maps conditioned to have many edges converge to the Brownian sphere, see e.g.
[
Mar22a
, Section 6.4]
for a more general statement.
However, when the probability
\nu_{q}
is centred and has the asymptotic behaviour
\nu_{q}(k)\sim ck^{-1-\alpha}
as
k\to\infty
for some unimportant constant
c>0
and an (important) index
\alpha\in(1,2)
, such as in the explicit examples (
1.1
) in the introduction, then the excursion converges to that of
X^{\alpha}
. In this regime, Le Gall & Miermont
[
LGM11
]
proved Theorem
2.4
(i) and (ii), especially the convergence after extraction of a subsequence to a limiting space; and they also computed the Hausdorff dimension
2\alpha
of these limits.

In the aforementioned bijection, the vertices of a Boltzmann map relate to the negative jumps of the
\nu_{q}
-excursion. From this, one can prove that the number of vertices in such a map conditioned to have
n
edges concentrates around
\nu_{q}(-1)n
, see again
[
Mar22a
, Section 6.4]
.
We have considered in
[
KM23
]
maps
M^{n,K_{n}}
with weights in the
\alpha
-stable regime as above and multiconditioned to have
n
edges and
K_{n}
vertices.
The corresponding excursion
W^{n,K_{n}}
has the law of a
\nu_{q}
-random excursion conditioned both to have duration
n+1
and a total
K_{n}-1
of negative increments.
Then Theorem 4.4(i) in
[
KM23
]
shows that if we set
r_{n}=(\alpha\Gamma(-\alpha)n)^{1/\alpha}
and if there exists
\vartheta\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\frac{K_{n}-\nu_{q}(-1)n}{r_{n}(1-\nu_{q}(-1))}\enskip\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{n\to\infty}\enskip\vartheta,

(6.2)

then

(r_{n}^{-1}W^{n,K_{n}}_{\lfloor nt\rfloor})_{t\in[0,1]}\enskip\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{(d)}_{n\to\infty}\enskip X^{\alpha,\vartheta,\mathrm{ex}}.

(6.3)

Let us mention that the statement in
[
KM23
]
actually applies more generally to non-centred distributions, and the entire domain of attraction of a stable law.
The case
\alpha=2
is also treated there, although in this regime the limit
X^{2,\vartheta,\mathrm{ex}}
is simply the Brownian excursion independently of
\vartheta
, as it can be checked using Girsanov’s formula (see Section
6.3
below).

Let
\mathsf{L}(W^{n,K_{n}})
be the discrete looptree coded by the Łukasiewicz path
W^{n,K_{n}}
in the sense of Section
2.1
.
By a direct application of our previous results, namely theorems
2.1
and
1.1
for looptrees and theorems
2.4
and
1.2
for maps we infer the following results.

Corollary 6.1
.

Let
\alpha\in(1,2)
and
\vartheta\in(-\infty,\infty)
and let
K_{n}
satisfy (
6.2
). Then

r_{n}^{-1}\,\mathsf{L}(W^{n,K_{n}})\enskip\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{(d)}_{n\to\infty}\enskip{L}(X^{\alpha,\vartheta,\mathrm{ex}}),

and the limit satisfies almost surely:

\dim_{H}{L}(X^{\alpha,\vartheta,\mathrm{ex}})=\dim_{p}{L}(X^{\alpha,\vartheta,\mathrm{ex}})=\diminf{L}(X^{\alpha,\vartheta,\mathrm{ex}})=\dimsup{L}(X^{\alpha,\vartheta,\mathrm{ex}})=\alpha.

In addition, the sequence
((2r_{n})^{-1/2}M^{n,K_{n}})_{n}
is tight and every subsequential limit, say
M^{\alpha,\vartheta}
, has almost surely:

\dim_{H}M^{\alpha,\vartheta}=\dim_{p}M^{\alpha,\vartheta}=\diminf M^{\alpha,\vartheta}=\dimsup M^{\alpha,\vartheta}=2\alpha.

One can also derive the convergence without extraction of the profile and some other statistics on the distances in the map by application of Theorem
2.4
.

One motivation of this work, following
[
KM23
]
, was to make explicit this result, based on the convergence (
6.3
).
In the rest of this section, we consider the continuum objects
X^{\alpha,\vartheta,\mathrm{ex}}
,
{L}(X^{\alpha,\vartheta,\mathrm{ex}})
, and
M^{\alpha,\vartheta}
and their behaviour as the two parameters
\alpha
,
\vartheta
vary.

6.2.
Varying the index of stability and the drift

Our main result in this section deals with the asymptotic behaviour of
X^{\alpha,\vartheta,\mathrm{ex}}
in the Skorokhod
J_{1}
topology as
\alpha
and
\vartheta
vary.
It extends Theorem
1.3
in the introduction which focused on a fixed
\alpha
.

Theorem 6.2
.

Assume that the pair
(\alpha,\vartheta)\in(1,2)\times(-\infty,\infty)
converges to a limit
(\alpha_{\ast},\vartheta_{\ast})\in[1,2]\times[-\infty,\infty]
.

(i)

In the three cases when either both
\alpha_{\ast}=2
and
\vartheta_{\ast}<\infty
, or
\vartheta_{\ast}=-\infty
, or both
\alpha_{\ast}=1
and
\vartheta_{\ast}<-1
, it holds:

\biggl(\frac{|\vartheta|^{(2-\alpha)/(\alpha-1)}}{(\alpha-1)\alpha^{1/(\alpha-1)}}\biggr)^{1/2}X^{\alpha,\vartheta,\mathrm{ex}}\enskip\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{(d)}\enskip\boldsymbol{e},

where
\boldsymbol{e}
is the standard Brownian excursion.

(ii)

In the two cases when either both
\alpha_{\ast}<2
and
\vartheta_{\ast}=\infty
or both
\alpha_{\ast}=1
and
\vartheta_{\ast}>-1
it holds:

\Bigl(\frac{1}{\vartheta+1}\cdot X^{\alpha,\vartheta,\mathrm{ex}}_{(1-t)-}\Bigr)_{t\in[0,1]}\enskip\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{(d)}\enskip(t\,\mathbf{1}_{\{t\neq 1\}})_{t\in[0,1]}.

The time-reversal in the second statement is only a way to formally state that
(\vartheta+1)^{-1}X^{\alpha,\vartheta,\mathrm{ex}}_{t}\to(1-t)\mathbf{1}_{\{0<t\leq 1\}}
since this limit is not right-continuous at
0
. Alternatively, one could extend all paths on the negative half-line by setting them equal to
0
on
(-\infty,0)
, allowing for a jump at
0
.
Let us recall Figure
4
in the introduction for simulations of
X^{\alpha,\vartheta,\mathrm{ex}}
displaying four different regimes from this theorem and let us refer to Figure
11
for a diagram for these regimes.
We leave open the two regimes in which either

(\alpha,\vartheta)\to(2,\infty)
or
(\alpha,\vartheta)\to(1,-1)
,
for which the behaviour depends more precisely on the speed of each convergence, see also the discussion in Section
6.7
below.

Figure 11
:
Diagram representing the different regimes in Theorem
6.2
. At each point
(\alpha,\vartheta)
in the open strip lies an excursion of an
\alpha
-stable process with drift
-\vartheta
. On the plain line part of the boundary, these excursions converge to the Brownian excursion; on the dashed line part, they converge to the linear drift. The two points at the intersection of these regimes are excluded. Finally the dotted vertical line, corresponding to
\vartheta=0
was first considered in
[
CK14
]
.

For a fixed
\alpha\in(1,2)
, this result complements
[
KM23
, Theorem 4.4]
.
Indeed, we previously quoted Item (i) in this theorem in (
6.3
), when
(K_{n}-\nu_{q}(-1)n)/r_{n}
has a finite limit.
Items (ii) and (iii) in
[
KM23
, Theorem 4.4]
focus on the case when this limit is infinite. Then, under a different scaling which depends on the speed of this convergence, the discrete path
W^{n,K_{n}}
converges in distribution towards the Brownian excursion (in the case of
-\infty
) and to the linear drift
(1-t)\mathbf{1}_{\{0<t\leq 1\}}
(in the case of
+\infty
).
Theorem
6.2
thus completes this diagram by showing that, once suitably rescaled, the excursion
X^{\alpha,\vartheta,\mathrm{ex}}
further converges in distribution to these two limits as
\vartheta\to\pm\infty
. This provides a way to interpolate continuously between these extreme models, passing through a stable regime for a given
\alpha\in(1,2)
.

For
\vartheta
fixed equal to
0
, Theorem
6.2
recovers
[
CK14
, Proposition 3.5 and Proposition 3.6]
.
We see here that when
\alpha\uparrow 2
, the limit is always the Brownian excursion, whatever the (finite) value of
\vartheta
. However the behaviour when
\alpha\downarrow 1
depends on the value of
\vartheta
: we recover the linear drift when
\vartheta>-1
, but the Brownian excursion instead when
\vartheta<-1
.
To understand intuitively this change of behaviour, one can notice that the unconditioned process
X^{\alpha}
converges in probability to the linear drift
t\mapsto-t
as
\alpha\downarrow 1
.
This can be easily shown using the explicit expression of the characteristic function given in (
6.1
).
Then informally,
X^{\alpha}
naturally wants to end near
-1
as
\alpha\downarrow 1
and without jumping. Thus if we impose to end at a
X^{\alpha}_{1}=\vartheta<-1
, then it will follow the corresponding drift and avoid any positive jump, hence Brownian fluctuations, whereas if we impose to end at
X^{\alpha}_{1}=\vartheta>-1
, then the easiest way for it is to make a single jump of size about
\vartheta+1
and drift approximately like
-t
otherwise.

This second case is a new instance of the phenomenon known as the ‘one big jump principle’, well-known for discrete conditioned random walks such as in
[
DDS08
,
AL11
]
and more recently
[
BBY23
]
. Closer to this work, Konstantopoulos & Richardson
[
KR02
]
considered also spectrally positive Lévy processes, with a stable flavour, but rather somehow conditioned in the regular sense on the event
\{X^{\alpha}_{1}>\vartheta\}
as
\vartheta\to\infty
, see also
[
DJ12
, Theorem 6]
.

Combining Theorem
6.2
with Corollary
6.1
and either Theorem
5.2
for looptrees or Theorem
5.3
for maps we infer the following results.

Corollary 6.3
.

Assume that the pair
(\alpha,\vartheta)
converges to
(\alpha_{\ast},\vartheta_{\ast})\in[1,2]\times[-\infty,\infty]
.
Then the following convergences hold for the Gromov–Hausdorff–Prokhorov topology.

(i)

In the three cases when either both
\alpha_{\ast}=2
and
\vartheta_{\ast}<\infty
, or
\vartheta_{\ast}=-\infty
, or both
\alpha_{\ast}=1
and
\vartheta_{\ast}<-1
, it holds:

\biggl(\frac{4|\vartheta|^{(2-\alpha)/(\alpha-1)}}{(\alpha-1)\alpha^{1/(\alpha-1)}}\biggr)^{1/2}{L}(X^{\alpha,\vartheta,\mathrm{ex}})\enskip\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{(d)}\enskip{T}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\biggl(\frac{9|\vartheta|^{(2-\alpha)/(\alpha-1)}}{(\alpha-1)\alpha^{1/(\alpha-1)}}\biggr)^{1/4}M^{\alpha,\vartheta}\enskip\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{(d)}\enskip{S},

where
{T}
is the standard Brownian tree
and
{S}
the standard Brownian sphere.

(ii)

If the pair
(\alpha,\vartheta)
converges to
(\alpha_{\ast},\vartheta_{\ast})
and either both
\alpha_{\ast}<2
and
\vartheta_{\ast}=\infty
or both
\alpha_{\ast}=1
and
\vartheta_{\ast}>-1
, then it holds instead:

|\vartheta+1|^{-1}{L}(X^{\alpha,\vartheta,\mathrm{ex}})\enskip\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{(d)}\enskip{C}\qquad\text{and}\qquad|\vartheta+1|^{-1/2}M^{\alpha,\vartheta}\enskip\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{(d)}\enskip{T},

where
{C}
is the circle with unit length
and
{T}
is again the standard Brownian tree.

In the first claim, let us notice that the continuous part associated with
X^{\alpha,\vartheta,\mathrm{ex}}
, as defined in (
2.4
), vanishes since the Lévy process has no Gaussian component (recall the discussion a few lines below (
2.4
)). The limit of the excursion in this regime is the Brownian excursion, whose associated looptree is the Brownian tree, and the parameters
1/2
and
1/3
in Theorem
5.2
simply translate into multiplicative constants
1/2
and
1/\sqrt{3}
respectively (recall the discussion after this theorem). This explains the difference of constants compared to Theorem
6.2
.
Finally, let us note that actually, the last claim is not included in Theorem
5.2
and
5.3
since the limit path
(1-t)\mathbf{1}_{\{0<t<1\}}
is not an excursion of a Lévy process, but this degenerate regime can be studied by hand, see also
[
Mar22b
, Section 4]
for more general results under a ‘pure jump’ property.

6.3.
Exponential tilting

Lut us here recall a change of measure in Lévy processes using the exponential martingale, called the Esscher transform, which we shall use in the next subsection. We refer the reader to
[
Kyp14
, Chapter 3.3]
for details.
Let
X
be any Lévy process and let
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
be such that
\psi(\kappa)=\log\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{e}^{-\kappa X_{1}}]
is well-defined. Then one can define a process
X^{(\kappa)}
characterised by the absolute continuity relation: for every
t\geq 0
and for every continuous and bounded functional
F
,

\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}[F(X^{(\kappa)}_{s},s\leq t)]=\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}[F(X_{s},s\leq t)\operatorname{e}^{-\kappa X_{t}-\psi(\kappa)t}].

(6.4)

By
[
Kyp14
, Theorem 3.9]
, the process
X^{(\kappa)}
remains a Lévy process.
Further, as noted in
[
Kyp14
, Corollary 3.10]
, if
X
has no negative jump, then
X^{(\kappa)}
is well-defined for any
\kappa\geq 0
and
X^{(\kappa)}
also has no negative jump, and for every
\kappa,\lambda\geq 0
, we have:

\psi^{(\kappa)}(\lambda)=\log\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{e}^{-\lambda X^{(\kappa)}_{1}}]=\log\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{e}^{-\lambda X_{1}-\kappa X_{1}-\psi(\kappa)}]=\psi(\lambda+\kappa)-\psi(\kappa),

so
X^{(\kappa)}
has drift
-\psi^{\prime}(\kappa)
.
In the particular case of the standard Brownian motion, so
\psi(\lambda)=\lambda^{2}/2
, the process
X^{(\kappa)}
is well-defined for any
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
and reduces to the Brownian motion minus the drift with slope
\kappa
.

The following result shows that this exponential tilting has no effect on a bridge-type conditioning.
We let
X
be a general spectrally positive Lévy process with infinite variation paths and whose characteristic function is integrable (
1.3
).

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
\kappa>0
and define
X^{(\kappa)}
as in (
6.4
).
Then for every
\vartheta\in\mathbb{R}
, the laws of
(X_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
under
\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(\,\cdot\mid X_{1}=\vartheta)
and of
(X^{(\kappa)}_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
under
\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(\,\cdot\mid X^{(\kappa)}_{1}=\vartheta)
are equal.

The point of this change of measure is to make the endpoint
\vartheta
‘typical’ by matching it with the drift
-\psi^{\prime}(\kappa)
, when possible.
We shall use it on our stable Lévy process
X^{\alpha}
with
\psi(\lambda)=\lambda^{\alpha}
for
\lambda\geq 0
, so one must impose
\kappa\geq 0
and so
\vartheta=-\alpha\kappa^{\alpha-1}\leq 0
.

Proof.

Recall from the discussion in the beginning of Section
2.2
that the integrability condition (
1.3
) on
(X_{t})_{t}
ensures that it indeed admits transition densities
(p_{t})_{t}
which are bi-continuous and positive everywhere, and which are used to define bridge versions, via the absolute continuity relation (
2.1
).
We easily derive from (
6.4
) transition densities
(p^{(\kappa)}_{t})_{t}
for
(X^{(\kappa)})_{t}
, namely:

p^{(\kappa)}_{t}(x)=p_{t}(x)\operatorname{e}^{-\kappa x-\psi(\kappa)t}.

They inherit the continuity and positivity properties of
(p_{t})_{t}
, so we may define bridges associated with
X^{(\kappa)}
in the same way as
X
.
Also, note that for
0<t<1
and
\vartheta\in\mathbb{R}
, we have:

\frac{p^{(\kappa)}_{1-t}(\vartheta-X^{(\kappa)}_{t})}{p^{(\kappa)}_{1}(\vartheta)}=\frac{p_{1-t}(\vartheta-X^{(\kappa)}_{t})\operatorname{e}^{-\kappa(\vartheta-X^{(\kappa)}_{t})-\psi(\kappa)(1-t)}}{p_{1}(\vartheta)\operatorname{e}^{-\kappa\vartheta-\psi(\kappa)}}=\frac{p_{1-t}(\vartheta-X^{(\kappa)}_{t})}{p_{1}(\vartheta)}\operatorname{e}^{\kappa X^{(\kappa)}_{t}+\psi(\kappa)t}.

The exponential term on the right is exactly the inverse of that in (
6.4
).
Combined with the characterisation (
2.1
) of the bridges, we infer for any
t\in(0,1)
and any continuous and bounded function
F
:

\displaystyle\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}[F(X^{(\kappa)}_{s},s\leq t)\mid X^{(\kappa)}_{1}=\vartheta]

\displaystyle=\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}\Bigl[F(X^{(\kappa)}_{s},s\leq t)\frac{p^{(\kappa)}_{1-t}(\vartheta-X^{(\kappa)}_{t})}{p^{(\kappa)}_{1}(\vartheta)}\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}\Bigl[F(X^{(\kappa)}_{s},s\leq t)\frac{p_{1-t}(\vartheta-X^{(\kappa)}_{t})}{p_{1}(\vartheta)}\operatorname{e}^{\kappa X^{(\kappa)}_{t}+\psi(\kappa)t}\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}\Bigl[F(X_{s},s\leq t)\frac{p_{1-t}(\vartheta-X_{t})}{p_{t}(\vartheta)}\Bigr]

\displaystyle=\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}[F(X_{s},s\leq t)\mid X_{1}=\vartheta].

This indeed identifies the two bridge laws.
∎

Corollary 6.5
.

For any
\vartheta\in\mathbb{R}
, a unit duration Brownian bridge from
0
to
\vartheta
has the same law as the sum of a standard Brownian bridge and the linear drift with slope
\vartheta
.

Proof.

We have already observed that if
X=B
is a standard Brownian motion, then
B^{(\kappa)}
is well-defined for any
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
and reduces to a Brownian motion minus the drift with slope
\kappa
.
For
\kappa=-\vartheta
, Lemma
6.4
thus shows that
the law of
(B_{t}+\vartheta t)_{t\in[0,1]}
under
\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(\,\cdot\mid B_{1}+\vartheta=\vartheta)
does not depend on
\vartheta\in\mathbb{R}
.
On the other hand, we have already argued that is the same as
the law of
(B_{t}+\vartheta t)_{t\in[0,1]}
under
\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(\,\cdot\mid B_{1}=0)
.
∎

6.4.
The Brownian regime

Let us first prove Theorem
6.2
(i)
on the convergence of the rescaled excursion path
X^{\alpha,\vartheta,\mathrm{ex}}
to a Brownian excursion.
According to Lemma
5.1
, it suffices to prove the convergence of the transition densities.
In the case
\alpha\uparrow 2
and
\vartheta
has a finite limit, we can easily do it directly, but
otherwise we adapt this reasoning using the above Esscher transform first.
Let us start with the first case.

Proof of Theorem
6.2
(i)
in the case
\alpha_{\ast}=2
and
\vartheta_{\ast}\in(-\infty,\infty)
.

Let us set for every
t\in[0,1]
:

\Bigl(\frac{|\vartheta|^{(2-\alpha)/(\alpha-1)}}{(\alpha-1)\alpha^{1/(\alpha-1)}}\Bigr)^{1/2}(X^{\alpha}_{t}-\vartheta t).

(6.5)

Our aim is to show that under under the excursion law, it converges in distribution to the standard Brownian excursion.
Notice that the scaling factor converges to
1/\sqrt{2}
, so we may as well replace it by this value.

For every
t>0
, let
p^{\alpha,\vartheta}_{t}
denote the density of
X^{\alpha}_{t}-\vartheta t
and let
p^{2,\vartheta_{\ast}}_{t}
that of
X^{2}_{t}-\vartheta_{\ast}t
where
X^{2}
is
\sqrt{2}
times a Brownian motion.
Then by inverse Fourier transform, we have the following uniform bound:

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}|p^{\alpha,\vartheta}_{t}(x)-p^{2,\vartheta_{\ast}}_{t}(x)|\leq\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{e}^{iu(X^{\alpha}_{t}-\vartheta t)}]-\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{e}^{iu(X^{2}_{t}-\vartheta_{\ast}t)}]|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u.

One can easily check from the explicit expression (
6.1
) of the characteristic function of
X^{\alpha}_{t}
that the integrand on the right-hand side converges pointwise to
0
as
(\alpha,\vartheta)\to(2,\vartheta_{\ast})
.
Further it is easily seen to the be dominated by an integrable function uniformly for
\alpha
bounded away from
1
.
We conclude from the dominated convergence theorem that
p^{\alpha,\vartheta}_{t}
converges uniformly to
p^{2,\vartheta_{\ast}}_{t}
.
By applying Lemma
5.1
with the endpoint
0
, this implies after a shift in both conditionings that
(X^{\alpha}_{t}-\vartheta t)_{t\in[0,1]}
under
\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(\,\cdot\mid X^{\alpha}_{1}=\vartheta)
converges in law to
(X^{2}-\vartheta_{\ast}t)_{t\in[0,1]}
under
\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(\,\cdot\mid X^{2}_{1}=\vartheta_{\ast})
.
By Corollary
6.5
, the latter has the same law as
\sqrt{2}
times the standard Brownian bridge.
The excursions further converge by the Vervaat transform.
∎

We know turn to the second case, using the
Esscher transform
presented in Section
6.3
.

Proof of Theorem
6.2
(i)
when either
\vartheta_{\ast}=-\infty
or both
\alpha_{\ast}=1
and
\vartheta_{\ast}<-1
.

We shall assume that
\vartheta<0
.
Let
\kappa=(|\vartheta|/\alpha)^{1/(\alpha-1)}>0
be the solution to
\vartheta=-\alpha\kappa^{\alpha-1}
and observe that under either assumption
the ratio
|\vartheta|/\alpha
is asymptotically larger than
1
. In particular
\kappa
tends to infinity and even faster than any negative power of
\alpha-1
.
Let
X^{(\alpha,\kappa)}
be defined by (
6.4
) from the stable process
X^{\alpha}
and
let then
Y^{(\alpha,\kappa)}
be the process given by:

Y^{(\alpha,\kappa)}_{t}=\biggl(\frac{|\vartheta|^{(2-\alpha)/(\alpha-1)}}{(\alpha-1)\alpha^{1/(\alpha-1)}}\biggr)^{1/2}(X^{(\alpha,\kappa)}_{t}-\vartheta t)=\frac{\kappa}{\sqrt{\alpha(\alpha-1)\kappa^{\alpha}}}(X^{(\alpha,\kappa)}_{t}+\alpha\kappa^{\alpha-1}t),

for every
t\geq 0
.
It is enough to check that
Y^{(\alpha,\kappa)}
under
\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(\,\cdot\mid Y^{(\alpha,\kappa)}_{1}=0)
converges in distribution to the Brownian bridge. Indeed, by Lemma
6.4
, the law of
(X^{(\alpha,\kappa)}_{t}-\vartheta t)_{t\in[0,1]}
under
\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(\,\cdot\mid X^{(\alpha,\kappa)}_{1}=\vartheta)
is the same as that of
X^{\alpha,\vartheta,\mathrm{br}}
, and the desired result then follows by applying the Vervaat transform.
As for the case
\alpha\to 2
, by Lemma
5.1
, the convergence of the bridge of
Y^{(\alpha,\kappa)}
will follow from the convergence of its densities, and relying on inverse Fourier transform, it suffices to show that the characteristic function of
Y^{(\alpha,\kappa)}_{t}
converges to that of the centred Gaussian with variance
t
and is dominated by an integrable function independent of
\alpha
and
\kappa
.

First, recall that the Laplace transform of
X^{\alpha}
extends to the complex half plane,
namely for any complex number
z=(a+ib)
with
a>0
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}[\exp(-zX^{\alpha}_{t})]

\displaystyle=\exp\bigl(t|z|^{\alpha}\operatorname{e}^{i\alpha\arg(z)}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\exp\bigl(t(a^{2}+b^{2})^{\alpha/2}\bigl(\cos(\alpha\arctan(b/a))+i\sin(\alpha\arctan(b/a))\bigr)\bigr).

Consequently, fix
u\in\mathbb{R}
and let
x=(\alpha(\alpha-1)\kappa^{\alpha})^{-1/2}u
to ease notation, then we have:

\displaystyle\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}[\exp(iuY^{(\alpha,\kappa)}_{t})]

\displaystyle=\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}\bigl[\exp\bigl(i\kappa x(X^{\alpha}_{t}+\alpha\kappa^{\alpha-1}t)-\kappa X^{\alpha}_{t}-\kappa^{\alpha}t\bigr)\bigr]

\displaystyle=\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}\bigl[\exp\bigl(-(\kappa-i\kappa x)X^{\alpha}_{t}\bigr)\bigr]\exp(i\alpha x\kappa^{\alpha}t-\kappa^{\alpha}t)

\displaystyle=\exp\bigl(t(\kappa^{2}+(\kappa x)^{2})^{\alpha/2}\bigl(\cos(\alpha\arctan(-x))+i\sin(\alpha\arctan(-x))\bigr)\bigr)\exp(i\alpha x\kappa^{\alpha}t-\kappa^{\alpha}t)

\displaystyle=\exp\bigl(t\kappa^{\alpha}\bigl((1+x^{2})^{\alpha/2}\cos(\alpha\arctan(x))-1\bigr)-it\kappa^{\alpha}\bigl((1+x^{2})^{\alpha/2}\sin(\alpha\arctan(x))-\alpha x\bigr)\bigr).

Notice that
x\to 0
, then elementary calculus shows that

(1+x^{2})^{\alpha/2}\cos(\alpha\arctan(x))-1\sim-\frac{\alpha(\alpha-1)x^{2}}{2}=-\frac{u^{2}}{2\kappa^{\alpha}},

as well as

(1+x^{2})^{\alpha/2}\sin(\alpha\arctan(x))-\alpha x\sim\frac{\alpha x^{3}}{2}=\frac{u^{3}}{2\sqrt{\alpha}((\alpha-1)\kappa^{\alpha})^{3/2}}=o(\kappa^{-\alpha}),

since
\kappa
grows faster than any negative power of
\alpha-1
.
We infer that

\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}[\exp(iuY^{(\alpha,\kappa)}_{t})]\enskip\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{\kappa\to\infty}\enskip\exp\Bigl(-\frac{u^{2}t}{2}\Bigr).

Next we claim that there exist universal constants
C,q>0
such that for all
u\in\mathbb{R}
,

|\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}[\exp(iuY^{(\alpha,\kappa)}_{t})]|\leq\exp(-C|u|^{q}t).

(6.6)

Let us again put
x=u/\sqrt{\alpha(\alpha-1)\kappa^{\alpha}}
to ease notation and recall the explicit expression of the characteristic function above, namely:

\frac{1}{t}\log|\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}[\exp(iuY^{(\alpha,\kappa)}_{t})]|=\kappa^{\alpha}(1+x^{2})^{\alpha/2}\cos(\alpha\arctan x)-\kappa^{\alpha}.

Since the function is even, we focus on the case
x>0
.
First, one can refine the preceding asymptotic equivalence to show that

-\frac{\alpha(\alpha-1)x^{2}}{2}\leq(1+x^{2})^{\alpha/2}\cos(\alpha\arctan x)-1\leq-\frac{\alpha(\alpha-1)x^{2}}{2}(1-12x^{2}).

Hence for
|x|\leq 1/\sqrt{24}
, the second inequality yields:

\kappa^{\alpha}(1+x^{2})^{\alpha/2}\cos(\alpha\arctan x)-\kappa^{\alpha}\leq-\frac{\alpha(\alpha-1)\kappa^{\alpha}x^{2}}{4},

and the claimed bound (
6.6
) thus holds
in this case, with
q=2
.
On the other hand,
letting
y=\arctan x\in(0,\pi/2)
, the derivative of
x^{-\alpha}((1+x^{2})^{\alpha/2}\cos(\alpha\arctan x)-1)
has the same sign as

\cos(\alpha y)-\tan(y)\sin(\alpha y)-1=\cos(y)\cos(\alpha y)-\sin(y)\sin(\alpha y)-1=\cos((\alpha+1)y)-1<0.

Therefore the function is decreasing, and since it vanishes at
0
by the preceding bounds, then letting
-c<0
denote its value at
x=1/\sqrt{24}
, we infer that for every
x\geq 1/\sqrt{24}
, we have:

\kappa^{\alpha}(1+x^{2})^{\alpha/2}\cos(\alpha\arctan x)-\kappa^{\alpha}\leq-c(\kappa x)^{\alpha}=-c\alpha^{-\alpha/2}(\alpha-1)^{-\alpha/2}\kappa^{\alpha(1-\alpha/2)}u^{\alpha}.

Since
\kappa
grows faster than any negative power of
\alpha-1
, then the claimed bound (
6.6
) also holds
in this case,
with
q=\alpha
.

We conclude from Lebesgue’s theorem that

\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}[\exp(iuY^{(\alpha,\kappa)}_{t})]-\operatorname{e}^{-u^{2}t/2}|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u

converges to
0
, so by inverse Fourier transform, the density of
Y^{(\alpha,\kappa)}_{t}
converges uniformly to that of a Brownian motion at time
t
, and so do the bridges and excursions from Lemma
5.1
.
∎

6.5.
Technical estimates on stable laws

We next want to consider the behaviour when either
\alpha\downarrow 1
or
\vartheta\to\infty
.
In order to transfer estimates from the unconditioned process
X^{\alpha}
to a bridge conditioned on
X^{\alpha}_{1}=\vartheta
, we need some estimates on the transition densities of this process.
Recall that the characteristic function of
X^{\alpha}_{t}
is given by:

\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}[\exp(iuX^{\alpha}_{t})]=\exp\Bigl(t|u|^{\alpha}\Bigl(\cos\Bigl(\frac{\pi\alpha}{2}\Bigr)-i\sigma(u)\sin\Bigl(\frac{\pi\alpha}{2}\Bigr)\Bigr)\Bigr),

for every
u\in\mathbb{R}
,
so by inverse Fourier, the density
p^{\alpha}_{t}
of
X^{\alpha}_{t}
is precisely given by:

\displaystyle p^{\alpha}_{t}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\operatorname{e}^{-iux}\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{e}^{iuX^{\alpha}_{t}}]\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\operatorname{e}^{tu^{\alpha}\cos(\pi\alpha/2)}\cos\bigl(ux+tu^{\alpha}\sin(\pi\alpha/2)\bigr)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u.

It is bounded, and precisely the change of variable
v=-\cos(\pi\alpha/2)tu^{\alpha}
yields:

p^{\alpha}_{t}(x)\leq\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\operatorname{e}^{tu^{\alpha}\cos(\pi\alpha/2)}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u\leq\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\alpha|t\cos(\pi\alpha/2)|^{1/\alpha}}v^{\frac{1}{\alpha}-1}\operatorname{e}^{-v}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v=\frac{\Gamma(1/\alpha)}{\alpha\pi|t\cos(\pi\alpha/2)|^{1/\alpha}}.

This upper bound is finite for any
\alpha\in(1,2)
and as
\alpha\downarrow 1
, we have
\cos(\pi\alpha/2)=-\sin(\pi(\alpha-1)/2)\sim-\pi(\alpha-1)/2
, so:

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}p^{\alpha}_{t}(x)\leq\frac{\Gamma(1/\alpha)}{\alpha\pi|t\cos(\pi\alpha/2)|^{1/\alpha}}\enskip\mathop{\sim}_{\alpha\downarrow 1}\enskip\frac{2}{\pi^{2}t(\alpha-1)},

(6.7)

where we used that
(\alpha-1)^{1-1/\alpha}\to 1
as
\alpha\to 1
.
Note that the scaling property (or a simple change of variable in the inverse Fourier transform) implies that
p^{\alpha}_{t}(x)=t^{-1/\alpha}p^{\alpha}_{1}(t^{-1/\alpha}x)
. For this reason, below we restrict to
t=1
.

When
\alpha\in(1,2)
is fixed, Zolotarev
[
Zol86
, Chapter 2.5]
provides precise series expansions as
x\to\pm\infty
of the density
p^{\alpha}_{1}(x)
, which corresponds to
g(x,\alpha,1)
in the notation there. In particular, by
[
Zol86
, Equation 2.5.4]
we have:

p^{\alpha}_{1}(x)\enskip\mathop{\sim}_{x\to\infty}\enskip\frac{\alpha\Gamma(\alpha)}{\pi}\sin(\pi(2-\alpha))x^{-\alpha-1}.

Note that as
\alpha\downarrow 1
, we have
\sin(\pi(2-\alpha))=\sin(\pi(\alpha-1))\sim\pi(\alpha-1)
.
In the next lemma, we let both
x
converge to a finite or infinite limit and
\alpha\downarrow 1
.

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
x_{\ast}\in(-1,\infty]
, then as the pair
(\alpha,x)
converge to
(1,x_{\ast})
, we have:

\frac{(1+x)^{\alpha+1}p^{\alpha}_{1}(x)}{\alpha-1}\enskip\mathop{\longrightarrow}\enskip 1.

In addition, for every
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
C>0
such that the upper bound

\frac{(1+x)^{\alpha+1}p^{\alpha}_{1}(x)}{\alpha-1}\leq C

holds uniformly for all
\alpha\in(1,2-\varepsilon]
and
x\geq-1+\varepsilon
.

The proof of involves heavy computations relying on some results from
[
Zol86
]
, and is deferred to Section
6.8
.
Combined with (
6.7
), we infer that when either
\alpha\downarrow 1
or
\vartheta\to\infty
, we have for any
t\in(0,1)
fixed:

\frac{\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}p^{\alpha}_{t}(x)}{p^{\alpha}_{1}(\vartheta)}\leq\frac{2}{\pi^{2}t}\frac{(\vartheta+1)^{\alpha+1}}{(\alpha-1)^{2}}(1+o(1)).

(6.8)

In addition, using also the scaling property
p^{\alpha}_{t}(x)=t^{-1/\alpha}p^{\alpha}_{1}(t^{-1/\alpha}x)
, for every
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
C_{\varepsilon}>0
such that the bound

\frac{p^{\alpha}_{t}(x)}{p^{\alpha}_{1}(\vartheta)}\leq t^{-1/\alpha}C_{\varepsilon}\Bigl(\frac{\vartheta+1}{t^{-1/\alpha}x+1}\Bigr)^{\alpha+1}

(6.9)

holds for every
x\geq t^{1/\alpha}(-1+\varepsilon)
and
\alpha\leq 2-\varepsilon
.
This will enable us to transfer bounds on probabilities from the unconditioned process to the bridge version.

6.6.
The condensation regime

Let us next turn to Theorem
6.2
(ii)
and prove that when either
\vartheta\to\infty
while
\alpha
stays bounded away from
2
, or
\alpha\to 1
and
\vartheta>-1
stays bounded away from
-1
, we have:

(|\vartheta+1|^{-1}(X^{\alpha}_{t}-\vartheta t))_{t\in[0,1]}\enskip\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{(d)}\enskip(\mathbf{1}_{\{U\leq t\}}-t)_{t\in[0,1]},

(6.10)

under the conditional law
\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(\,\cdot\mid X^{\alpha}_{1}=\vartheta)
,
where
U
has the uniform distribution on
[0,1]
.
The actual claim of the theorem, under the excursion law, then follows from the Vervaat transform as before.

The idea is to cut the trajectory into three components as follows.
Let
\pi^{\alpha}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x)=\frac{\alpha(\alpha-1)}{\Gamma(2-\alpha)}x^{-\alpha-1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
denote the Lévy measure of
X^{\alpha}
and set

c=(\alpha-1)^{p}(\vartheta+1)^{q}

with some
p\in(0,1)
small and
q\in(0,1)
close to
1
.
Then let us write:

X^{\alpha}_{t}=\sum_{s\leq t}\Delta X^{\alpha}_{s}\mathbf{1}_{\{\Delta X^{\alpha}_{s}>c\}}+X^{\alpha,\leq c}_{t}-t\int_{c}^{\infty}x\pi^{\alpha}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x).

(6.11)

On the right, the integral equals the drift of the sum, therefore the middle term
X^{\alpha,\leq c}
is the process
X^{\alpha}
to which we remove every jump larger than
c
and that we recenter to give it mean
0
.

Let us first notice that

\int_{c}^{\infty}x\pi^{\alpha}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x)=\frac{\alpha}{\Gamma(2-\alpha)}c^{-(\alpha-1)}=\frac{\alpha}{\Gamma(2-\alpha)}(\alpha-1)^{-p(\alpha-1)}(\vartheta+1)^{-q(\alpha-1)},

which converges to
1
as both
\alpha\downarrow 1
and
\vartheta
is bounded away from
\infty
and it is small compared to
\vartheta
when the latter tends to infinity.
In any case, in the regimes of Theorem
6.2
(ii)
, we have:

\frac{\vartheta+\int_{c}^{\infty}x\pi^{\alpha}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x)}{\vartheta+1}\enskip\longrightarrow\enskip 1.

(6.12)

Thus if we decompose
(X^{\alpha}_{t}-\vartheta t)/(\vartheta+1)
as in (
6.11
), then the drift component converges to
-t
.
We shall prove that, for some well-chosen values of
p
and
q
, under the conditional law
\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(\,\cdot\mid X^{\alpha}_{1}=\vartheta)
, with high probability the path
X^{\alpha,\leq c}
is small compared to
\vartheta+1
and the path
\sum_{s\leq t}\Delta X^{\alpha}_{s}\mathbf{1}_{\{\Delta X^{\alpha}_{s}>c\}}
is composed of a single jump, with size about
\vartheta+1
.
This implies (
6.10
).

Let us first prove that the small jumps component
X^{\alpha,\leq c}
from (
6.11
) is small compared to
\vartheta+1
.
This relies on the following lemma inspired from
[
KR02
]
.

Lemma 6.7
.

Fix
0<r<p<1
and
0<q<s<1
and let

c=(\alpha-1)^{p}(\vartheta+1)^{q}
and

K=(\alpha-1)^{r}(\vartheta+1)^{s}
.
Then

\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\sup_{s\leq 1}|X^{\alpha,\leq c}_{s}|>K\bigm|X^{\alpha}_{1}=\vartheta\Bigr)\enskip\mathop{\longrightarrow}\enskip 0

as either
\vartheta\to\infty
and
\alpha\to\alpha_{\ast}<2
or
\vartheta\to\vartheta_{\ast}>-1
and
\alpha\to 1
.

Notice that

\frac{K}{\vartheta+1}=(\alpha-1)^{r}(\vartheta+1)^{s-1}\enskip\longrightarrow\enskip 0,

as either
\alpha\downarrow 1
or
\vartheta\to\infty
.
Combined with (
6.12
), we deduce that

(\vartheta+1)^{-1}\Bigl(X^{\alpha,\leq c}_{t}-\vartheta t-t\int_{c}^{\infty}x\pi^{\alpha}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x)\Bigr)\enskip\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}}\enskip-t,

(6.13)

under
\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(\,\cdot\mid X^{\alpha}_{1}=\vartheta)
and for the uniform topology on
[0,1]
.

Proof.

Let us start with the unconditioned process.
Note that
X^{\alpha,\leq c}
remains a Lévy process, and since the increments are bounded, then the Laplace transform is well-defined on the whole real axis and for every
t>0
and
\lambda>0
, we have:

\log\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}[\exp(\lambda X^{\alpha,\leq c}_{1})]=\int_{0}^{c}(\operatorname{e}^{\lambda u}-1-\lambda u)\pi^{\alpha}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u).

Recall the bound
\operatorname{e}^{x}-1-x\leq x^{2}
for
0\leq x\leq 1
, then for
\lambda=1/c
we get:

\log\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}[\exp(c^{-1}X^{\alpha,\leq c}_{1})]\leq\int_{0}^{c}(u/c)^{2}\frac{\alpha(\alpha-1)}{\Gamma(2-\alpha)}u^{-\alpha-1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u=\frac{\alpha(\alpha-1)}{(2-\alpha)\Gamma(2-\alpha)}c^{-\alpha},

Applying the maximal inequality to the exponential martingale with parameter
c^{-1}
, we obtain that for any
K>0
, we have

\displaystyle\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\sup_{s\leq 1/2}|X^{\alpha,\leq c}_{s}|>K\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\Bigl(-\frac{K}{c}+\frac{1}{2}\log\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}[\exp(c^{-1}X^{\alpha,\leq c}_{1})]\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\Bigl(-\frac{K}{c}+\frac{\alpha(\alpha-1)c^{-\alpha}}{2(2-\alpha)\Gamma(2-\alpha)}\Bigr),

see
[
KR02
, Proposition 1]
for details.
Let us next transfer this bound to the bridge version by absolute continuity. Since the bridge is invariant under time- and space-reversal, then we have, using (
6.8
) for the last inequality,

\displaystyle\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\sup_{s\leq 1}|X^{\alpha,\leq c}_{s}|>K\bigm|X^{\alpha}_{1}=\vartheta\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\mathbf{1}_{\{\sup_{s\leq 1/2}|X^{\alpha,\leq c}_{s}|>K\}}\frac{p^{\alpha}_{1/2}(\vartheta-X^{\alpha}_{1/2})}{p^{\alpha}_{1}(\vartheta)}\Bigr]

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\sup_{s\leq 1/2}|X^{\alpha,\leq c}_{s}|>K\Bigr)\frac{\sup p^{\alpha}_{1/2}}{p^{\alpha}_{1}(\vartheta)}

\displaystyle\leq 4\exp\Bigl(-\frac{K}{c}+\frac{\alpha(\alpha-1)c^{-\alpha}}{2(2-\alpha)\Gamma(2-\alpha)}\Bigr)\frac{4}{\pi^{2}}\frac{(\vartheta+1)^{\alpha+1}}{(\alpha-1)^{2}}(1+o(1)).

Our choice of
c
and
K
yields:

\frac{K}{c}=(\alpha-1)^{r-p}(\vartheta+1)^{s-q}\qquad\text{and}\qquad(\alpha-1)c^{-\alpha}=(\alpha-1)^{1-p}(\alpha-1)^{-p(\alpha-1)}(\vartheta+1)^{-q\alpha}.

From
r<p<1
and
0<q<s
we infer that
(\alpha-1)c^{-\alpha}\to 0
when either
\alpha\downarrow 1
or
\vartheta\to\infty
, and that

\exp(-K/c)

goes to
0
faster than
(\alpha-1)^{2}(\vartheta+1)^{-(\alpha+1)}
.
∎

We finally consider the jumps of
X^{\alpha}
larger than
c
in the decomposition (
6.11
).
By (
6.13
), if we remove these jumps from
(X^{\alpha}_{t}-\vartheta t)/(\vartheta+1)
, then the remaining path under
\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(\,\cdot\mid X^{\alpha}_{1}=\vartheta)
converges to the linear drift
-t
. Since the path has to end at
X^{\alpha}_{1}=\vartheta\to\vartheta_{\ast}>-1
, then it necessarily has at least one jump larger than
c
, and the sum of these jumps, rescaled by
\vartheta+1
, converges to
1
.
Let us prove that with high probability, there exists only one such jump, therefore necessarily of size
(\vartheta+1)(1+o(1))
.
Let us first rule out the possibility of more than two jumps larger than
c
.

Lemma 6.8
.

Suppose that either
\alpha\to\alpha_{\ast}\in(1,2)
and
\vartheta\to\infty
or
\alpha\downarrow 1
and
\vartheta\to\vartheta_{\ast}\in(-1,\infty]
.
Let
p\in(0,1/6)
and
1>q>(\alpha+1)/(2\alpha)
, which is indeed asymptotically smaller than
1
.
Let
c=(\alpha-1)^{p}(\vartheta+1)^{q}
, then

\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(\#\{t\in(0,1)\colon\Delta X^{\alpha}_{t}>c\}\geq 3\mid X^{\alpha}_{1}=\vartheta)\enskip\longrightarrow\enskip 0.

Proof.

Let us first consider the unconditioned process. The jump sizes of
X^{\alpha}
are then given by a Poisson random measure, hence:

\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(\#\{t\in(0,1)\colon\Delta X^{\alpha}_{t}>c\}\geq 3)=\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Poisson}(\overline{\pi}^{\alpha}(c))\geq 3)\sim\frac{\overline{\pi}^{\alpha}(c)^{3}}{6}.

The tail of the Lévy measure
\pi^{\alpha}
is given by:

\overline{\pi}^{\alpha}(c)=\int_{c}^{\infty}\frac{\alpha(\alpha-1)}{\Gamma(2-\alpha)}x^{-\alpha-1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\frac{\alpha-1}{\Gamma(2-\alpha)}c^{-\alpha}=\frac{\alpha-1}{\Gamma(2-\alpha)}(\alpha-1)^{-p\alpha}(\vartheta+1)^{-q\alpha},

which converges to
0
. Let us transfer this result to the bridge by absolute continuity and the bound (
6.8
) on the ratio of densities.
Precisely, for every
\varepsilon>0
, it holds:

\displaystyle\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(\#\{t\in(0,1-\varepsilon)\colon\Delta X^{\alpha}_{t}>c\}\geq 3\mid X^{\alpha}_{1}=\vartheta)

\displaystyle=\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\mathbf{1}_{\{\#\{t\in(0,1-\varepsilon)\colon\Delta X^{\alpha}_{t}>c\}\geq 3\}}\frac{p^{\alpha}_{\varepsilon}(\vartheta-X^{\alpha}_{1-\varepsilon})}{p^{\alpha}_{1}(\vartheta)}\Bigr]

\displaystyle\leq\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(\#\{t\in(0,1)\colon\Delta X^{\alpha}_{t}>c\}\geq 3)\frac{\sup p^{\alpha}_{\varepsilon}}{p^{\alpha}_{1}(\vartheta)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\overline{\pi}^{\alpha}(c)^{3}}{6}\frac{\sup p^{\alpha}_{\varepsilon}}{p^{\alpha}_{1}(\vartheta)}(1+o(1)).

Recall that
