\overline{\pi}^{\alpha}(c)=\frac{\alpha-1}{\Gamma(2-\alpha)}(\alpha-1)^{-p\alpha}(\vartheta+1)^{-q\alpha}
and recall the bound (
6.8
) on the ratio of densities, so the last line is bounded by some constant times

(\alpha-1)^{1-3p\alpha}(\vartheta+1)^{\alpha+1-3q\alpha},

and we have

1-3p\alpha>1-\alpha/2
is bounded away from
0

and

\alpha+1-3q\alpha<-(\alpha+1)/2<-1
.
This shows that with high probability the bridge does not make more than two jumps larger than
c
before time
1-\varepsilon
. By exchangeability, the probability that it makes one of its two largest jumps after time
1-\varepsilon
is small when
\varepsilon
is. Hence it does not make more than two jumps at all, with high probability.
∎

Remark 6.9
.

Let us point out that when
\alpha
stays bounded away from
1
and
\vartheta\to\infty
, the preceding argument can be adapted to show directly that

\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(\#\{t\in(0,1)\colon\Delta X^{\alpha}_{t}>c\}\geq 2\mid X^{\alpha}_{1}=\vartheta)\enskip\longrightarrow\enskip 0.

Indeed, the probability to make at least two jumps larger than
c
before time
1-\varepsilon
is now bounded above by a constant times
(\alpha-1)^{-2p\alpha}(\vartheta+1)^{\alpha+1-2q\alpha}
, which converges to
0
in this case.
However this fails when
\alpha\downarrow 1
.

Let us finally conclude that there exists a single jump, which completes the proof of the theorem.

Proof of Theorem
6.2
(ii)
.

Let us introduce some notation: let us
c
be as in Lemma
6.8
and set

J_{t}=(\vartheta+1)^{-1}\sum_{s\leq t}\Delta X^{\alpha}_{s}\mathbf{1}_{\{\Delta X^{\alpha}_{s}>c\}}\qquad{and}\qquad D_{t}=(\vartheta+1)^{-1}(X^{\alpha}_{t}-\vartheta t)-J_{t},

and then

Y_{t}=J_{t}+D_{t}=(\vartheta+1)^{-1}(X^{\alpha}_{t}-\vartheta t),

which corresponds to the decomposition (
6.11
).
By (
6.13
), under
\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(\,\cdot\mid X^{\alpha}_{1}=\vartheta)
, the path
(D_{t})_{t}
converges to the linear drift
t \mapsto-t
. Hence the path
(J_{t})_{t}
has to contain at least one jump. We just proved in Lemma
6.8
that with high probability, it has at most two, and actually only one when
\alpha_{\ast}>1
.
Let us prove that this remains true when
\alpha\downarrow 1
.

Let us work under
\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(\,\cdot\mid X^{\alpha}_{1}=\vartheta)
.
Fix an integer
K\geq 1
, let
\varepsilon=1/K
and let us split the interval
(0,1)
into subintervals of length
\varepsilon
. Our aim is to prove that with high probability, the path
(J_{t})_{t}
does not have jumps larger than
c
in two different subintervals. Indeed, on the other hand by exchangeability, the probability that the two largest jumps occur in a time-frame
\varepsilon
is small, so we can conclude that with high probability, it has at most (and hence exactly) one jump larger than
c
, necessarily of size approximately
\vartheta+1
.
Fix
\delta>0
and let us assume that
\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|D_{t}+t|\leq\delta/2
. Then
Y_{t}\geq D_{t}\geq-t-\delta/2
for all
t\in[0,1]
. Also, since
Y_{1}=0
almost surely then
Y_{1-t}\leq t+\delta/2
.

Let
1\leq k\leq K-1
and denote by
1-t=k\varepsilon
the right-edge of the
k
’th subinterval.
Then

\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(Y_{1-t}\in(-1+t+\delta,t-\delta)\mid X^{\alpha}_{1}=\vartheta)=\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\mathbf{1}_{\{Y_{1-t}\in(-1+t+\delta,t-\delta)\}}\frac{p^{\alpha}_{t}(\vartheta-X^{\alpha}_{1-t})}{p^{\alpha}_{1}(\vartheta)}\Bigr].

Notice that
\vartheta-X^{\alpha}_{1-t}=\vartheta t-(\vartheta+1)Y_{1-t}\geq-t+\delta(\vartheta+1)
on the event in the indicator function. We may then appeal to (
6.9
) to upper bound the ratio of the densities and infer that to for some constant
C
, which depends only on
\delta
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(Y_{1-t}\in(-1+t+\delta,t-\delta)\mid X^{\alpha}_{1}=\vartheta)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C\,t^{-1/\alpha}\mathop{}\!\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\mathbf{1}_{\{Y_{1-t}\in(-1+t+\delta,t-\delta)\}}\Bigl(\frac{\vartheta+1}{t^{-1/\alpha}(\vartheta-X^{\alpha}_{1-t})+1}\Bigr)^{\alpha+1}\Bigr]

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C\,t^{-1/\alpha}\Bigl(\frac{\vartheta+1}{t^{-1/\alpha}\delta(\vartheta+1)-t^{-(\alpha-1)/\alpha}+1}\Bigr)^{\alpha+1}\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(Y_{1-t}\in(-1+t+\delta,t-\delta)).

The ratio in the last line is bounded above uniformly in
\alpha
and
\vartheta
. As for the last probability, recall that
Y_{1-t}=D_{1-t}+J_{1-t}
with
\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|D_{t}+t|\leq\delta/2
so
Y_{1-t}>-1+t+\delta
implies that
J_{1-t}
is nonzero, namely that the process
X^{\alpha}
has at least one jump larger than
c
before time
1-t
. However in this unconditioned model the jumps are given by a Poisson measure, namely:

\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(J_{1-t}>0)=\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(\#\{s\in(0,1-t)\colon\Delta X^{\alpha}_{s}>c\}\geq 1)=\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Poisson}((1-t)\overline{\pi}^{\alpha}(c))\geq 1)\sim(1-t)\overline{\pi}^{\alpha}(c).

Recall that

\overline{\pi}^{\alpha}(c)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(2-\alpha)}(\alpha-1)^{1-p\alpha}(\vartheta+1)^{-q\alpha}\to 0
as either
\alpha\to 1
or
\vartheta\to\infty
(or both) since
p<1
.
Hence

\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(Y_{1-t}\in(-1+t+\delta,t-\delta)\mid X^{\alpha}_{1}=\vartheta)\enskip\longrightarrow\enskip 0,

so with high probability, either
|Y_{1-t}+(1-t)|\leq\delta
, or
|Y_{1-t}-t|\leq\delta
.
Recall that
1-t=k\varepsilon
is the right-edge of the
k
’th subinterval of length
\varepsilon=1/K
. Since
K
is fixed, then this holds jointly for all these intervals: with high probability under
\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(\,\cdot\mid X^{\alpha}_{1}=\vartheta)
, at the end of each such interval, the process
Y
is either
\delta
-close to the linear drift
-t
, or
\delta
-close to this drift plus
1
.

Recall one last time that high probability under
\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(\,\cdot\mid X^{\alpha}_{1}=\vartheta)
, we may decompose
Y=J+D
with
D
being
\delta/2
-close to the drift
t\mapsto-t
, with
J
having only (at most two) positive jumps and remaining constant otherwise, and with
Y_{1}=Y_{0}=0
.
Then if
J
does not make any jump larger than
\delta/2
before time
t
, then the path
(Y_{s})_{s\leq t}
remains
\delta
-close to
(-s)_{s\leq t}
. Also if
Y_{t}
is
\delta
-close to
1-t
, then
J
does not make jumps larger than
2\delta
after time
t
since otherwise
Y_{1}>0
.
We infer from the previous paragraph that with high probability, the process
J
only has jumps larger than
2\delta
in a single interval of length
\varepsilon
, in which
Y
transitions from being close to
t\mapsto-t
to being close to
t\mapsto 1-t
.
By exchangeability, the probability that the two largest jumps of
Y
occur in a time-frame at most
\varepsilon
is small when
\varepsilon
is; hence the transition of
Y
occurs by a single jump with high probability and this concludes the proof.
∎

6.7.
Remarks on the regime
\vartheta=-1
and
\alpha\downarrow 1

Recall that we have excluded the two cases
(\alpha,\vartheta)\to(1,-1)
and
(\alpha,\vartheta)\to(2,\infty)
, in which case the asymptotic behaviour depends more precisely on the rate of convergence.
For example, the proof of in the Brownian regime extends as soon as the parameter in the Esscher transform
\kappa=(|\vartheta|/\alpha)^{1/(\alpha-1)}
tends to infinity faster than any negative power of
\alpha-1
, which holds e.g. for
\vartheta=\pm(\alpha-(\alpha-1)^{2})
.
Let us here briefly discuss the case when
\vartheta
is fixed equal to
-1
and
\alpha\downarrow 1
, which seems to display an interesting behaviour.

Recall the small discussion just after the statement of Theorem
6.2
claiming that the unconditioned process
X^{\alpha}
converges in probability to the drift
t\mapsto-t
as
\alpha\downarrow 1
. More precisely, from the explicit expression (
6.1
) of the characteristic function, it is straightforward to check that

(\lambda_{\alpha}(X^{\alpha}_{t}+t),t\geq 0)\enskip\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}}_{\alpha\downarrow 1}\enskip(-t,t\geq 0)\qquad\text{where}\qquad\lambda_{\alpha}\log\lambda_{\alpha}=\frac{1}{\alpha-1}.

In particular, the endpoint
\vartheta=-1
looks typical for
X^{\alpha}_{1}
at first glance, but actually the process wants to end near
-1-\lambda_{\alpha}^{-1}
. We then conjecture as in the case
\vartheta>-1
that the easiest way to end exactly at
-1
is to make a single jump of size approximately
\lambda_{\alpha}^{-1}\to 0
and drift linearly otherwise, in a ‘one-small jump principle’.

Conjecture 6.10
.

Under the coniditional law
\mathop{}\!\mathbb{P}(\,\cdot\mid X^{\alpha}_{1}=-1)
, we have

(\lambda_{\alpha}(X^{\alpha}_{t}+t))_{t\in[0,1]}\enskip\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{(d)}_{\alpha\downarrow 1}\enskip(\mathbf{1}_{\{U\leq t\}}-t)_{t\in[0,1]}

where
\lambda_{\alpha}>0
is the solution of
\lambda_{\alpha}\log\lambda_{\alpha}=1/(\alpha-1)
.

We believe that the argument of the previous subsection can be adapted, after simply replacing
\vartheta+1
by
\lambda_{\alpha}^{-1}
, and taking
p=1-q>0
small.
Indeed, define now

c=(\alpha-1)^{p}\lambda_{\alpha}^{-(1-p)}=(\alpha-1)(\log\lambda_{\alpha})^{1-p}=\lambda_{\alpha}^{-1}(\log\lambda_{\alpha})^{-p},

and let us split the process
X^{\alpha}
as in (
6.11
), namely write:

\lambda_{\alpha}(X^{\alpha}_{t}+t)=\lambda_{\alpha}\Bigl(\sum_{s\leq t}\Delta X^{\alpha}_{s}\mathbf{1}_{\{\Delta X^{\alpha}_{s}>c\}}+X^{\alpha,\leq c}_{t}-t\Bigl(\int_{c}^{\infty}x\pi^{\alpha}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x)-1\Bigr)\Bigr).

One can easily check that the drift coefficient does indeed converge to
-t
.
Then the argument to control the first two terms on the right for the unconditioned process are the same as before.
The key missing point is to extend Lemma
6.6
and find the asymptotic behaviour of the density
p^{\alpha}_{1}(-1)
as
\alpha\downarrow 1
to transfer the results to the bridge.

6.8.
Proof of the technical estimates on stable laws

Let us finally prove Lemma
6.6
. Recall that it claims that if
p^{\alpha}_{1}
is the density at time
1
of the stable process
X^{\alpha}
then for every
x_{\ast}\in(-1,\infty]
, we have:

\frac{(1+x)^{\alpha+1}p^{\alpha}_{1}(x)}{\alpha-1}\enskip\mathop{\longrightarrow}\enskip 1

(6.14)

as the pair
(\alpha,x)
converge to
(1,x_{\ast})
and that moreover for every
\delta>0
, there exists
C>0
such that the upper bound

\frac{(1+x)^{\alpha+1}p^{\alpha}_{1}(x)}{\alpha-1}\leq C

(6.15)

holds uniformly for all
\alpha\leq 2-\delta
and
x\geq-1+\delta
.

Proof of Lemma
6.6
.

The density
p^{\alpha}_{1}(x)
corresponds to
g(x,\alpha,1)
in the notation of Zolotarev
[
Zol86
]
. Equation 2.4.6 there provides the following series expansion:

g(x,\alpha,1)=\frac{1}{\pi}\sum_{n\geq 1}(-x)^{n-1}\frac{\Gamma(n/\alpha+1)}{\Gamma(n+1)}\sin(n\pi(\alpha-1)/\alpha).

Once divided by
\alpha-1
, the
n
’th term converges to
\pi n(-x)^{n-1}
as
\alpha\downarrow 1
and its absolute value is less than
\alpha^{-1}n\pi|x|^{n-1}
for all
\alpha
. Thus, when
|x_{\ast}|<1
, we infer from dominated convergence that

\frac{(1+x)^{\alpha+1}p^{\alpha}_{1}(x)}{\alpha-1}\enskip\mathop{\longrightarrow}\enskip(1+x_{\ast})^{2}\sum_{n\geq 1}n(-x_{\ast})^{n-1}=1.

In addition, for every
\delta>0
the left-hand side is bounded above by a constant, which depends on
\delta
but is uniform in
\alpha\in(1,2)
and
x\in(-1+\delta,1-\delta)
.
Our claims (
6.14
) and (
6.15
) thus hold for
|x|<1-\delta
.

Let us henceforth assume
x>0
is bounded away from
0
. An asymptotic result as
x\to\infty
when
\alpha>1
is fixed is proved in
[
Zol86
, Equation 2.5.4]
by relating the
\alpha
-stable law to an
\alpha^{\prime}=1/\alpha<1
stable law. Precisely
[
Zol86
, Equation 2.5.5]
reads: for
x>0
,

g(x,\alpha,1)=x^{-\alpha-1}g(x^{-\alpha},\alpha^{\prime},\beta)\qquad\text{where}\qquad\alpha^{\prime}=1/\alpha\enskip\text{and}\enskip\beta=2(\alpha-1)-1,

(6.16)

where we recall that the left-hand side equals
p^{\alpha}_{1}(x)
.
It thus remains to study the behaviour of
g(y,\alpha^{\prime},\beta)
, with
y>0
bounded away from
\infty
; we recall the definition of the latter just a few lines below.
It seems that the proof of of
[
Zol86
, Theorem 2.5.1]
(with
n=1
) when
\alpha
is fixed and
y\to 0
does not extend here, because the term
\cos(\pi\alpha\beta/2)^{-2/\alpha}
in
[
Zol86
, Equation 2.5.2]
blows up as
\alpha\to 1
. We shall somehow use a tighter bound on the rest, without bounding the real part by the modulus.

Let
\gamma=\pi\alpha^{\prime}\beta/2
and
\varepsilon=\pi(\alpha-1)\to 0
.
The change of variable
v=u^{\alpha^{\prime}}\operatorname{e}^{i\gamma}
yields:

\displaystyle g(y,\alpha^{\prime},\beta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\Re\int_{0}^{\infty}\operatorname{e}^{iuy}\operatorname{e}^{-u^{\alpha^{\prime}}\exp(i\gamma)}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Re\bigl(\operatorname{e}^{-i\gamma/\alpha^{\prime}}\operatorname{e}^{iyv^{1/\alpha^{\prime}}\exp(-i\gamma/\alpha^{\prime})}\bigr)\operatorname{e}^{-v}v^{\frac{1}{\alpha^{\prime}}-1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\Re\bigl(\operatorname{e}^{-i\pi\beta/2}\operatorname{e}^{iyv^{\alpha}\exp(-i\pi\beta/2)}\bigr)\operatorname{e}^{-v}v^{\alpha-1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v.

Observe that

\cos(\pi\beta/2)=\sin(\varepsilon)

and

\sin(\pi\beta/2)=-\cos(\varepsilon)
, which implies that the real part in the integral equals:

\displaystyle\Re\big(\operatorname{e}^{-i\pi\beta/2}\operatorname{e}^{iyv^{\alpha}\exp(-i\pi\beta/2)}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\Re\bigl((\sin(\varepsilon)+i\cos(\varepsilon))\operatorname{e}^{-yv^{\alpha}\cos(\varepsilon)+iyv^{\alpha}\sin(\varepsilon)}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{e}^{-yv^{\alpha}\cos(\varepsilon)}\Re\bigl((\sin(\varepsilon)+i\cos(\varepsilon))(\cos(yv^{\alpha}\sin(\varepsilon))+i\sin(yv^{\alpha}\sin(\varepsilon))\bigr)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{e}^{-yv^{\alpha}\cos(\varepsilon)}\bigl(\sin(\varepsilon)\cos(yv^{\alpha}\sin(\varepsilon))-\cos(\varepsilon)\sin(yv^{\alpha}\sin(\varepsilon))\bigr).

Notice that for
z>0
it holds both

1-z^{2}/2\leq\cos(z)\leq 1
and
z\geq\sin(z)\geq z-z^{3}/6
.
We infer that:

-\sin(\varepsilon)z^{2}/2\leq(\sin(\varepsilon)\cos(z)-\cos(\varepsilon)\sin z)-(\sin(\varepsilon)-\cos(\varepsilon)z)\leq\cos(\varepsilon)z^{3}/6.

In addition, by dominated convergence, for any integer
k\geq 1
, as
y\to y_{\ast}\in[0,\infty)
and
\alpha\to 1
it holds:

\int_{0}^{\infty}\operatorname{e}^{-yv^{\alpha}\cos(\varepsilon)}\operatorname{e}^{-v}v^{k\alpha-1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v\enskip\mathop{\longrightarrow}\enskip\int_{0}^{\infty}\operatorname{e}^{-(y_{\ast}+1)v}v^{k-1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v=\frac{(k-1)!}{(y_{\ast}+1)^{k}}.

Combining the previous displays, we infer that, as
y\to y_{\ast}\in[0,\infty)
and
\alpha\to 1
, we have:

\displaystyle g(y,\alpha^{\prime},\beta)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\operatorname{e}^{-yv^{\alpha}\cos(\varepsilon)}\Bigl(\sin(\varepsilon)-\cos(\varepsilon)yv^{\alpha}\sin(\varepsilon)+\cos(\varepsilon)\frac{(yv^{\alpha}\sin(\varepsilon))^{3}}{6}\Bigr)\operatorname{e}^{-v}v^{\alpha-1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\Bigl(\frac{\sin(\varepsilon)}{y+1}-\frac{\cos(\varepsilon)\sin(\varepsilon)y}{(y+1)^{2}}+\frac{\cos(\varepsilon)(y\sin(\varepsilon))^{3}}{(y+1)^{4}}\Bigr)(1+o(1))

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\frac{\sin(\varepsilon)}{(y+1)^{2}}\Bigl((y+1)-\cos(\varepsilon)y+\frac{\cos(\varepsilon)\sin(\varepsilon)^{2}y^{3}}{(y+1)^{2}}\Bigr)(1+o(1))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{\varepsilon}{(y+1)^{2}}(1+o(1)),

and similarly

\displaystyle g(y,\alpha^{\prime},\beta)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\operatorname{e}^{-yv^{\alpha}\cos(\varepsilon)}\Bigl(\sin(\varepsilon)-\cos(\varepsilon)yv^{\alpha}\sin(\varepsilon)-\sin(\varepsilon)\frac{(yv^{\alpha}\sin(\varepsilon))^{2}}{2}\Bigr)\operatorname{e}^{-v}v^{\alpha-1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{\varepsilon}{(y+1)^{2}}(1+o(1)).

Recall that
\varepsilon=\pi(\alpha-1)
so finally

\frac{(y+1)^{2}g(y,\alpha^{\prime},\beta)}{\alpha-1}\enskip\mathop{\longrightarrow}\enskip 1.

Then our claim (
6.14
) for
x_{\ast}\in(0,\infty]
follow from the identity (
6.16
).

We now turn to the second claim (
6.15
).
Recall that we already proved it when
\alpha\in(1,2)
and
|x|\leq 1-\rho
for any
\rho>0
.
Fix
\delta\in(0,1/2)
and let us define
\kappa,\rho\in(0,1)
by:

\kappa=1-\frac{(\pi\delta)^{2}}{2}+\frac{(\pi\delta)^{4}}{24}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\rho=1-\Bigl(\frac{\cos(\pi\delta)}{1-\kappa}\Bigr)^{1/2},

where the positivity of
\rho
follows from the bound
\cos(z)<1-z^{2}/2+z^{4}/24
for
z>0
. The value of
\rho
is chosen to have

1+x^{-\alpha}\cos(\pi(\alpha-1))>\kappa>0\quad\text{for all}\enskip\alpha\in(1,2-\delta)\enskip\text{and}\enskip x\geq 1-\rho.

(6.17)

Indeed, this is clear when
\alpha\leq 3/2
since then the cosine is nonnegative. For
\alpha\in(3/2,2-\delta)
, we have
0>\cos(\pi(\alpha-1))>\cos(\pi(1-\delta))=-\cos(\pi\delta)
and when
x\geq 1-\rho
, it holds
x^{-\alpha}\leq(1-\rho)^{-2}=(1-\kappa)/\cos(\pi\delta)
.
Now let us prove the bound (
6.15
) for
\alpha\in(1,2-\delta)
and
x\geq 1-\rho
.
Thanks to the identity (
6.16
), this equivalent to showing that there exists
C>0
which depends on
\delta
such that the upper bound

\frac{(1+y^{1/\alpha})^{\alpha+1}g(y,\alpha^{\prime},\beta)}{\alpha-1}\leq C

(6.18)

holds uniformly for all
\alpha\in(1,2-\delta)
and
y\in[0,(1-\rho)^{-\alpha}]
.
Let us note already that
(1+y^{1/\alpha})^{\alpha+1}
stays bounded, we proceed to showing that
g(y,\alpha^{\prime},\beta)\leq C(\alpha-1)
.

Since
v^{\alpha}>v
when
v>1
and
v^{\alpha}<v
when
v<1
, then after also a straightforward change of variable in each integral, we get for any
c>0
and every
k\geq 1
:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\operatorname{e}^{-(c-1)v^{\alpha}}\operatorname{e}^{-v}v^{k\alpha-1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{1}\operatorname{e}^{-cv^{\alpha}}v^{k\alpha-1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v+\int_{1}^{\infty}\operatorname{e}^{-cv}v^{k\alpha-1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{c}\frac{\operatorname{e}^{-u}u^{k-1}}{\alpha c^{k}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u+\int_{c}^{\infty}\frac{\operatorname{e}^{-u}u^{k\alpha-1}}{c^{k\alpha}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}u.

After bounding each integral by the same ones over the entire half-line, we infer:

\int_{0}^{\infty}\operatorname{e}^{-(c-1)v^{\alpha}}\operatorname{e}^{-v}v^{k\alpha-1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v\leq\frac{\Gamma(k)}{\alpha c^{k}}+\frac{\Gamma(k\alpha)}{c^{k\alpha}}.

Let us keep the same notation
\varepsilon=\pi(\alpha-1)
as before and apply this bound to
c=1+y\cos(\varepsilon)
.
Note that according to (
6.17
), we have
c>\kappa>0
for all
\alpha\in(1,2-\delta)
and
y\in[0,(1-\rho)^{-\alpha}]
.
Then the right-hand side above is bounded uniformly for such
\alpha
and
y
, say by
C(k)
.
We infer as above that:

\displaystyle g(y,\alpha^{\prime},\beta)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\operatorname{e}^{-yv^{\alpha}\cos(\varepsilon)}\Bigl(\sin(\varepsilon)-\cos(\varepsilon)yv^{\alpha}\sin(\varepsilon)+\cos(\varepsilon)\frac{(yv^{\alpha}\sin(\varepsilon))^{3}}{6}\Bigr)\operatorname{e}^{-v}v^{\alpha-1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\sin(\varepsilon)\bigl(C(1)+|\cos(\varepsilon)|yC(2)+|\cos(\varepsilon)|y^{3}\sin(\varepsilon)^{2}C(4)/6\bigr),

and the term in parenthesis above is bounded uniformly for
\alpha\in(1,2-\delta)
and
y\in[0,(1-\rho)^{-\alpha}]
. Since
\sin(\varepsilon)=\sin(\pi(\alpha-1))\leq\alpha-1
, then (
6.18
) follows.
∎
